Hochschild cohomology of the Fukaya category via Floer cohomology with coefficients
Jack Smith

Abstract
. Given a monotone Lagrangian L L in a compact symplectic manifold X X , we construct a commutative diagram relating the closed–open string map 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 λ : QH ∗ ⁡ ( X ) → HH ∗ ⁡ ( ℱ ​ ( X ) λ ) \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}) to a variant of the length-zero closed–open map on L L incorporating 𝐤 ⁡ [ H 1 ⁡ ( L , ℤ ) ] \mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})] coefficients, denoted 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 𝐋 0 \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}} . The former is categorically important but very difficult to compute, whilst the latter is geometrically natural and amenable to calculation. We further show that, after a suitable completion, injectivity of 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 𝐋 0 \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}} implies injectivity of 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 λ \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda} . Via Sheridan’s version of Abouzaid’s generation criterion, this gives a powerful tool for proving split-generation of the Fukaya category. We illustrate this by showing that the real part of a monotone toric manifold (of minimal Chern number at least 2 2 ) split-generates the Fukaya category in characteristic 2 2 . We also give a short new proof (modulo foundational assumptions in the non-monotone case) that the Fukaya category of an arbitrary compact toric manifold is split-generated by toric fibres.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Hochschild cohomology of the Fukaya category

Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact monotone symplectic manifold of dimension
2n
. The monotone Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(X)
of
X
, constructed by Sheridan
[
54
]
(see Ritter–Smith
[
48
]
for the non-compact version), is an
A_{\infty}
-category which encodes rich information about the Lagrangian submanifolds of
X
and their Floer theory. More precisely, it is a collection of categories
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, labelled by
\lambda
in our coefficient field
\mathbf{k}
. This
\lambda
prescribes the weighted count of pseduoholomorphic discs with boundary on each Lagrangian.

A powerful way to understand
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, for example to prove homological mirror symmetry, is to find an object
L^{\flat}
that split-generates it, in the sense that every object in the category can be built from
L^{\flat}
by repeatedly taking mapping cones and splitting off summands. Here
L^{\flat}
denotes a Lagrangian
L
equipped with various extra data. Given such a split-generator, the Yoneda embedding lets us view
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
concretely as a category of
A_{\infty}
-modules over
\operatorname{end}^{*}(L^{\flat})=\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
. More geometrically, if
L^{\flat}
split-generates
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
then
L
must intersect every other Lagrangian supporting a non-zero object. Identifying split-generators is therefore a central problem in categorical symplectic topology.

One of the key tools for proving split-generation is the following result, which is discussed in more detail, and refined in various ways, in
Appendix

A
.

Theorem 1.1

(Generation criterion, Abouzaid
[
1
]
, Sheridan
[
54
, Corollary 2.19]
)
.

If
L^{\flat}
is an object in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, and if the composition

(1)

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\xrightarrow{\ \ \text{restrict}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))

is injective, then
L^{\flat}
split-generates
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
.
∎

Here
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})
is the Hochschild cohomology of the Fukaya category, which is an important Hodge-theoretic invariant in its own right,
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
is the Hochschild cohomology of the Floer
A_{\infty}
-algebra of
L^{\flat}
, and
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
is the closed–open string map: a geometrically defined unital
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism. These Hochschild invariants are algebraically formidable objects, involving all of the
A_{\infty}
-operations. They, and the map
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
, are also extremely difficult to calculate directly, since they are defined by counts of pseudoholomorphic discs with arbitrarily many boundary inputs. These require delicate perturbation schemes to makes sense of, especially when inputs are repeated.

The goal of this paper is to take these objects and connect them to more intuitive and computable geometric constructions, arising from Floer cohomology with coefficients. Our approach is related to the family Floer theory of Fukaya
[
28
]
, Abouzaid
[
2
]
, and Cho–Hong–Lau
[
14
]
, and is consistent with the philosophy that repeated insertion of degree
1
boundary inputs should be equivalent to formal expansion of local systems. The main innovation is that we work with mixed objects lying between the two sides of the mirror: Hochschild cochains of the Fukaya category with coefficients in matrix factorisations. In this introduction we briefly summarise Floer cohomology with coefficients, before describing the motivating example for this work. We then set up and state the main results.

1.2.
Floer cohomology with coefficients

In the Fukaya category we typically allow our Lagrangians to be equipped with rank-
1
local systems
\mathcal{L}
over
\mathbf{k}
, i.e. flat
\mathbf{k}
-line bundles. These line bundles weight the operations on the category according to their monodromies or parallel transport maps around the boundaries of the pseudoholomorphic discs being counted.

When working with a single Lagrangian
L
we can essentially consider all local systems at once, by working over the group algebra
S=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
and weighting each disc by the monomial describing its boundary homology class. We will denote this
Floer algebra with coefficients
by
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
, for reasons that will become clear shortly. Whilst this is a very natural geometric construction, it does not make sense within the Fukaya category over
\mathbf{k}
; see
Section

2.3
. Instead, we enlarge our coefficient ring to
S
, denoting the new category by
\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)
, and define the object
\mathbf{L}\in\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)
to be
L
equipped with the tautological rank-
1
local system over
S
. Then the Floer algebra with coefficients is exactly the ordinary Floer algebra of
\mathbf{L}
, justifying our choice of notation. More generally, we will write
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}
and
\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{*}
for Floer complexes and their cohomology computed in
\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)
. This may implicitly involve viewing objects of
\mathcal{F}(X)
as objects of
\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)
by extension of coefficients.

Returning momentarily to the map
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
from (
1
), it has a
length-zero
version

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),

obtained by projecting the bar-type Hochschild cochain complex onto its shortest piece. In general, this loses much of the information of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
, but it is much easier to calculate since the discs it counts have no boundary inputs and so sophisticated perturbation schemes are not necessary. Its codomain is also much easier to understand since it only involves the differential on
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
, and not the higher operations. By now incorporating weights from
S
we can define analogous map

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L}).

In practice it is no harder to compute than
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}
, but carries more information in the form of the universal boundary weights, as opposed to the specific weights corresponding to
\mathcal{L}
.

1.3.
The motivating example

If
X
is a compact monotone toric manifold and
L
is the monotone toric fibre then it is well-known, following Batyrev
[
8
]
and Givental
[
37
,
36
]
, that
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
can be described as the Jacobian ring
\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
of the superpotential of
L
, whose definition we recall in
Section

1.4
. Assuming that
\mathbf{k}
is algebraically closed, or at least contains all critical points of
W_{L}
, the quantum cohomology decomposes as a product of ideals
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)_{\alpha}
labelled by critical points
\alpha
. Under the isomorphism
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\cong\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
these correspond to the completions
(\operatorname{Jac}W_{L})_{\alpha}
of
\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
at each
\alpha
. There is an induced decomposition of the Fukaya category into pieces
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\alpha}
, each of which contains the object
L_{\alpha}
given by
L
equipped with the local system (whose monodromy is) described by
\alpha
.

In
[
25
, Corollary 1.3.1]
Evans–Lekili prove that each
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\alpha}
is split-generated by
L_{\alpha}
, concluding the proof of one direction of homological mirror symmetry in this setting
[
15
, Corollary 9.2]
. They do not use the generation criterion, which here says that
L_{\alpha}
split-generates
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\alpha}
if
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L_{\alpha}}
is injective on
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)_{\alpha}
. Instead, they exploit the fact that
L
is a free orbit of a Hamiltonian torus action on
X
. However, it is a consequence of their argument
[
25
, Corollary 6.2.8]
that

(2)

\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)_{\alpha}\cong\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\alpha})\cong\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L_{\alpha},L_{\alpha}))

for each
\alpha
, where the second isomorphism uses Morita invariance of Hochschild cohomology. It is natural to expect that the composition of the two isomorphisms in (
2
) coincides with
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L_{\alpha}}
.

To connect this to Floer cohomology with coefficients, the Jacobian ring
\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
arises in this setting as
\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
(see
Proposition

C.3
; the
\mathrm{op}
is irrelevant here since the algebra is commutative, but it will be important later) and the isomorphism
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\cong\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
is realised by precisely the map
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
(see
Theorem

C.5
, which is derived from
[
57
,
32
]
). We can then view each
(\operatorname{Jac}W_{L})_{\alpha}
as the Floer cohomology
HF_{\widehat{S}}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
with coefficients in a suitable completion
\widehat{S}
of
S
, and the isomorphism
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)_{\alpha}\cong(\operatorname{Jac}W_{L})_{\alpha}
as the corresponding completion
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
. We thus have a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L_{\alpha},L_{\alpha}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HF}_{\widehat{S}}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

in which all groups are isomorphic to each other. The vertical arrow is known to be an isomorphism and the horizontal arrow is expected to be one. Given the formal similarity of these two closed–open-type maps, it is natural to ask whether they can be related to each other, for example by extending the diagram to a commuting square in which the bottom and right-hand arrows are isomorphisms. The present work provides an affirmative answer to this question, and hence (by showing that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L_{\alpha}}
is an isomorphism) an alternative proof that each
L_{\alpha}
split-generates
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\alpha}
.

Remark 1.2
.

Partial results towards toric split-generation via injectivity of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}
have previously been obtained by Ritter
[
47
]
, in the case when
W
is Morse (he also deals with some non-compact
X
), and Tonkonog
[
60
]
, when
W
has at worst
A_{2}
singularities.

We also show, under foundational assumptions, that the analogous generation result,
L
, holds for general (not necessarily monotone) compact toric manifolds. These foundations are expected to be addressed in work announced by Abouzaid–Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono around 2016, which we believe also considers their application to the toric generation question.

1.4.
Ingredients

We now introduce the subjects of our main results. We follow Seidel’s conventions for
A_{\infty}
-algebras and -categories
[
51
]
except for the signs used to define the opposite algebra or category; see
Section

2.1
.

Let
L\subset X
be a monotone Lagrangian submanifold defining an object
L^{\flat}
in the Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
as in
Section

2.2
. Associated to
L
is its superpotential
W_{L}\in S=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
, which counts rigid pseudoholomorphic discs with boundary on
L
that send a boundary marked point to a generically chosen point in
L
, each weighted by its boundary homology class. We view this as a function on the space
\operatorname{H}^{1}(L;\mathbf{k}^{\times})\cong\operatorname{Spec}S
of rank-
1
local systems on
L
. For our present purposes, the object
L^{\flat}
comprises
L
equipped with such a local system
\mathcal{L}
satisfying
W_{L}(\mathcal{L})=\lambda
.

Given
L
and
\lambda
, there is a localised mirror functor

\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda}:\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}\to\mathrm{mf}(S,W_{L}-\lambda),

to the dg-category of matrix factorisations of
W_{L}-\lambda
. This functor was introduced by Cho–Hong–Lau in
[
15
]
, and is essentially defined by extending scalars from
\mathbf{k}
to
S
and then applying the the covariant hom-functor associated to
\mathbf{L}
. In particular, it sends each object
K
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
to
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},K)
. We recall the construction in more detail in
Section

2.4
. Cho, Hong, and Lau focused on the case where
L
is a torus, but their ideas immediately extend to general
L
.

Remark 1.3
.

One has to be a little careful about calling it a hom-functor, since
\mathbf{L}
lies in
\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)_{W_{L}}
rather than
\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)_{\lambda}
. This is what causes the ‘differential’ on
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},K)
to square to
W_{L}-\lambda
rather than
0
, and hence what causes the functor to land in matrix factorisations rather than cochain complexes.

Via the functor
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda}
we can view
\mathrm{mf}(S,W_{L}-\lambda)
as an
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
-bimodule, giving a Hochschild cochain complex
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}(S,W_{L}-\lambda))
and a pushforward map

(\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda})_{*}:\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}(S,W_{L}-\lambda)).

This is the first ingredient in our results.

The second ingredient is the
A_{\infty}
-algebra
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
. More precisely, we want the
opposite
algebra
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
, for reasons that will become clear the proof of
Proposition

3.3
. This is obtained by reversing the order of the inputs to operations and introducing an associated sign twist, namely (
10
). Its cohomology algebra
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}})
is the ordinary graded-opposite algebra to
\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
, which we denote by
\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
(
graded
-opposite just means that we include Koszul signs).

1.5.
Main results

We are now in a position to state our main results. The first relates the algebra
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
to the Hochschild cochain algebra
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}(S,W_{L}-\lambda))
.

Theorem A

(
Propositions

3.3
,
3.4
and
3.11
)
.

There is a geometrically defined
S
-linear
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphism

\Theta:\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}(S,W_{L}-\lambda)).

It is cohomologically unital and extends the module action of
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
on
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda}
in a natural sense.

The functor
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda}
and homomorphism
\Theta
are independent of auxiliary choices in a manner explained in
Section

3.4
. In particular, the induced maps on cohomology are canonical.

Our second main result is that
\Theta
is compatible with the relevant closed–open maps as follows.

Theorem B

(
Corollary

3.10
)
.

The following diagram commutes

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}(S,W_{L}-\lambda)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}((\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda})_{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}(\Theta)}

We reiterate that the top arrow is categorically important but algebraically complicated and difficult to calculate, whilst the left-hand arrow is simple, geometric, and relatively computable.

Remark 1.4
.

\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
carries an
A_{\infty}
-structure given by viewing it as the Floer cohomology of the diagonal in
X^{-}\times X
, but this has been little-studied. Since all of the maps in
B
are (the
1
-ary terms of)
A_{\infty}
-homomorphisms, one may hope to use them to compute this
A_{\infty}
-structure. For example, using knowledge of the
A_{\infty}
-structure on
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
when
L
is the equator in
X=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\operatorname{char}\mathbf{k}=2
, one can recover the non-trivial
A_{\infty}
-product on
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbf{k})
computed in
[
25
, Example 7.3.6]
.

To apply the generation criterion to the object
L^{\flat}
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, we need to consider the composition
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
with the restriction map from
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})
to
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
. Let
\mathcal{E}
denote the matrix factorisation
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda}(L^{\flat})=\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},L^{\flat})
, and let
\mathcal{B}
denote its endomorphism dg-algebra. The localised mirror functor and
\Theta
then induce
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphisms
\Phi:\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\to\mathcal{B}
and

\Theta_{L^{\flat}}:\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\mathcal{B})

respectively. Restricting (
3
) from
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
to
L^{\flat}
tells us that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\mathcal{B}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}(\Phi_{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}(\Theta_{L^{\flat}})}

It turns out that a slight algebraic modification will allow us to gain a better understand these maps. To set this up, note that the local system
\mathcal{L}
on
L^{\flat}
corresponds, via its monodromy, to a group homomorphism
\rho:\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbf{k}^{\times}
, and hence to an algebra homomorphism
S\to\mathbf{k}
. Let
\mathfrak{m}\subset S
denote the kernel of this algebra homomorphism, and let
\widehat{S}
denote the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completion of
S
. The constructions of
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{(\mathrm{op})}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\Phi
, and
\Theta_{L^{\flat}}
have immediate analogues over
\widehat{S}
, which we denote by
\operatorname{CF}_{\widehat{S}}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{(\mathrm{op})}
,
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
,
\widehat{\Phi}
, and
\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}
respectively. Note that the completion depends on the choice of
\mathcal{L}
although our notation does not explicitly show this. We then have a commuting diagram

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HF}_{\widehat{S}}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})}

Theorem C

(
Theorems

4.5
and
4.6
)
.

The map
\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}
is a quasi-isomorphism. So to prove that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
is injective on a subspace of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
it suffices to prove that
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
is injective on that subspace. More generally, the same result holds when
S
is replaced by the quotient
R=S/I
before completing, for any ideal
I
in
S
contained in
\mathfrak{m}
.

The generalisation to
S/I
is useful in applications, for example the following.

Theorem D

(
Proposition

5.6
)
.

Tonkonog’s criterion,
Theorem

5.2

[
60
, Theorem 1.7]
, for partial injectivity of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
is really a criterion for partial injectivity of
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
for
I=\mathfrak{m}^{2}
. This implies the corresponding statement for
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
by
C
. (In this case the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completion does nothing, since
I
contains a power of
\mathfrak{m}
.)

The proof of
C
uses a spectral sequence argument to reduce
S
or
R
coefficients to
\mathbf{k}
coefficients, where the result can be deduced from the general nonsense of
A_{\infty}
-(bi)modules. The
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completion allows us to apply the Eilenberg–Moore comparison theorem. Geometrically it may seem a little mysterious, but it can be understood as follows: we cannot expect
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
to know about the Floer theory of
L
away from the local system
\mathcal{L}
, so to relate its Hochschild invariants to
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
we should expect to have to complete, or at least localise, the latter at
\mathfrak{m}
.

When combined with the generation criterion,
C
gives a powerful new tool for proving split-generation. For example, suppose
X
is a compact monotone toric manifold whose minimal Chern number is at least
2
, and assume our coefficient field
\mathbf{k}
has characteristic
2
. Let
L^{\flat}
be the real locus of
X
equipped with the trivial local system. By building on work of Tonkonog
[
60
]
, for an appropriate choice of
I
as in
C
we construct a commutative diagram of
\mathbf{k}
-algebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{2*}_{R}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}}

Here
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
is an extension of quantum cohomology to
R=S/I
coefficients,
\pi
is reduction
R\to\mathbf{k}
modulo
\mathfrak{m}
, and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-linear extension of the Frobenius morphism
x\mapsto x^{2}
, in a sense explained in
Section

6.1
. Meanwhile,
\mathcal{D}_{R}
is an isomorphism we construct, extending the known isomorphisms
\operatorname{H}^{2*}(X)\to\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)
and
\operatorname{QH}^{2*}\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
of Duistermaat
[
22
]
and Haug and Hyvrier
[
38
,
40
]
. Using this diagram, we show the following.

Theorem E

(
Proposition

6.3
)
.

The map
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
is injective, so:

(i)

If the real locus
L^{\flat}
is orientable then it split-generates
\mathcal{F}(X)_{0}
.

(ii)

In general,
L^{\flat}
split-generates the ungraded Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(X)^{\mathrm{un}}_{0}
.

Remark 1.5
.

Our default Fukaya category is
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded, and each Lagrangian in it must be equipped with a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading in the sense of
[
50
]
. In our setup, existence of such a grading is equivalent to orientability; see
2.6
. When
\mathbf{k}
has characteristic
2
we can drop the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-gradings (we no longer need them to define various signs in our formulae), and obtain an ungraded category,
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{un}}
, which allows non-orientable Lagrangians. The algebraic structures, including the generation criterion, carry over to this ungraded setting as discussed in
[
6
]
, except for the relationship with eigenvalue decompositions covered in
Section

A.3
.

Injectivity of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
, and hence the split-generation results of
E
, were previously shown by Tonkonog
[
60
]
in several families of examples, including
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}
. Using a totally different approach, Evans–Lekili
[
25
, Example 7.2.4]
proved part
(i)
. The general question of split-generation by the real locus, however, has remained open.

Remark 1.6
.

Split-generation of the ungraded category by an orientable Lagrangian neither implies nor is implied by split-generation of the graded category: the ungraded category includes more objects, but one is allowed to construct more (i.e. ungraded) twisted complexes and to break off more summands.

Remark 1.7
.

The minimal Chern number assumption is used to ensure that the real locus has minimal Maslov number at least
2
, and hence that it defines a valid object in the monotone Fukaya category. The same hypothesis appears in
[
25
]
(via the requirement that the real locus is orientable) and in
[
60
]
. Alston–Amorim
[
5
]
have shown that even when the minimal Chern number is
1
the real locus is Floer-theoretically unobstructed in the sense of Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono
[
29
]
, i.e. that one can equip it with a bounding cochain so that it becomes a valid object in the Fukaya category. In
Section

1.6
we discuss some extensions of our results that incorporate bounding cochains, but they require additional assumptions related to strict unitality. We are not aware of these assumptions having been established in the case of the real locus, so we do not state our results for minimal Chern number
1
.

Corollary F
.

In the setting of
E
, if
K
is any monotone Lagrangian in
X
that supports a non-zero object in
\mathcal{F}(X)^{\mathrm{un}}_{\lambda}
for some
\lambda
, then
K
is non-displaceable from the real part
L
by Hamiltonian isotopies.

Proof.

In this situation,
K\times K
supports a non-zero object in
\mathcal{F}(X\times X)^{\mathrm{un}}_{0}
, which is split-generated by
L^{\flat}\times L^{\flat}
by
E
applied to
X\times X
. So
K\times K
is non-displaceable from
L\times L
, and hence
K
is non-displaceable from
L
.
∎

Remark 1.8
.

The idea of passing to
X\times X
to make the obstruction number
\lambda
vanish was used by Abreu–Macarini
[
3
, Remark 2.9]
, Alston–Amorim
[
5
, Theorem 1.3]
, and Tonkonog
[
60
, Theorem 1.5]
in similar situations. In particular, Alston–Amorim
[
5
, Corollary 1.4]
showed that
L^{\flat}
is non-displaceable from the toric fibre, whilst Tonkonog
[
60
, Corollary 3.10]
proved the analogue of
F
for a specific family of
X
.

Example 1.9
.

Amorim–Cho
[
6
]
introduced ungraded matrix factorisations and the idea that they should be mirror to non-orientable Lagrangians. They studied
L^{\flat}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
using the localised mirror functor associated to the monotone toric fibre
K
, and showed that it is mirror to

\left(\mathbf{k}[x^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1}]^{\oplus 4},\begin{pmatrix}0&1&1&\frac{1}{xy}\\
y&0&\frac{1}{x}&1\\
x&\frac{1}{y}&0&1\\
1&x&y&0\end{pmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{mf}^{\mathrm{un}}\bigg(\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(K;\mathbb{Z})]\cong\mathbf{k}[x^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1}],W_{K}=x+y+\frac{1}{xy}\bigg).

We instead study
L^{\flat}
using the localised mirror functor associated to
L
itself, working over the ring
R=S/I=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}/2)]
. In the case of
L^{\flat}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}
we have
R\cong\mathbf{k}[z]/(z^{2}-1)
,
W_{L}=0
, and

\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})\cong\mathbf{k}[h,z]/(h^{n+1}-z,z^{2}-1)\cong\mathbf{k}[h]/(h^{2(n+1)}-1),

where
h
is (the PSS image of) the real hyperplane class. Under the localised mirror functor associated to
L
,
L^{\flat}
is sent to the ungraded matrix factorisation

\big(\mathbf{k}[h,z]/(h^{n+1}-z,z^{2}-1),h(z-1)\big)\cong\left(R^{n+1},\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\dots&0&z-1\\
z-1&0&\dots&0&0\\
0&z-1&\dots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\dots&z-1&0\end{pmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{mf}^{\mathrm{un}}(R,0).

Returning now to the case of general
X
and
\mathbf{k}
, having seen that the horizontal map
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})
in (
4
) is an isomorphism it is natural to ask how much information the vertical map
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
remembers. We are able to answer this question in two important situations. The first is when
L
is a torus, which is of particular interest in mirror symmetry.

Theorem G

(
Proposition

7.3
)
.

If
L
is a torus and
\mathcal{L}
is a critical point of
W_{L}
then
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
is an isomorphism.

By arguments analogous to those used in
Section

3.4
to prove that
\Theta
is independent of auxiliary choices, this statement is also independent of choices. So it suffices to prove it for a specific choice of auxiliary data, and we can choose these data so that in fact
\widehat{\Phi}
is itself a quasi-isomorphism (using
[
58
]
, which builds on
[
15
]
), from which the result follows.

G
has the following immediate consequence.

Corollary H
.

If
L
is a torus and
\mathcal{L}
is a critical point of
W_{L}
then the map
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
corresponds to the formal expansion of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
about
\mathcal{L}
, after suitable identification of the codomains. In particular,
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
is injective on a subspace of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
if and only if the expansion of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
is injective on the same subspace.
∎

Example 1.10
.

Returning to the example of
Section

1.3
, we know that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
is an isomorphism

\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}

and that its completion about each critical point
\alpha
induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)_{\alpha}\to(\operatorname{Jac}W_{L})_{\alpha}.

H
then tells us that

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L_{\alpha}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)_{\alpha}\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L_{\alpha},L_{\alpha}))

is an isomorphism, so
L_{\alpha}
split-generates
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\alpha}
by the generation criterion, as proved by Evans–Lekili using completely different methods.

Remark 1.11
.

Suppose
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
decomposes as a product of algebras
Q\times Q^{\perp}
. It follows from general structural properties
[
34
, Theorem 8]
that if
L^{\flat}
lies in the
Q
-summand of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
and if
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}|_{Q}
is injective then, in addition to
L^{\flat}
split-generating this piece of the Fukaya category, we get that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}|_{Q}
is an
isomorphism
, i.e. it’s automatically surjective (for more detail on these decompositions of the category see
Section

A.1
).
H
thus has the following curious consequence: if
L
is a torus and the formal expansion
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
about a critical point of
W_{L}
is injective on
Q
and vanishes on
Q^{\perp}
(the latter ensures that
L^{\flat}
lies in the
Q
-summand of the category), then
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}|_{Q}
is an isomorphism. In particular, these hypotheses can never hold if the critical point is non-isolated, since then
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{\widehat{S}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
is infinite-dimensional.

H
can also be used to prove new, non-toric, generation results for Lagrangian tori. In favourable situations this can even be done with no knowledge beyond the ambient quantum cohomology ring and
W_{L}
itself. One useful tool here is the famous result of Auroux
[
7
, Lemma 6.7]
, Kontsevich, and Seidel that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}
sends twice the first Chern class
c_{1}
of
X
to
2W_{L}(\mathcal{L})\cdot 1_{L^{\flat}}=2\lambda\cdot 1_{L^{\flat}}
; see
Lemma

A.6
for a precise statement. The same argument shows the following.

Corollary I
.

For any
L
(not necessarily a torus) the map
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
sends
2c_{1}
to
2W_{L}\cdot 1_{\mathbf{L}}
, and if
L
is orientable then we can cancel the factors of
2
. So if
L
is a torus and
\mathcal{L}
is a critical point of
W_{L}
then, under the correspondence in
H
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}(c_{1})
corresponds to the formal expansion of
W_{L}
about
\mathcal{L}
.
∎

Example 1.12
.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{4}
be the quadric threefold and let us temporarily work over
\mathbb{Z}
. The quantum cohomology is

\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}[H,E]/(H^{2}-2E,E^{2}-H),

with
c_{1}=3H
.
X
admits a degeneration to the singular toric manifold whose polytope is a square-based pyramid. This gives a Lagrangian
3
-sphere
V\subset X
as the vanishing cycle, and a disjoint monotone Lagrangian torus
L\subset X
as the parallel transport of the barycentric torus from the central fibre. After choosing a basis for
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
to give an identification
S\cong\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},z^{\pm 1}]
, and equipping
L
with the standard spin structure, we have by
[
25
, Remark 7.1.3]
(using
[
44
, Theorem 1]
) that

W_{L}=x+y+z+\frac{1}{xy}+\frac{1}{yz}.

By
I
we then have
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(3H)=\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(c_{1})=W_{L}\cdot 1_{\mathbf{L}}
. Moreover, the
S
-subalgebra of
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
generated by the unit is

\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},z^{\pm 1}]/(x-z,y-1/x^{2},2x^{3}-1)=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1}]/(2x^{3}-1)=\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2},x]/(x^{3}-\tfrac{1}{2}).

Under these identifications we get
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(3H)=6x\in\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2},x]/(x^{3}-\tfrac{1}{2})
, and hence
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(H)=2x
.

Let us now pass back to field coefficients
\mathbf{k}
, with
\operatorname{char}\mathbf{k}\neq 2
. Then
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
decomposes as a direct sum of ideals
Q_{0}=(1-H^{3}/4)
and
Q_{1}=(H,E)
, whilst
\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
becomes
\mathbf{k}[x]/(x^{3}-\tfrac{1}{2})
, and the calculation
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(H)=2x
tells us that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
is injective on
Q_{1}
. This in turn implies that
L
split-generates the
Q_{1}
-summand of the Fukaya category. More precisely:

•

If
\operatorname{char}\mathbf{k}=3
then
\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
is local, with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}=(x+1,y-1,z+1)
. The
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completion
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
is therefore still injective on
Q_{1}
, so
L
equipped with the local system defined by
x=z=-1
and
y=1
split-generates the
Q_{1}
-summand of the category.

•

If
\operatorname{char}\mathbf{k}\neq 3
then, assuming
\mathbf{k}
contains all cube roots of
2
,
\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
contains three maximal ideals, corresponding to three local systems on
L
. Meanwhile
Q_{1}
decomposes as
\mathbf{k}\times\mathbf{k}\times\mathbf{k}
, and each completion
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
at a maximal ideal is injective on one of these factors. So the
Q_{1}
-summand of the category splits into three pieces, each split-generated by
L
with one of the three critical local systems.

Remark 1.13
.

When
\operatorname{char}\mathbf{k}\neq 2
the
Q_{0}
-summand of the category is split-generated by
V
, by
[
25
, Proposition 7.1.1]
(or by
[
55
, Lemma 4.6]
if
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{C}
). When
\operatorname{char}\mathbf{k}=2
the splitting into
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}
breaks down and the ring
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
is local. In this case,
V
split-generates the whole category, again by
[
25
, Proposition 7.1.1]
.

Returning once more to the general setting, the second situation in which we can describe the map
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
is when
L
is simply connected. In this case we have
S=\mathbf{k}
, so
\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{(\mathrm{op})}
reduces to
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{(\mathrm{op})}
and completions are irrelevant. So
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
is a map

(5)

\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}.

If
L^{\flat}
is also weakly exact, i.e. bounds no topological discs of positive symplectic area, then
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
reduces to
\operatorname{C}^{*}(L)
. We can then use the isomorphism

(6)

\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{C}^{*}(L))\cong\operatorname{H}_{-*}(\Lambda L^{-TL}),

which holds for simply connected
L
, to view
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
as a map

(7)

\operatorname{H}_{-*}(\Lambda L^{-TL})\to\operatorname{H}^{*}(L).

Here
\Lambda L
is the free loop space of
L
and
\Lambda L^{-TL}
is the Thom spectrum associated to the stable normal bundle of
L
, pulled back under the evaluation-at-basepoint map
\operatorname{ev}:\Lambda L\to L
. (We have dropped the
\mathrm{op}
from the codomain since
\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)
is graded-commutative.) Our final main result describes the maps (
5
) and (
7
) algebraically and geometrically respectively.

Theorem J
.

(i)

(
Proposition

7.4
) If
L
is simply connected then

\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*}):\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}

is projection to length zero.

(ii)

(
Proposition

7.5
) If
L
is simply connected and weakly exact then
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
is identified with

\operatorname{ev}_{*}:\operatorname{H}_{-*}(\Lambda L^{-TL})\to\operatorname{H}_{-*}(L^{-TL})\cong\operatorname{H}^{*}(L).

Remark 1.14
.

Strictly the projection to length zero from
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
lands in
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
, rather than
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}
. But it actually lands in the graded-centre of
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
, which is the same as the graded-centre of
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}
, so
(i)
makes sense.

Remark 1.15
.

We have been unable to locate the original reference for (
6
), but it seems to have been known to experts in the 1980s (if not earlier) at the level of modules, i.e. ignoring the string topology product on
\Lambda L
. We shall refer to
[
17
, Corollary 11]
, since it proves the isomorphism as algebras and gives an explicit description of the map that we will use. Our grading of Hochschild cohomology is the negative of that used in
[
17
]
, so we obtain
\operatorname{H}_{-*}
in place of their
\operatorname{H}_{*}
.

Remark 1.16
.

Some of our results apply slightly more generally than stated. In particular:

(i)

We could allow
X
to be non-compact (but keeping
L
compact) in
A
,
B
,
C
,
G
,
H
,
I
and
J
, as long as it is tame at infinity. If
X
is a Liouville manifold then we could also replace quantum cohomology with symplectic cohomology in the domain of all versions of the closed–open map. However, compactness is necessary for the generation criterion in the form of
Theorem

1.1
, since its proof relies on Poincaré duality, so
E
,
F
and
1.10
also require compactness. Similarly, Tonkonog’s criterion is based in the compact setting so
D
is too.

(ii)

We could have relaxed the assumption that
\mathbf{k}
is a field in some places.
A
holds over an arbitrary ground ring with the same proof (modulo
3.12
), and the commutative diagram in
B
holds over any ring if one defines the maps with compatible choices of auxiliary data. This compatibility is necessary because over a general ring it is not clear that the Hochschild cohomology of the Fukaya category is independent of choices (the usual proof of independence uses the spectral sequence associated to the length filtration on Hochschild cochains, whose first page—over a field!—is the Hochschild cohomology of the cohomology category).

(iii)

Given a class in
\operatorname{H}^{2}(X;\mathbf{k}^{\times})
, represented by a cocycle
b
, we could modify the quantum (or symplectic) cohomology and Fukaya category of
X
by weighting every pseudoholomorphic curve
u
by
b(u)
, as in
Section

C.4
. In the special case where
B
is a closed
\mathbb{C}
-valued
2
-form on
X
and
b=e^{B}
, this modification is equivalent to turning on the
B-field

B
. All of our arguments extend automatically to this setting with the obvious modifications. Similarly, one can equip
X
with a
background class
in
\operatorname{H}^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, represented by a cocycle
\mathsf{b}
, and then consider Lagrangians equipped with
relative
pin structures with respect to
\mathsf{b}
. Again, everything goes through straightforwardly. We will not discuss these options any further.

1.6.
(Non-)Monotonicity

So far we have restricted attention to monotone symplectic manifolds and Lagrangians, owing to three complications that arise in the non-monotone case. First, the Fukaya category and closed–open map have not been defined in general outside the monotone setting, and the generation criterion has not been proved. These foundational issues are expected to be addressed in work of Abouzaid–Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono, and we shall simply take them as black boxes via
Assumption

†

below. Second, there are extra algebraic complexities arising from the need to filter and complete various objects, to ensure convergence of curve counts. One consequence of this is that rather than working with the analogue of
S
, we work with unspecified algebras satisfying certain natural conditions. We will call these algebras
R
, as they play a similar role to the
R=S/I
appearing previously. Third, an additional unobstructedness hypothesis is needed, which is automatic for monotone
L
but is in general a non-trivial geometric condition. Modulo these issues,
A
,
B
and
C
naturally generalise to the non-monotone case as we describe momentarily. Despite the apparent algebraic and geometric restrictions, these results are strong and flexible enough to apply to interesting examples, which we demonstrate with the case of toric fibres.

To start setting up our results, in place of
\mathbf{k}
we take our ground field to be the Novikov field

\Lambda=\mathbb{C}\llbracket T^{\mathbb{R}}\rrbracket=\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}T^{s_{j}}:a_{j}\in\mathbb{C}\text{ and }s_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ with }s_{j}\to\infty\Big\}.

This carries a natural decreasing filtration by additive subgroups
\Lambda_{\geq s}
, for
s\in\mathbb{R}
, comprising those series
\sum a_{j}T^{s_{j}}
with
s_{j}\geq s
for all
j
. We will require that the objects we work with are filtered and complete in a similar way, made precise in
Definition

8.1
.

We now introduce our technical assumptions for later reference.

Assumption
\dagger
.

Sufficient technical foundations have been established to:

(i)

Define the Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
over
\Lambda
and prove the operations satisfy the
A_{\infty}
-relations. We assume moreover that the category is strictly unital. Objects are Lagrangians
L
equipped with analogous auxiliary data to the monotone case (the local system must now be
c
-filtered in the sense of
Definition

8.2
), plus a weak bounding cochain
b\in\operatorname{CF}^{\mathrm{odd}}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
in sufficiently positive filtration level, whose curvature is
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

(ii)

Construct
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})
as a unital
\Lambda
-algebra homomorphism. We assume that the construction can be made to land in strictly unital Hochschild cochains.

(iii)

Prove a generation criterion for summands of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
based on injectivity of

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))

on factors of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
.

Remark 1.17
.

The reader may be concerned about the assumptions of strict unitality, but for applications to split-generation it suffices to construct a strictly unital model for the Floer algebra and
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
map for the specific Lagrangian
L
of interest. In our main example, when
L
is a toric fibre, such a model is provided by
[
30
]
.

Suppose
Assumption

†

(i)
holds and fix a complete filtered augmented
\Lambda
-algebra
R
. The augmentation
R\to\Lambda
corresponds to a closed maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
in
R
. Using the same machinery as for
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
we can define the Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)_{r}
over
R
for any
r\in R
.

Fix an object
(L^{\flat},b)\in\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
.

Definition 1.18
.

We say
(L^{\flat},b)

lifts to
R
if the following holds. There exists a
c
-filtered rank-
1
local system
\mathcal{L}_{R}
over
R
on
L
, whose reduction modulo
\mathfrak{m}
is the local system
\mathcal{L}
on
L^{\flat}
. We denote
L
equipped with this local system (and the same grading and pin structure as
L^{\flat}
) by
\mathbf{L}
. We then require that
\mathbf{L}
admits a weak bounding cochain
\mathbf{b}
whose reduction mod
\mathfrak{m}
is
b
. In this case, we denote the curvature of
\mathbf{b}
by
W_{L}\in R
. Note that there may be multiple choices for
\mathcal{L}_{R}
and
\mathbf{b}
, and that
W_{L}
depends on these choices, although our notation does not reflect this.

Assume now that
(L^{\flat},b)
lifts to
R
, and fix a choice of
\mathcal{L}_{R}
and
\mathbf{b}
. We can then mimic the construction of
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda}
to give a localised mirror functor

\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),\lambda}:\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}\to\mathrm{mf}(R,W_{L}-\lambda).

This allows us to define
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}(R,W_{L}-\lambda))
, and the analogue of
A
is as follows.

Theorem A+

(
Proposition

8.5
)
.

Suppose
Assumption

†

(i)
holds and
(L^{\flat},b)
lifts to
R
. Given a choice of lift
(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b})
, there is a geometrically defined
R
-linear
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphism

\Theta:\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{R}((\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}))^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}(R,W_{L}-\lambda)).

It is cohomologically unital and extends the module action of
\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{R}((\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}))^{\mathrm{op}}
on
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),\lambda}
.

Remark 1.19
.

In order to define
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),\lambda}
and
\Theta
there was no need to start with
(L^{\flat},b)
or to assume that
R
was augmented. We could have just taken
R
to be a complete filtered
\Lambda
-algebra (not augmented),
W_{L}
to be an arbitrary element of
R
, and
(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b})
to be an arbitrary object in
\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)_{W_{L}}
. We could have done analogously in the monotone setting too. However, we chose to start with
(L^{\flat},b)
since our main interest is in showing that
(L^{\flat},b)
split-generates a piece of the Fukaya category.

If
Assumption

†

(ii)
also holds then we can similarly define

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b})}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}((\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}))^{(\mathrm{op})},

and
B
adapts in the obvious way.

Theorem B+

(
Proposition

8.6
)
.

In the setting of
A+
, suppose
Assumption

†

(ii)
also holds. Then the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HF}_{R}^{*}((\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}))^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}(R,W_{L}-\lambda)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}((\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),\lambda})_{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}(\Theta)}

Remark 1.20
.

Recall that the main point of
B
was to make
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
, and in particular
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
, more amenable to computation. If
\mathbf{b}
is non-zero then
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b})}
is rather difficult to calculate because it suffers from the problem of repeated inputs, which is what we wanted to avoid. So we will mostly be interested in the case where
\mathbf{b}
(and hence
b
) vanishes. We previously used a pearl model for computations, but we now have in mind a de Rham model as in
[
30
]
.

In the monotone case, the ring
R=S/I
is automatically Noetherian, which is used in the proof of
C
. In our present setting we have to include this assumption separately, but the analogue of
C
then holds.

Theorem C+

(
Proposition

8.7
)
.

In the setting of
A+
, suppose
R
is Noetherian. Then the
\mathfrak{m}
-adically completed map
\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}
is a quasi-isomorphism. So, under
Assumption

†

(ii)
, to prove that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
is injective on a subspace of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
it suffices to prove that
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b})}
is injective on that subspace.

Remark 1.21
.

In our main application
R
will be local and finite-dimensional over
\Lambda
, in which case it is automatically Noetherian and
\mathfrak{m}
-adically complete; see
Lemma

8.8
(iii)
.

From
C+
we obtain a generation criterion in terms of
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b})}
.

Corollary K
.

Suppose that
Assumption

†

holds in full, that
R
is a complete filtered augmented
\Lambda
-algebra which is Noetherian, and that
(L^{\flat},b)
is an object in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
which lifts to
R
as
(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b})
. If
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b})}
is injective on a factor of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, then
(L^{\flat},b)
split-generates the corresponding summand of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
.
∎

Using this, and by taking
R
to be a local factor of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, we show the following.

Theorem L

(
Proposition

8.16
)
.

Under
Assumption

†

, suppose
X
is a compact toric manifold,
R
is a local factor of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, and
\lambda
is the eigenvalue of the generalised eigenspace of
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
containing
R
. Then the
R
-summand of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
is split-generated by a specific toric fibre with a specific rank-
1
local system.

As mentioned in
Section

1.3
, we believe work announced by Abouzaid–Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono around 2016 establishes a version of
Assumption

†

and also considers this toric generation question.

Remark 1.22
.

If we also assume that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
respects the eigenvalue decomposition of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, in the sense that it vanishes on the generalised
\lambda^{\prime}
-eigenspace of
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
if
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
, then we conclude that in fact all pieces of the Fukaya category of
X
are split-generated by toric fibres with rank-
1
local systems.

Remark 1.23
.

In a similar spirit to
1.16
, we could allow
X
to be non-compact in
A+
,
B+
and
C+
if a suitable form of
Assumption

†

holds. And we could allow quantum cohomology and Floer theory to be modified by bulk deformations everywhere.

1.7.
Structure of the paper

Section

2
describes the technical setup for the paper, which is brought together in
Section

3
to construct
\Theta
and prove
A
and
B
.
Section

4
, which is almost entirely algebraic, then proves
C
. The remaining sections can be read somewhat independently of each other:
Section

5
looks at Tonkonog’s criterion in light of our results and proves
D
;
Section

6
studies the case of real toric Lagrangians and proves that they split-generate the Fukaya category in characteristic
2
(
E
);
Section

7
analyses the map
\widehat{\Phi}
in the settings of
G
and
J
; and
Section

8
discusses the non-monotone versions of our results, as sumarised in
Section

1.6
, including the proof that toric fibres split-generate (
L
). The paper ends with three appendices, covering respectively the generation criterion, some
A_{\infty}
-algebra lemmas used in the proof of
C
, and the quantum cohomology of monotone toric manifolds as needed for
Section

6
.

1.8.
Acknowledgements

I am grateful to Oscar Randal-Williams, Ivan Smith, and anonymous referees for useful comments, to Nick Sheridan for the triangle algebra argument in
Lemma

3.6
, and to Leonie Luginbühl and Clara Manco for initiating the sequence of events that led to this paper.

This work was carried out at St John’s College, Cambridge.

2.
Setup

Fix from now on a monotone symplectic
2n
-manifold
(X,\omega)
which is compact or tame at infinity and equipped with a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading
[
50
]
. Fix also a coefficient field
\mathbf{k}
. The Fukaya category and all (co)homology groups and algebraic operations will be over
\mathbf{k}
unless stated otherwise. In this section we summarise the background ideas we will use later. (In
Section

8
we will drop the monotonicity condition and work over the Novikov field
\Lambda
instead of
\mathbf{k}
, but we will flag this at the time.)

2.1.
Conventions

Recall that we mostly follow Seidel’s conventions for
A_{\infty}
-algebras and -categories
[
51
]
. (We will mainly discuss categories, with algebras given by the special case where there’s only one object.) In particular, the differential
\mathrm{d}
on a dg-category is related to the
\mu^{1}
operation on the corresponding
A_{\infty}
-category by

(8)

\mu^{1}(a)=(-1)^{\lvert a\rvert}\mathrm{d}a.

Similarly, the composition
\circ
on a dg-category or its cohomology is related to the
\mu^{2}
operation via

(9)

\mu^{2}(a_{2},a_{1})=(-1)^{\lvert a_{1}\rvert}a_{2}\circ a_{1}.

Higher order compositions are written from right to left, for example

\mu^{k}:\hom^{*}(O_{k-1},O_{k})\otimes\dots\otimes\hom^{*}(O_{0},O_{1})\to\hom^{*}(O_{0},O_{k}).

We write
\hom^{*}
for chain-level morphism spaces and
\operatorname{Hom}^{*}
for their cohomology.

The place where we differ from
[
51
]
is in our signs for the opposite
A_{\infty}
-algebra or -category.

Definition 2.1
.

Given an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
, with operations
\mu_{\mathcal{C}}
, its
opposite category

\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
, and the same morphism spaces as graded modules. Its operations
\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}
are defined by

(10)

\mu_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}^{l}(c_{l},\dots,c_{1})=(-1)^{\triangle_{l}+l-1}\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{l}(c_{1},\dots,c_{l})

for all
c_{1},\dots,c_{l}
, where

\triangle_{l}=\sum_{i<j}(\lvert c_{i}\rvert-1)(\lvert c_{j}\rvert-1).

These are easily checked to satisfy the
A_{\infty}
-relations, and if
\mathcal{C}
is cohomologically unital then so is
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
, with the same cohomological units.

Lemma 2.2
.

The cohomology category
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}})
is the ordinary graded-opposite category
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathcal{C})^{\mathrm{op}}
to
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
.

Remark 2.3
.

By
graded
-opposite we mean that it includes Koszul signs.

Proof of
Lemma

2.2
.

Let
\circ_{\mathcal{C}}
,
\circ_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}
, and
\circ_{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}
denote the compositions in
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
,
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}})
, and
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathcal{C})^{\mathrm{op}}
respectivey. For cocycles
c_{2}
and
c_{1}
in
\mathcal{C}
or
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
, representing cohomology classes
[c_{2}]
and
[c_{1}]
, we have

[c_{2}]\circ_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}[c_{1}]=(-1)^{\lvert c_{1}\rvert}[\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}(c_{2},c_{1})]=(-1)^{\lvert c_{2}\rvert\lvert c_{1}\rvert+\lvert c_{2}\rvert}[\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{C}}(c_{1},c_{2})]\\
=(-1)^{\lvert c_{2}\rvert\lvert c_{1}\rvert}[c_{1}]\circ_{\mathcal{C}}[c_{2}]=[c_{2}]\circ_{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}[c_{1}].\qed

2.2.
The Fukaya category

Next we recap the definition of the monotone Fukaya category.

Recall that a Lagrangian submanifold
L\subset X
is monotone if there is a positive real constant
\tau
such that the Maslov index and area homomorphisms

\mu,\omega:\pi_{2}(X,L)\to\mathbb{R}

are related by
\omega=\tau\mu
. Recall also that the minimal Maslov number
N_{L}
of
L
is defined to be the positive generator of the image
\mu(\pi_{2}(X,L))\subset\mathbb{Z}
if this image is non-zero, and infinity otherwise.

For each
\lambda\in\mathbf{k}
the compact monotone Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
is
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded
A_{\infty}
-category defined as follows (see
[
54
,
48
]
for details). The objects are Lagrangians
L
in
X
such that:

(i)

L
is compact, connected, and monotone, with minimal Maslov number
N_{L}
at least
2
.

(ii)

The image of
\pi_{1}(L)
in
\pi_{1}(X)
is trivial.

(iii)

L
is equipped with a pin structure (not needed if
\operatorname{char}\mathbf{k}=2
) and a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading with respect to the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading of
X
.

(iv)

L
carries a rank-
1
local system
\mathcal{L}
over
\mathbf{k}
such that
W_{L}(\mathcal{L})=\lambda
, where
W_{L}
is the superpotential of
L
as defined as in
Section

1.4
.

Formally, one usually fixes a finite or countable collection of such Lagrangians to work with, although each may be allowed to carry arbitrarily many different local systems.

Remark 2.4
.

We drop the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-gradings on
X
and
L
if
\operatorname{char}\mathbf{k}=2
, and obtain the
ungraded
Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{un}}
appearing in
E
. We will give all arguments assuming the existence of
\mathbb{Z}/2
-gradings, but these are really only used to define various signs in our formulae, and the same arguments go through in the ungraded case.

Remark 2.5
.

Some authors replace our definition of monotonicity with the slightly stronger condition that the Maslov index and area are positively proportional on
\operatorname{H}_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
rather than
\pi_{2}(X,L)
, or that the Maslov class and symplectic form are positively proportional in
\operatorname{H}^{2}(X,L;\mathbb{R})
. Assumption
(ii)
can then be weakened to
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
being trivial in
\operatorname{H}_{1}(X;\mathbb{Z})
.

Remark 2.6
.

Recall from
[
50
]
that a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading of
X
is a homotopy class of square root of the complex line bundle
(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{n}TX)^{\otimes 2}
. There is always a canonical choice, namely
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{n}TX
, and other choices are all obtained by twisting by an element
t
of
\operatorname{H}^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, i.e. tensoring with an isomorphism class of real line bundle
\mathcal{T}
on
X
. A
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading of
L
with respect to this
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading of
X
is then a homotopy class of trivialisation of
(\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}}^{n}TL)\otimes\mathcal{T}|_{L}
, i.e. an orientation of
L
twisted by
\mathcal{T}
. In light of
(ii)
, or its variant in
2.5
, the bundle
\mathcal{T}|_{L}
is trivial, so
L
has a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading if and only if it’s orientable.

The morphism spaces are Floer cochain complexes and the
A_{\infty}
-operations are defined by counting rigid pseudoholomorphic discs using a choice of regular perturbation data. (We will freely use the terminology of Floer data and perturbation data from
[
51
, Section (8e)]
, but the casual reader uninterested in technicalities can safely ignore such discussions.)

Definition 2.7
.

We write
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
for the
A_{\infty}
-operations on
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
.

From now on, all Lagrangians will be assumed to satisfy
(i)
–
(iii)
. We will write
L^{\flat}
for a pair
(L,\mathcal{L})
as in
(iv)
, as we may want to distinguish the submanifold
L\subset X
from the object
L^{\flat}
of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, but for Lagrangians named with other letters we will gloss over this distinction and simply say things like ‘take a Lagrangian
K
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
’.

There are different geometric models available for Floer cochain complexes in this setting. For arbitrary Lagrangians
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
we may take
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(K_{1},K_{2})
to be generated by intersection points between
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
, if they intersect transversely, or more generally by Hamiltonian chords from
K_{1}
to
K_{2}
. This chord model is the one set up by Seidel
[
51
]
and adapted to the monotone case by Sheridan
[
54
]
and Ritter–Smith
[
48
]
. In this model, inputs and outputs of pseudoholomorphic discs are boundary punctures asymptotic to chords. In the special case when
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are equal to a common
L^{\flat}
, we can instead use a
pearl model
, as developed by Cornea–Lalonde
[
18
]
, Biran–Cornea
[
9
]
, and Sheridan
[
53
]
. Here generators of
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
are critical points of a Morse function on
L
, and
A_{\infty}
-operations count ‘pearly trees’ built from Morse flowlines and pseudoholomorphic discs.

In
[
58
]
, following Cho–Hong–Lau
[
15
]
, we used the pearl model for
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
but could equally well have used the chord model as long as the perturbations were chosen appropriately. Likewise, in the present paper we are free to use either model. We will describe moduli spaces and draw pictures in the chord model, i.e. using discs without Morse trees, as they are cleaner to work with, but everything can be straightforwardly translated by the reader who prefers the pearl model.

2.3.
The Fukaya category over
S

Fix an object
L^{\flat}=(L,\mathcal{L})
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, with superpotential
W_{L}
in
S=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
. The Fukaya category over
S
, denoted by
\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)
and with Floer complexes and cohomology denoted by
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}
and
\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{*}
, has already appeared informally and is defined completely analogously to the category over
\mathbf{k}
. We denote the operations on the category by
\mu_{S}
. Recall that we may implicitly view objects of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
as objects of
\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)_{\lambda}
by extending scalars from
\mathbf{k}
to
S
, i.e. applying
S\otimes_{\mathbf{k}}{-}
to their local systems.

Recall also that the object
\mathbf{L}\in\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)_{W_{L}}
, central to our results, is given by
L
with the tautological local system, whose monodromy around a loop
\gamma\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
is the monomial in
S
corresponding to
\gamma
. The Floer algebra of
L
with coefficients is
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
, and
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
is obtained from this by reversing the order of inputs and twisting signs as in
Definition

2.1
. We denote the operations on
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
by
\mu_{\mathbf{L}}
.

We could try to interpret
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
purely within
\mathcal{F}(X)
by considering the object
(L,\mathcal{S})
, where
\mathcal{S}
is the universal abelian local system over
\mathbf{k}
. Explicitly, this would involve equipping
L
with the rank-
\lvert\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\rvert
local system over
\mathbf{k}
whose fibres are modelled on
S
, and whose monodromy around
\gamma\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
is again given by multiplication by the corresponding monomial. Use of such higher-rank local systems is permitted in principle but leads to various complications
[
42
]
. In any case, the resulting Floer complexes do not give what we want:
\operatorname{CF}^{*}((L,\mathcal{S}),(L,\mathcal{S}))
has the right differential but the underlying module is too large, as each Floer generator gives rise to a copy of
\operatorname{End}_{\mathbf{k}}(S)
instead of
S
; and
\operatorname{CF}^{*}((L,\mathcal{S}),L^{\flat})
has the right module but the differential is wrong.

Remark 2.8
.

The complex
\operatorname{CF}^{*}((L,\mathcal{S}),L^{\flat})
coincides with
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},L^{\flat})
and can be interpreted as the lifted Floer complex of
L
, in the sense of Damian
[
20
]
, associated to the universal abelian cover.

2.4.
The localised mirror functor

Next we describe more precisely the localised mirror functor

\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda}:\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}\to\mathrm{mf}(S,W_{L}-\lambda)

mentioned in
Section

1.4
. For further details see the original paper of Cho–Hong–Lau
[
15
]
. Related ideas appear in
[
20
]
. From now on we will abbreviate
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda}
and
\mathrm{mf}(S,W_{L}-\lambda)
simply to
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
and
\mathrm{mf}
respectively. We write
\mu_{\mathrm{mf}}^{1}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{mf}}^{2}
for the operations on
\mathrm{mf}
.

Recall that the
A_{\infty}
-Yoneda embedding
[
27
,
26
]
associates to each object
L^{\flat}\in\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
an
A_{\infty}
-module
Y_{L^{\flat}}
over
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
. By definition, such a module is an
A_{\infty}
-functor from
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
to the dg-category of chain complexes over
\mathbf{k}
. The specific module
Y_{L^{\flat}}
is defined to send each object
K\in\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
to the cochain complex
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},K)
with differential
\mathrm{d}a=(-1)^{\lvert a\rvert}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}(a)
; the
(-1)^{\lvert a\rvert}
is our standard sign change for passing between dg- and
A_{\infty}
-language as in (
8
). For each
k\geq 1
, the
k
th component

Y_{L^{\flat}}^{k}:\operatorname{CF}^{*}(K_{k-1},K_{k})[1]\otimes\dots\otimes\operatorname{CF}^{*}(K_{0},K_{1})[1]\to\hom^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},K_{0}),\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},K_{k}))[1]

is then given by the
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{k+1}
operation, with sign twisted, via

(11)

Y_{L^{\flat}}^{k}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})(a_{0})=(-1)^{\lvert a_{0}\rvert}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{k+1}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1},a_{0}).

Remark 2.9
.

With these conventions,
Y_{L^{\flat}}
is a left module and the Yoneda embedding is contravariant. One could instead, with suitable sign changes, take
Y_{L^{\flat}}(K)=\operatorname{CF}^{*}(K,L^{\flat})
, which would give a right module and make the Yoneda embedding covariant. We use the above conventions because it is really the module
Y_{L^{\flat}}
that we care about. In particular, we want this module to be covariant when viewed as a functor to chain complexes.

Definition 2.10
.

The localised mirror functor
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
is defined in exactly the same way as
Y_{L^{\flat}}
, but with
\mathbf{L}
in place of
L^{\flat}
and
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}
in place of
\operatorname{CF}^{*}
. Explicitly,
K\in\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
is sent to
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},K)
with ‘differential’
\mathrm{d}a=(-1)^{\lvert a\rvert}\mu_{S}^{1}(a)
. Following
[
58
]
, we refer to this
\mathrm{d}
as the
squifferential
. The usual proof that
\mathrm{d}^{2}=0
in monotone Floer theory shows that on
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},K)
we have
\mathrm{d}^{2}=W_{L}-\lambda
. Morally this is because

\mathrm{d}^{2}a=-\mu_{S}^{1}(\mu_{S}^{1}(a))=\mu_{S}^{2}(a,\mu_{\mathbf{L}}^{0})+(-1)^{\lvert a\rvert-1}\mu_{S}^{2}(\mu_{K}^{0},a)=a\cdot W_{L}-\lambda\cdot a.

The components
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{k}
are similarly given by the
\mu_{S}^{k+1}
operation, twisted by a sign as in (
11
). The fact that these do indeed define an
A_{\infty}
-functor is shown in
[
15
, Theorem 6.4]
, with different sign conventions, and in
[
58
, Lemma 3.3]
in a slightly different setting but with the same sign conventions. We sketch the argument pictorially below.

Remark 2.11
.

In characteristic
2
the localised mirror functor can be extended to the ungraded Fukaya category, now landing in ungraded matrix factorisations
[
6
]
.

2.5.
Diagrams for operations

We depict the
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{k}
operation on
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
schematically as a disc with
k
Floer inputs (open circles) and one Floer output (an open square) on the boundary, as shown in the left-hand part of
Fig.

1
.

Figure 1.
The
A_{\infty}
-operation
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{5}
(left), the squifferential on
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K)=\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},K)
(centre), and the component
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{3}
(right).

The unlabelled inputs are implicitly in anticlockwise order when read from right to left. We depict the squifferential on a matrix factorisation
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K)=\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},K)
and the components
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{k}
similarly, with a thick boundary segment indicating the position of the
\mathbf{L}
in these operations. See the centre and right-hand part of
Fig.

1
. We refer to the Floer input and output at the end of the thick segment of the boundary as the
distinguished
input and output.

The
A_{\infty}
-functor equations for
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
can be verified by considering equivalent moduli spaces to those defining
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
but in virtual dimension
1
, rather than
0
. These moduli spaces can be compactified in the usual way, and the fact that the boundary comprises zero (signed) points gives us the required relations. To see this, consider the possible codimension-
1
degenerations of the right-hand disc in
Fig.

1
. Note that we are talking about degenerations of the
map
; these may involve degeneration of the domain, but may instead only involve strip breaking at the inputs or output. Four things can happen: some of the non-distinguished inputs can bubble off to give a
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
operation; the squifferential can bubble off at either the distinguished input or output, corresponding to the differential on
\mathrm{mf}
; or the disc can break along the thick segment into two discs of the same form, corresponding to the composition on
\mathrm{mf}
.

Figure 2.
Codimension-
1
degenerations of a
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{3}
disc.

These four possibilities are shown in
Fig.

2
, from left to right. An open circle on top of an open square denotes a Floer generator which is an output of one disc and an input of another.

In a similar way, the operations
\mu_{\mathbf{L}}
on
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
can be depicted as discs whose entire boundary is thick, as shown in
Fig.

3
.

\cdot
\cdot
\cdot

Figure 3.
The operations
\mu_{\mathbf{L}}
on
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Inputs are now implicitly in
clockwise
order when read from right to left. More generally, our convention is that if the boundary has both thick and thin segments then inputs on the
interior
of the thick segment are clockwise and the rest are anticlockwise.

These diagrams do not in themselves encode the signs attached to the discs we are counting. We specify these separately by relating them to the standard signs attached to discs defining
\mu_{S}
operations, or to other well-established operations. For example, we could say that
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{3}(a_{3},a_{2},a_{1})(a_{0})
is defined by the right hand picture in
Fig.

1
, counted with the same signs as for
\mu_{S}^{4}(a_{3},\dots,a_{0})
but with a sign twist of
(-1)^{\lvert a_{0}\rvert}
. Or we could say that the
\mu_{\mathbf{L}}^{l}
operation on
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
is defined by
Fig.

3
but with a sign twist of
(-1)^{\triangle_{l}+l-1}
.

2.6.
Hochschild cohomology

Next we recall various versions of the Hochschild cochain complex. If
\mathcal{C}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
- (or
\mathbb{Z}/2
-)graded
A_{\infty}
-category over
\mathbf{k}
then the
\mathbb{Z}
- (respectively
\mathbb{Z}/2
-)graded Hochschild cochain complex
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
is given by

\operatorname{CC}^{t}(\mathcal{C})=\prod_{k\geq 0}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{objects}\\
O_{0},...,O_{k}\end{subarray}}\hom^{t}_{\mathbf{k}}(\hom^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(O_{k-1},O_{k})[1]\otimes\dots\otimes\hom^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(O_{0},O_{1})[1],\hom^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(O_{0},O_{k})).

The differential on this complex, with sign shifted as in (
8
), satisfies

(12)

\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{CC}}(\varphi)^{k}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})=\sum_{i,j}(-1)^{(\lvert\varphi\rvert-1)\maltese_{i}}\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k-j+1}(a_{k},\dots,a_{i+j+1},\varphi^{j}(a_{i+j},\dots,a_{i+1}),a_{i},\dots,a_{1})\\
+\sum_{i,j}(-1)^{\maltese_{i}+\lvert\varphi\rvert}\varphi^{k-j+1}(a_{k},\dots,a_{i+j+1},\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(a_{i+j},\dots,a_{i+1}),a_{i},\dots,a_{1})

for all
\varphi\in\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
, where
\maltese_{i}=\lvert a_{1}\rvert+\dots+\lvert a_{i}\rvert-i
. The complex carries
A_{\infty}
-operations defined by

\mu_{\operatorname{CC}}^{2}(\varphi_{2},\varphi_{1})^{k}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})=\sum(-1)^{(\lvert\varphi_{2}\rvert-1)\maltese_{l}+(\lvert\varphi_{1}\rvert-1)\maltese_{i}}\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{k-j-m+1}(a_{k},\dots,a_{l+m+1},\\
\varphi_{2}^{m}(a_{l+m},\dots,a_{l+1}),a_{l},\dots,a_{i+j+1},\varphi_{1}^{j}(a_{i+j},\dots,a_{i+1}),a_{i},\dots,a_{1})

and similarly for
\mu_{\operatorname{CC}}^{\geq 3}
. If
\mathcal{C}
is strictly unital then
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
is quasi-isomorphic to the
reduced
Hochschild complex
\overline{\operatorname{CC}}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
given by those
\varphi\in\operatorname{CC}^{*}
which vanish whenever one or more input is the strict unit.

We will mainly be interested in the case where
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
. We will also be interested in the Hochschild cohomology of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
with coefficients in
\mathrm{mf}
, viewed as a
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
-bimodule via
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
. This mixed Hochschild complex is given by

(13)

\operatorname{CC}^{t}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf})=\prod_{k\geq 0}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Lagrangians}\\
K_{0},...,K_{k}\end{subarray}}\hom^{t}_{\mathbf{k}}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(K_{k-1},K_{k})[1]\otimes\dots\otimes\operatorname{CF}^{*}(K_{0},K_{1})[1],\\
\hom^{*}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K_{0}),\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K_{k}))).

The differential, with sign modified via (
8
), is

\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{CC}}(\varphi)^{k}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})=\\
\smash{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,p,r\\
q_{1},\dots,q_{p}\\
s_{1},\dots,s_{r}\end{subarray}}}(-1)^{(\lvert\varphi\rvert-1)\maltese_{i}}\mu_{\mathrm{mf}}^{p+r+1}(\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{s_{r}}(a_{k},\dots,a_{k-s_{r}+1}),\dots,\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{s_{1}}(a_{i+j+s_{1}},\dots,a_{i+j+1}),\\
\hfill\varphi^{j}(a_{i+j},\dots,a_{i+1}),\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{q_{p}}(a_{i},\dots,a_{i-q_{p}+1}),\dots,\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{q_{1}}(a_{q_{1}},\dots,a_{1}))\\[6.45831pt]
+\sum_{i,j}(-1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert+\maltese_{i}}\varphi^{k-j+1}(a_{k},\dots,a_{i+j+1},\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{j}(a_{i+j},\dots,a_{i+1}),a_{i},\dots,a_{1})

To declutter the notation we will write expressions like this as

\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{CC}}(\varphi)=\sum(-1)^{(\lvert\varphi\rvert-1)\maltese_{i}}\mu_{\mathrm{mf}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\dots),\dots,\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\dots),\varphi(\dots),\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}\underbrace{(\dots),\dots,\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\dots)}_{i})\\
+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert+\maltese_{i}}\varphi(\dots,\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}}).

Note that the first
i
refers to the total number of inputs to the
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
operations to the right of
\varphi
, not to the number of
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
operations themselves. Since
\mathrm{mf}
is a dg-category, i.e.
\mu_{\mathrm{mf}}^{\geq 3}=0
, this simplifies to

(14)

\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{CC}}(\varphi)=\mu^{1}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\varphi(\dots))+\sum\mu^{2}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\dots),\varphi(\dots))+\sum(-1)^{(\lvert\varphi\rvert-1)\maltese_{i}}\mu^{2}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\varphi(\dots),\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}}))\\
+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert+\maltese_{i}}\varphi(\dots,\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}}).

Similarly, the product on
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf})
(with sign modified by (
9
)) simplifies to

(15)

\mu_{\operatorname{CC}}^{2}(\psi,\varphi)=\sum(-1)^{(\lvert\psi\rvert-1)\maltese_{i}}\mu^{2}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\psi(\dots),\varphi(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})),

and we have
\mu^{\geq 3}_{\operatorname{CC}}=0
. See
[
51
, Section (1d)]
for further discussion of this mixed Hochschild complex, viewed as endomorphisms of
\varphi
in the
A_{\infty}
-category of
A_{\infty}
-functors
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}\to\mathrm{mf}
.

Remark 2.12
.

For the ungraded Fukaya category, in characteristic
2
, we can define everything by the same formulae but ignoring the signs. Hochschild cohomology is itself ungraded in this case.

2.7.
The closed–open map

The last thing we need to recall for now is the closed–open string map, so fix a class
\alpha
in
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
and a pseudocycle
[
63
]

Z\to X
representing its Poincaré dual. We will use
Z
to define
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}(\alpha)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha)
.

The class
