\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}(\alpha)\in\operatorname{HH}^{\lvert\alpha\rvert}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})
is represented by the cocycle
\sigma
defined as follows. For each
k\geq 0
the component

\sigma^{k}:\operatorname{CF}^{*}(K_{k-1},K_{k})[1]\otimes\dots\otimes\operatorname{CF}^{*}(K_{0},K_{1})[1]\to\operatorname{CF}^{*}(K_{0},K_{k}),

counts rigid pseudoholomorphic discs with
k
inputs (anticlockwise) and a single output on the boundary, and with an interior marked point constrained to lie on
Z
. We depict this as shown on the left in
Fig.

4
, with the solid dot indicating the marked point on
Z
.

\cdot
\cdot
\cdot

Figure 4.
The cocycles
\sigma
(left) and
\sigma_{\mathbf{L}}
(right), representing
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}(\alpha)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha)
respectively.

This cocycle depends on a choice of regular perturbation data, which should be compatible with degeneration of the domain in the following sense. Whenever the domain degenerates, the induced perturbation data on the disc component carrying the interior marked point should agree with those defining
\sigma
, whilst the induced perturbation data on the other components should agree with those defining the
A_{\infty}
-operations
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
. Suitable perturbation data can be constructed by induction on
k
in the usual way, as in
[
51
, Section 9]
. The Hochschild cohomology class of
\sigma
is independent of this choice.

The class
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha)\in\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{\lvert\alpha\rvert}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{(\mathrm{op})}
is represented by the cocycle
\sigma_{\mathbf{L}}
that counts rigid pseudoholomorphic discs with a single interior marked point constrained to
Z
, and a single output on the boundary, as depicted on the right in
Fig.

4
. We can use arbitrary regular perturbation data that agree with the Floer datum for
\mathbf{L}
at the output. The signs are the same as those for
\sigma^{0}
(i.e. the
k=0
component of
\sigma
), which we will not need explicit expressions for.

The fact that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
is an algebra homomorphism is proved in
[
54
, Proposition 2.1]
. Applying the
k=0
part of this proof to
\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)_{W_{L}}
shows that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
is an algebra homomorphism to
\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
. Since
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
is graded-commutative,
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
is also an algebra homomorphism to
\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
.

The homomorphism
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
is unital because for
\alpha=1_{X}
, represented by
Z=X
, the cocycle
\sigma_{\mathbf{L}}
defines the cohomological unit in
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{(\mathrm{op})}

[
54
, Section 2.4]
. However, unitality of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
is more subtle. In
[
35
]
Ganatra introduces the
2
-pointed
Hochschild complex
\operatorname{{}_{2}CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})
, which describes endomorphisms of the diagonal bimodule, and a corresponding
2
-pointed closed–open map
\operatorname{{}_{2}\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
. We are slightly modifying his notation here to align it with ours. He constructs a chain map

\Psi:\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\to\operatorname{{}_{2}CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})

which intertwines
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
with
\operatorname{{}_{2}\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}

[
35
, Proposition 5.6]
, and which is a quasi-isomorphism when working over a field
[
35
, Proposition 2.5]
. Unitality of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
is then proved over
\mathbb{C}
in
[
54
,
48
]
by showing that
\operatorname{{}_{2}\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
is unital (over any ring) and invoking Ganatra’s results to transfer this (over a field) to
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
. Unitality of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
over an arbitrary coefficient ring seems to be unknown.

Remark 2.13
.

The restriction to field coefficients here is analogous to the projectivity hypothesis in
[
62
, Corollary 9.1.5]
, which interprets Hochschild cohomology in terms of bimodule
\operatorname{Ext}
groups.

3.
Proof of
A
and
B

With this setup in place, we are ready to prove our first two main theorems. Recall that
A
asserts the existence of a cohomologically unital
S
-linear
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphism

\Theta:\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}),

extending the module action of
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
on
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
in a way we shall make precise in
Section

3.3
.
B
then asserts that
\Theta
fits into a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}({\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}_{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}(\Theta)}

3.1.
Constructing
\Theta

First we define the map
\Theta
. To do this, for each tuple
c_{1},\dots,c_{l}
in
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
with
l\geq 1
we need to specify the element
\Theta^{l}(c_{l},\dots,c_{1})
in
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf})
. This amounts to defining, for each
k\geq 0
, each
(k+1)
-tuple of objects
K_{1},\dots,K_{k}
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, each
k
-tuple of morphisms

(16)

(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})\in\operatorname{CF}^{*}(K_{k-1},K_{k})\times\dots\times\operatorname{CF}^{*}(K_{0},K_{1}),

and each
x
in
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K_{0})=\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},K_{0})
, an element

(17)

\Theta^{l}(c_{l},\dots,c_{1})(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})(x)\in\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K_{k})=\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},K_{k}).

Definition 3.1
.

The element (
17
) is defined by counting analogous curves to those defining
\mu^{1}_{\mathrm{mf}}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
but with additional inputs on the thick segment of the boundary, and twisted by a sign. More precisely, we count rigid discs of the form shown in
Fig.

5
,

a_{1}
a_{k}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
c_{1}
c_{l}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
x

Figure 5.
The discs defining
\Theta(c_{l},\dots,c_{1})(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})(x)
.

with inputs
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}
anticlockwise on the ordinary segment of the boundary, and
c_{1},\dots,c_{l}
clockwise on the thick segment. Each disc contributes with the same sign as it would towards

\mu_{S}^{k+l+1}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1},x,c_{1},\dots,c_{l})

but with a twist of

(18)

(-1)^{(\square_{l}-1)(\lvert x\rvert-1)+\triangle_{l}+l-1}.

Here
\triangle_{l}=\sum_{i<j}(\lvert c_{i}\rvert-1)(\lvert c_{j}\rvert-1)
as before, and
\square_{l}=\sum_{j}(\lvert c_{j}\rvert-1)
; we call this
\square
rather than
\maltese
to distinguish it from the corresponding quantity for the
a_{i}
.

As usual, we have to choose regular perturbation data, and we do this by induction on the number of inputs, compatibly with degeneration of the domain in the following sense. After a degeneration, each of the components looks like the domain of a curve defining
\Theta
(but with strictly fewer
a
- or
c
-inputs),
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
,
\mu_{\mathbf{L}}
, or
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
, for which we have already defined perturbation data. We require that the perturbation data induced by the degeneration agree with those already defined on each component.

Remark 3.2
.

Discs of a similar form to
Fig.

5
, with a distinguished input and output separated by arbitrarily many inputs on either side, appear in the definition of Ganatra’s
2
-pointed closed–open map
[
35
, Section 5.6]
.

3.2.
\Theta
is a homomorphism

The heart of
A
is the following result.

Proposition 3.3
.

\Theta
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphism.

Before proving this, we introduce a useful shorthand: we add a bar to operations involving sign twists to denote the same operations without the sign twists. For example,
\mathop{\overline{\Theta}}\nolimits
,
\mathop{\overline{\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}}\nolimits
, and
\mathop{\overline{\mu}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{L}}
count the same discs as
\Theta
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
, and
\mu_{\mathbf{L}}
but with the signs that would be carried by the plain
\mu_{S}
operations with the corresponding inputs.

Proof of
Proposition

3.3
.

Consider the moduli spaces of discs analogous to those defining
\Theta
but in virtual dimension
1
. The boundaries of their compactifications comprise degenerate configurations illustrated in
Fig.

6
, and give rise to relations between
\Theta
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
,
\mu_{\mathbf{L}}
,
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
, and
\mu^{1}_{S}
, just as we obtained the
A_{\infty}
-functor equations for
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
in
Section

2.5
.

Figure 6.
Degenerations corresponding to terms in (
19
).

(The
\mu_{S}^{1}
terms represent squifferentials on matrix factorisations, modulo sign twists, and didn’t appear in the discussion of perturbation data in
Definition

3.1
since they do not correspond to degeneration of the domain.) The resulting relations are exactly analogous to those proving the
A_{\infty}
-relations for
\mu_{S}
, except for the twisting of signs in
\Theta
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
, and
\mu_{\mathbf{L}}
. Temporarily ignoring the sign twists, we obtain

(19)

\mu^{1}_{S}(\mathop{\overline{\Theta}}\nolimits(\dots)(\dots)(x))+(-1)^{\square_{l}}\mathop{\overline{\Theta}}\nolimits(\dots)(\dots)(\mu^{1}_{S}(x))+\sum(-1)^{\square_{l}-\square_{l-j}}\mathop{\overline{\Theta}}\nolimits(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\dots}},\mathop{\overline{\mu}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{L}}(\dots),\dots)(\dots)(x)\\
+\sum(-1)^{\square_{l}+\lvert x\rvert-1+\maltese_{i}}\mathop{\overline{\Theta}}\nolimits(\dots)(\dots,\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})(x)+\sum\mathop{\overline{\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}}\nolimits(\dots)(\mathop{\overline{\Theta}}\nolimits(\dots)(\dots)(x))\\
+\sum(-1)^{\square_{l}}\mathop{\overline{\Theta}}\nolimits(\dots)(\dots)(\mathop{\overline{\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}}\nolimits(\dots)(x))+\sum(-1)^{\square_{l}-\square_{j}}\mathop{\overline{\Theta}}\nolimits(\dots)(\dots)(\mathop{\overline{\Theta}}\nolimits(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\dots}})(\dots)(x))=0.

Some of the sums may be empty if the number of inputs is small. For example, if
k=0
(no
a
-inputs) then the fourth sum is empty, whilst if
l=1
(only one
c
-input) then the final sum is empty. The clockwise ordering of the
c
-inputs is needed to make the composition of
\mathop{\overline{\Theta}}\nolimits
operations in the final sum come out the right way round.

Next we reinstate the sign twists for
\Theta
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
, and
\mu_{\mathbf{L}}
, namely (
18
),
(-1)^{\lvert\text{distinguished input}\rvert}
, and (
10
). After simplifying, and cancelling off an overall factor of
(-1)^{\square_{l}(\lvert x\rvert-1)+\triangle_{l}+l}
, (
19
) becomes

(20)

\mu^{1}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\Theta(\dots)(\dots))+\sum(-1)^{\square_{l}+\maltese_{i}+1}\Theta(\dots)(\dots,\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})\\
+\sum\mu^{2}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\dots),\Theta(\dots)(\dots))+\sum(-1)^{\square_{l}\maltese_{i}}\mu^{2}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\Theta(\dots)(\dots),\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}}))\\
+\sum(-1)^{(\square_{l}-\square_{j})\maltese_{i}}\mu^{2}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\Theta(\dots)(\dots),\Theta(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\dots}})(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}}))\\
=\sum(-1)^{\square_{j}}\Theta(\dots,\mu_{\mathbf{L}}(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\dots}})(\dots).

Note that we have eliminated
x
and are now viewing this as an equality in

\hom_{\mathrm{mf}}^{*}(\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K_{0}),\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K_{k}))

rather than
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K_{k})
. Using the expressions (
14
) and (
15
) for
\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{CC}}
and
\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{CC}}
we can rewrite (
20
) as

\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{CC}}(\Theta(\dots))+\sum\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{CC}}(\Theta(\dots),\Theta(\dots))=\sum(-1)^{\square_{j}}\Theta(\dots,\mu_{\mathbf{L}}(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\dots}}).

This an equality in
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf})
, and is exactly the
A_{\infty}
-homomorphism equation for
\Theta
that we wanted to prove.
∎

3.3.
Compatibility with the module action

Next we explain, and prove, the sense in which
\Theta
extends the module action of
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
on
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
. First note that for each object
K
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
the matrix factorisation
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K)=\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},K)
is naturally a right module for
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
, and hence a left module for
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
. This module action is described by a chain map

T_{K}:\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{end}^{*}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K)),

which is given explicitly by

(21)

T_{K}(c)(x)=(-1)^{\lvert c\rvert\lvert x\rvert+\lvert c\rvert}\mu_{S}^{2}(x,c)

for
c\in\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
and
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K)
. Here the
(-1)^{\lvert c\rvert\lvert x\rvert}
is a Koszul sign whilst the
(-1)^{\lvert c\rvert}
corresponds to the usual sign (
9
) relating products to
\mu^{2}
operations.

The relationship between this module action and
\Theta
is then as follows.

Lemma 3.4
.

For each object
K
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, the projection of
\Theta^{1}
to length zero on
K
is homotopic to
T_{K}
.

Proof.

The projection of
\Theta^{1}
to length zero corresponds to the
l=1
,
k=0
case of
Definition

3.1
. Comparing this definition with (
21
) proves the result, after noting that (
18
) reduces to
(-1)^{\lvert c\rvert\lvert x\rvert+\lvert c\rvert}
. We only get ‘homotopic to’ since we didn’t specify that the perturbation data used to define
\Theta
should be compatible with those used to define
T_{K}
.
∎

The remaining assertion of
A
is that
\Theta
is cohomologically unital. We defer this to
Corollary

3.11
, since we will deduce it from
B
.

3.4.
Independence of choices

Our constructions so far depend on choices of Floer and perturbation data for
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
,
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
, and
\Theta
, as well as a choice of Lagrangians to allow as objects in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
. We now explain the sense in which the homomorphism
\Theta
is independent of these choices. We break this into two parts, depending on which choices are being changed, but these parts can of course be combined to deal with arbitrary changes of auxiliary data.

First, suppose we fix a choice of Floer and perturbation data for
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
. That is, we fix the domain of
\Theta
. Now suppose we have defined
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
, and
\Theta
using two different sets of auxiliary choices. We indicate these with subscripts
1
and
2
. The invariance result is then that the maps
\Theta_{1}
and
\Theta_{2}
are related by a zigzag, in the following sense.

Lemma 3.5
.

There exists a Fukaya category, denoted
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,+}
, containing
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,1}
and
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,2}
as full subcategories, such that the functors
{\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}_{i}
extend to
{\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}_{+}:\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,+}\to\mathrm{mf}
, and the homomorphisms
\Theta_{i}
extend to
\Theta_{+}:\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,+},\mathrm{mf})
. The following diagram then tautologically commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,1},\mathrm{mf})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,2},\mathrm{mf})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,+},\mathrm{mf}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{+}}
restrict
restrict

and if
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,1}
and
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,2}
have essentially the same objects, in the sense that any Lagrangian in one is split-generated by the Lagrangians in the other, then the two restriction arrows are quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

Except for the last part, this is a standard double category argument, as in
[
51
, Section (10a)]
. Concretely, we construct a category whose set of objects is the disjoint union of the sets of objects in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,1}
and
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,2}
. We call objects originating in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}

objects of type
i
. When defining operations involving only objects of type
i
, we use auxiliary data from
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
,
{\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}_{i}
, and
\Theta_{i}
. For operations involving mixtures of objects of the two types, we make arbitrary choices of auxiliary data, compatible with those already chosen.

Now suppose that
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,1}
and
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,2}
have essentially the same objects, so that the inclusions
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}\to\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,+}
are quasi-equivalences. The fact that the restriction maps induce isomorphisms on Hochschild cohomology then follows from standard Morita invariance properties. Explicitly: one applies the Eilenberg–Moore comparison theorem (
Theorem

4.5
) to the length filtration; because we’re working over a field this reduces the problem to the cohomology categories; and there one can give an explicit homotopy inverse
[
51
, Lemma 2.6]
.
∎

Now suppose instead that we fix auxiliary data for
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
, but choose two sets of data for
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
, again denoted by subscripts
1
and
2
. For each
i
we can construct a homomorphism
\Theta_{i}:\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}_{i}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf})
. As above, the invariance result is that these maps are related by a zigzag, but this time it has the following form.

Lemma 3.6
.

There exists a cohomologically unital
A_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
, equipped with a cohomologically unital
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphism

\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf})

and cohomologically unital
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphisms

I_{i}:\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}_{i}\to\mathcal{A},

such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CF_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CF_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{2}}

Proof.

We adapt the arguments of
[
46
, Section 2.4]
, based on the triangle algebra of
[
41
, Section 4.5]
, in conjunction with another double category argument. First, we view
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}_{1}
and
\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}_{2}
as the endomorphism algebras of objects
\mathbf{L}_{1}
and
\mathbf{L}_{2}
in
\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)_{W_{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. The usual Floer continuation map construction gives a quasi-isomorphism
f:\mathbf{L}_{1}\to\mathbf{L}_{2}
, and we define
\mathcal{A}
to be

\begin{pmatrix}\hom^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{1},\mathbf{L}_{1})&\hom^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{2},\mathbf{L}_{1})[1]\\
0&\hom^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{2},\mathbf{L}_{2})\end{pmatrix},

with
A_{\infty}
-operations given by summing over
\mu_{S}
with all possible insertions of
f
. All such sums are finite since we can never insert more than one copy of
f
.

This
\mathcal{A}
is the upper-triangular version of the triangle algebra in
[
46
]
, and has cohomological unit

\left[\begin{pmatrix}e_{\mathbf{L}_{1}}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\right]=\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&e_{\mathbf{L}_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\right].

Following that paper, but dualising (i.e. reversing directions of maps), we define the maps
I_{i}
by taking
I_{i}^{1}
to be the inclusion of the
i
th diagonal summand into
\mathcal{A}
, and taking all higher components
I_{i}^{\geq 2}
to be zero. These maps are immediately seen to be cohomologically unital
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphisms, and are quasi-isomorphisms by dualising
[
46
, Lemma 2.17]
.

Next, we define
\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}
in a similar way to
\Theta
but with perturbation data depending on where the inputs live (i.e. in which
\hom^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{i},\mathbf{L}_{j})
), and again summing over all possible insertions of
f
. We choose these perturbation data to coincide with those defining
\Theta_{i}
when all inputs lie in
\hom^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{i},\mathbf{L}_{i})
. The composition
\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}\circ I_{i}
then tautologically coincides with
\Theta_{i}
, completing the proof.
∎

Since we are working over a field
\mathbf{k}
, the
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphisms are automatically invertible. We could therefore straighten out the zigzags in the above results if desired.

3.5.
Transferring the closed–open map to matrix factorisations

We next prove commutativity of the diagram in
B
. Explicitly, for an arbitrary class
\alpha\in\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
we want to show that

\operatorname{H}({\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}_{*})\circ\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}(\alpha)=\operatorname{H}(\Theta)\circ\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha).

After fixing a pseudocycle
Z\to X
Poincaré dual to
\alpha
, and choosing suitable perturbation data, we have cocycles
\sigma\in\operatorname{CC}^{\lvert\alpha\rvert}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})
and
\sigma_{\mathbf{L}}\in\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{\lvert\alpha\rvert}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
representing
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}(\alpha)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha)
respectively, as defined in
Section

2.7
. Our task is then to show that
{\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}_{*}(\sigma)-\Theta^{1}(\sigma_{\mathbf{L}})
is exact, which we shall do by constructing geometrically a Hochschild cochain
\tau\in\operatorname{CC}^{\lvert\alpha\rvert-1}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf})
satisfying

\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{CC}}(\tau)={\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}_{*}(\sigma)-\Theta^{1}(\sigma_{\mathbf{L}}).

Such a
\tau
is specified by its values
\tau^{k}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})(x)
, for
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}
as in (
16
) and
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K_{0})=\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},K_{0})
.

Definition 3.7
.

The element
\tau^{k}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})(x)\in\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(K_{k})=\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},K_{k})
is defined by counting rigid discs of the form shown in
Fig.

7
.

x
a_{1}
a_{k}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot

Figure 7.
The definition of
\tau^{k}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})(x)
.

As in
Section

2.7
, the solid dot indicates an interior marked point constrained to lie on
Z
. Each disc counts with the same sign as it would towards
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}(\alpha)(a_{k},\dots,a_{1},x)
but twisted by
(-1)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert\lvert x\rvert+\lvert\alpha\rvert+\lvert x\rvert}
. We write
\mathop{\overline{\tau}}\nolimits
for the same cochain but without the sign twist. (Of course,
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}(\alpha)(a_{k},\dots,a_{1},x)
does not strictly make sense, since
x
is a morphism in
\mathcal{F}_{S}(X)
rather than
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, but this is irrelevant for the purpose of defining signs.)

We choose regular perturbation data by induction, compatibly with degeneration of the domain in the following sense. After degeneration, each component looks like the domain of a curve defining
\tau
(but with strictly fewer inputs),
\sigma
,
\Theta^{1}
,
\sigma_{\mathbf{L}}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
, or
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
, and we require that perturbation data induced by the degeneration agree with those already defined. See
Fig.

8
for examples of codimension-
1
degenerations.

Figure 8.
Example codimension-
1
degenerations of a
\tau^{2}(a_{2},a_{1})
disc. From left to right:
\Theta^{1}(\sigma_{\mathbf{L}})(a_{2},a_{1})
;
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{1}(a_{2})\circ\tau^{1}(a_{1})
;
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{3}(\sigma^{0},a_{2},a_{1})
;
\tau^{1}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}(a_{2},a_{1}))
.

We ask that the perturbation data induced by the degeneration agrees with those already defined on each component.

Remark 3.8
.

The relationship between
\tau
and
\sigma
is analogous to the relationship between
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
and
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
(add a single thick segment to the boundary). Similarly, the relationship between
\tau
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
is analogous to the relationship between
\sigma
and
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
(add an interior marked point constrained to
Z
).

We now verify that
\tau
has the desired property.

Proposition 3.9
.

The cochain
\tau
satisfies

\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{CC}}(\tau)={\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}_{*}(\sigma)-\Theta^{1}(\sigma_{\mathbf{L}}).

Passing to cohomology gives the following.

Corollary 3.10
.

For any
\alpha
in
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
we have

\operatorname{H}({\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}_{*})\circ\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}(\alpha)=\operatorname{H}^{*}(\Theta)\circ\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha),

i.e. the diagram in
B
commutes.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.9
.

Consider the boundaries of the compactified moduli spaces of discs equivalent to those defining
\tau
but in virtual dimension
1
. This gives relations analogous to those proving that
\sigma
is a Hochschild cocycle, i.e.

(22)

\sum(-1)^{(\lvert\alpha\rvert-1)\maltese_{i}}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\dots,\sigma(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert+\maltese_{i}}\sigma(\dots,\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})=0,

except for the presence of
\mathbf{L}
and the twisting of signs in
\tau
,
\Theta
, and
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
. The analogue of (
22
) is then

\mathop{\overline{\Theta}}\nolimits^{1}(\sigma_{\mathbf{L}})(\dots)(x)+\sum\mathop{\overline{\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}}\nolimits(\dots)(\mathop{\overline{\tau}}\nolimits(\dots)(x))+\mu^{1}_{S}(\mathop{\overline{\tau}}\nolimits(\dots)(x))\\
+\sum(-1)^{(\lvert\alpha\rvert-1)(\lvert x\rvert-1+\maltese_{i})}\mathop{\overline{\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}}\nolimits(\dots,\sigma(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})(x)+(-1)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert}\mathop{\overline{\tau}}\nolimits(\dots)(\mu^{1}_{S}(x))\\
+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert}\mathop{\overline{\tau}}\nolimits(\dots)(\mathop{\overline{\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}}\nolimits(\dots)(x))+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert+\lvert x\rvert-1+\maltese_{i}}\mathop{\overline{\tau}}\nolimits(\dots,\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})(x)=0

for all
x
. Passing back to the sign-twisted versions and cancelling off
(-1)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert(\lvert x\rvert-1)}
, this becomes

\Theta^{1}(\sigma^{0}_{\mathbf{L}})(\dots)(x)+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert+\maltese_{i}+1}\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\dots)(\tau(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})(x))+(-1)^{\lvert x\rvert}\mu^{1}_{S}(\tau(\dots)(x))\\
+\sum(-1)^{(\lvert\alpha\rvert-1)\maltese_{i}+1}\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\dots,\sigma(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})(x)+(-1)^{\lvert x\rvert-1}\tau(\dots)(\mu^{1}_{S}(x))\\
+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert\maltese_{i}+\maltese_{i}+1}\tau(\dots)(\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})(x))+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert-1+\maltese_{i}}\tau(\dots,\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})(x)=0.

The third and fifth terms combine to give
\mu^{1}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\tau(\dots))(x)
, so we can drop the explicit
x
and get

\Theta^{1}(\sigma^{0}_{\mathbf{L}})(\dots)+\sum\mu^{2}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\dots),\tau(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}}))+\mu^{1}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\tau(\dots))-\sum(-1)^{(\lvert\alpha\rvert-1)\maltese_{i}}\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\dots,\sigma(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})\\
+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert\maltese_{i}}\mu^{2}_{\mathrm{mf}}(\tau(\dots),\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}}))+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert-1+\maltese_{i}}\tau(\dots,\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})=0.

The second, third, fifth, and sixth terms are
\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{CC}}(\tau)
, using (
14
), whilst the fourth term is
-{\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}}_{*}\sigma
, so rearranging gives the result.
∎

3.6.
Unitality of
\Theta

It is now easy to complete the proof of
A
.

Corollary 3.11
.

The
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphism
\Theta
is cohomologically unital.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the commutative diagram in
B
using the unitality of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
, and
\operatorname{H}((\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L}[\lambda])_{*})
.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

We mentioned in
1.16
that
A
holds over an arbitrary ground ring. However, the above proof of cohomological unitality breaks down because, as noted at the end of
Section

2.7
, it is really the
2
-pointed closed–open map
\operatorname{{}_{2}\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
that is known to be unital, and this is only known to coincide with
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
over a field. To prove that
\Theta
is cohomologically unital over an arbitrary ring one can use the resemblance between
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf})
and the
2
-pointed Hochschild complex to directly mimic the proof of unitality of the
\operatorname{{}_{2}\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
in
[
54
, Lemma 2.3]
. We leave the details to the interested reader.

4.
Proof of
C

Unsurprisingly, in this section we prove
C
. Recall that the setup is as follows. We denote by
\mathcal{E}
the matrix factorisation
\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},L^{\flat})
given by the image of
L^{\flat}\in\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
under
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
, and by
\mathcal{B}
its endomorphism dg-algebra. The map
\Theta
induces an
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphism

\Theta_{L^{\flat}}:\operatorname{CF}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\mathcal{B}).

The monodromy of the local system
\mathcal{L}
on
L^{\flat}
corresponds to a homomorphism
S\to\mathbf{k}
, whose kernel we denote by
\mathfrak{m}
. For any ideal
I
in
S
contained in
\mathfrak{m}
we write
R
for
S/I
and
\widehat{R}
for its
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completion. All of the constructions we previously made over
S
we can make over
\widehat{R}
instead, and we denote the results by
\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{(\mathrm{op})}
,
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
, and so on. The statement of
C
is that

\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}:\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}})

is a quasi-isomorphism, i.e. that the chain map
\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}^{1}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

4.1.
Simplifying the complexes

The chain map
\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}^{1}
is
S
-linear (in fact,
\widehat{R}
-linear) so respects the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic filtrations on the domain and codomain and induces a map between the associated gradeds. The first step towards proving that it is a quasi-isomorphism is to study these filtrations and associated gradeds, which are much simpler. This is the goal of the present subsection.

As preliminary step note that
S=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
is a quotient of a Laurent polynomial ring over
\mathbf{k}
in finitely many variables (since
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
is finitely generated), so is Noetherian. Thus
\mathfrak{m}
is finitely generated, and so the following properties hold.

Lemma 4.1
.

(i)

\widehat{R}
is
\mathfrak{m}
-adically complete, i.e. the natural map
\widehat{R}\to\varprojlim\widehat{R}/\mathfrak{m}^{p}\widehat{R}
is an isomorphism.

For all
p
and
q
with
p\leq q
we have:

(ii)

\mathfrak{m}^{p}\widehat{R}=\ker(\widehat{R}\to R/\mathfrak{m}^{p})
, and this is the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completion
\widehat{\mathfrak{m}^{p}R}
of
\mathfrak{m}^{p}R=(\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/I
.

(iii)

\mathfrak{m}^{p}\widehat{R}/\mathfrak{m}^{q}\widehat{R}=\mathfrak{m}^{p}R/\mathfrak{m}^{q}R=(\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/(\mathfrak{m}^{q}+I)
, and this is finite-dimensional over
\mathbf{k}
.

Proof.

Parts
(i)
and
(ii)
are special cases of
[
59
, Lemma 10.96.3]
.

The second equality in
(iii)
is obvious, whilst the first comes from taking the short exact sequence

0\to\mathfrak{m}^{q}R\to\mathfrak{m}^{p}R\to\mathfrak{m}^{p}R/\mathfrak{m}^{q}R\to 0,

applying
[
59
, Lemma 10.96.4]
to get a short exact sequence
0\to\widehat{\mathfrak{m}^{q}R}\to\widehat{\mathfrak{m}^{p}R}\to\mathfrak{m}^{p}R/\mathfrak{m}^{q}R\to 0
, and then using
(ii)
to rewrite this as
0\to\mathfrak{m}^{q}\widehat{R}\to\mathfrak{m}^{p}\widehat{R}\to\mathfrak{m}^{p}R/\mathfrak{m}^{q}R\to 0
. To prove finite-dimensionality, it suffices to show that
\mathfrak{m}^{p}/\mathfrak{m}^{q}
is finite-dimensional, and by induction it is enough to deal with the case
q=p+1
. For this, note that
\mathfrak{m}^{p}/\mathfrak{m}^{p+1}\cong(R/\mathfrak{m})\otimes_{R}\mathfrak{m}^{p}
, and
R/\mathfrak{m}=\mathbf{k}
, so a finite
\mathbf{k}
-spanning set for
\mathfrak{m}^{p}/\mathfrak{m}^{p+1}
can be obtained by taking a finite generating set for
\mathfrak{m}^{p}
and tensoring with
1\in R/\mathfrak{m}
.
∎

We can now prove the two results we need.

Lemma 4.2
.

\operatorname{CF}_{\widehat{R}}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
is
\mathfrak{m}
-adically complete and for each
p
we have an identification of complexes

\operatorname{gr}^{p}\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}=((\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/(\mathfrak{m}^{p+1}+I))\otimes\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}.

Undecorated tensor products are over
\mathbf{k}
, as usual, and the differential on the right-hand side is defined to be the identity on the first tensor factor and the ordinary Floer differential on the second.

Proof.

First note that
CF^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
is a free
\widehat{R}
-module of finite rank. So completeness follows from
Lemma

4.1
(i)
, whilst the description of
\operatorname{gr}^{p}CF^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
at the level of vector spaces follows from
Lemma

4.1
(iii)
. The differential on
\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
is given in our diagrammatic notation by counting discs with thick boundary, a single boundary input, and a single boundary output. Reducing modulo
\mathfrak{m}
converts the thick boundary, which corresponds to an
S
or
\widehat{R}
weight, to an ordinary boundary, corresponding to the parallel transport of the local system
\mathcal{L}
. In other words, it reduces the differential to that on
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}
, with the
((\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/(\mathfrak{m}^{p+1}+I))
factor just coming along for the ride.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}})
is
\mathfrak{m}
-adically complete and for each
p
we have an identification of complexes

(23)

\operatorname{gr}^{p}\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}})=((\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/(\mathfrak{m}^{p+1}+I))\otimes\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))).

The differential on the left-hand side is the associated graded of (
14
), restricted to the single object
L^{\flat}
so that
\mu_{\mathrm{mf}}
becomes
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}
becomes
\widehat{\Phi}
. The differential on the right-hand side is defined by the same formula reduced modulo
\mathfrak{m}
on the second tensor factor, and by the identity on the first tensor factor.

Proof.

For each
p
the ideal
\mathfrak{m}^{p}\subset S
is finitely generated, say by
s_{1},\dots,s_{k}
, so we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{p}\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}s_{j}\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),s_{j}\widehat{\mathcal{B}})=\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\mathfrak{m}^{p}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}).

We also have for all
p
and
q
with
p\leq q
that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{p}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}/\mathfrak{m}^{q}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\hom^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\mathbf{L},L^{\flat}),\mathfrak{m}^{p}\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\mathbf{L},L^{\flat})/\mathfrak{m}^{q}\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\mathbf{L},L^{\flat}))

\displaystyle=\hom^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\mathbf{L},L^{\flat}),((\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/(\mathfrak{m}^{q}+I))\otimes\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))

\displaystyle=\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),((\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/(\mathfrak{m}^{q}+I))\otimes\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))

\displaystyle=((\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/(\mathfrak{m}^{q}+I))\otimes\operatorname{end}^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})).

The second equality follows from similar arguments to
Lemma

4.2
.

We thus have for all
p
that

\displaystyle\operatorname{gr}^{p}\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\mathfrak{m}^{p}\widehat{\mathcal{B}})\big/\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\mathfrak{m}^{p+1}\widehat{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{r\geq 0}\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})[1]^{\otimes r},\operatorname{gr}^{p}\widehat{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{r\geq 0}((\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/(\mathfrak{m}^{p+1}+I))\otimes\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})[1]^{\otimes r},\operatorname{end}^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})))

\displaystyle=((\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/(\mathfrak{m}^{p+1}+I))\otimes\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))),

proving the second part of the lemma. In the fourth equality we used finite-dimensionality from
Lemma

4.1
(iii)
.

For the first part of the lemma, note that we similarly have

\displaystyle\varprojlim\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}})/\mathfrak{m}^{p}\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{r\geq 0}\varprojlim\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})[1]^{\otimes r},\widehat{\mathcal{B}}/\mathfrak{m}^{p}\widehat{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{r\geq 0}\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})[1]^{\otimes r},\varprojlim\widehat{\mathcal{B}}/\mathfrak{m}^{p}\widehat{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}}).

Here in the last equality we used completeness of
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
, which follows from
Lemma

4.1
(i)
plus the fact that
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
is a free
\widehat{R}
-module of finite rank.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

An equivalent way to describe the differential on the second tensor factor on the right-hand side of (
23
) is that we view
\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
as a
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
-bimodule via
\operatorname{gr}\widehat{\Phi}
, which is the map induced by the Yoneda module
Y_{L^{\flat}}
, viewed as an
A_{\infty}
-functor
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}\to\mathrm{Ch}_{\mathbf{k}}
(chain complexes over
\mathbf{k}
). Explicitly, writing
\mu^{*}_{\operatorname{end}}
for the
A_{\infty}
-algebra operations on
\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
, the bimodule operations are

(24)

\begin{gathered}\mu^{0|1|0}(\zeta)(x)=-\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{end}}(\zeta)(x)=(-1)^{\lvert x\rvert+1}\big(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}(\zeta(x))-\zeta(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}(x))\big),\\
\mu^{k|1|0}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1},\zeta)(x)=-\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{end}}(Y_{L^{\flat}}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1}),\zeta)(x)=(-1)^{\lvert x\rvert+1}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1},\zeta(x)),\\
\mu^{0|1|l}(\zeta,a_{l},\dots,a_{1})(x)=(-1)^{\maltese_{l}+1}\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{end}}(\zeta,Y_{L^{\flat}}(a_{l},\dots,a_{1}))(x)=(-1)^{\lvert x\rvert}\zeta(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(a_{l},\dots,a_{1},x)),\\
\quad\mu^{k|1|l}=0.\end{gathered}

Here
k
and
l
are positive integers,
x
and the
a_{i}
are in
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
, and
\zeta
is in
\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
. The differential on
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})))
, as defined by (
14
) (restricted to
L^{\flat}
and reduced modulo
\mathfrak{m}
), is expressed in terms of these bimodule operations by (
50
).

4.2.
Applying Eilenberg–Moore

To leverage the simplifications we have just made, we will use the Eilenberg–Moore comparison theorem. Before stating this, recall that a filtration
F^{p}C
of a complex
C
is
exhaustive
if
\bigcup_{p}F^{p}C=C
and
complete
if the natural map
C\to\varprojlim C/F^{p}C
is an isomorphism.

Theorem 4.5

(Eilenberg–Moore comparison theorem
[
23
]
,
[
62
, Theorem 5.5.11]
)
.

Suppose
C
and
D
are cochain complexes equipped with filtrations that are exhaustive and complete, and
f:C\to D
is a filtered chain map. If there exists
r\geq 0
such that
f
induces an isomorphism between the
r
th pages,
E_{r}
, of the associated spectral sequences, then
f
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Unsurprisingly, we shall apply this to the chain map

\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}^{1}:\operatorname{CF}_{\widehat{R}}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}}),

where the domain and codomain are both equipped with the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic filtrations. By
Lemmas

4.2
and
4.3
these filtrations are complete, and they are obviously exhaustive. We already noted that
\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}^{1}
preserves the filtrations, so, by Eilenberg–Moore, the generalised
C
is implied by the following result.

Proposition 4.6
.

The map
\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}^{1}
induces an isomorphism between the
E_{1}
pages of the associated spectral sequences. In other words, the associated graded map

\operatorname{gr}\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}^{1}:\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}})

is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

By
Lemmas

4.2
and
4.3
, for each
p
we can view
\operatorname{gr}^{p}\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}^{1}
as a map

((\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/(\mathfrak{m}^{p+1}+I))\otimes\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}\to((\mathfrak{m}^{p}+I)/(\mathfrak{m}^{p+1}+I))\otimes\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))).

By inspecting the definition of
\Theta
, this map can be written as
\operatorname{id}_{(I+\mathfrak{m}^{p})/(I+\mathfrak{m}^{p+1})}\otimes\theta
, where
\theta
is the reduction of
\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}^{1}
modulo
\mathfrak{m}
. This can be seen by ‘reducing diagrams mod
\mathfrak{m}
’ as in
Lemma

4.2
. To show that
\operatorname{gr}\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}^{1}
is a quasi-isomorphism it therefore suffices to show that

\theta:\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})))

is a quasi-isomorphism. And from the construction of
\Theta
in
Definition

3.1
we have an explicit formula for
\theta
, namely

(25)

\theta(c)(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})(x)=(-1)^{\lvert c\rvert(\lvert x\rvert-1)}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1},x,c)

for all
c
,
a_{i}
, and
x
in
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{(\mathrm{op})}
.

In
Appendix

B
we study the purely algebraic problem of understanding complexes of the form
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\hom_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{N}))
, where
\mathcal{A}
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra,
\mathcal{N}
is a (left)
\mathcal{A}
-module, and both are cohomologically unital. Here
\hom_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{N})
is viewed as an
\mathcal{A}
-bimodule using the
\mathcal{A}
-module actions on
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{N}
. In the case where
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{N}=\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
, this bimodule action on
\hom_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{N})
agrees with the one defined on
\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
by (
24
). This can be proved by directly comparing (
24
) with (
51
) after setting
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}=\mu_{\mathcal{N}}=-\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
in the latter. The minus sign here is our standard convention for viewing an
A_{\infty}
-algebra as a module over itself.

We can therefore apply the analysis of
Appendix

B
to
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})))
. In particular,
Corollary

B.4
tells us that the map

\Pi:\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})))\to\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})

given by
\Pi(\varphi)=(-1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert}\varphi^{0}(e_{L^{\flat}})
is a quasi-isomorphism. Here
e_{L^{\flat}}
is a chain-level representative for the unit in
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
. To complete the proof of
Proposition

4.6
, and hence of the generalised
C
, it therefore suffices to show that the chain map
\Pi\circ\theta:\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
is a quasi-isomorphism.

To do this, we just plug (
25
) into the definition of
\Pi
. We see that for all
c\in\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{(\mathrm{op})}

\Pi\circ\theta(c)=(-1)^{\lvert c\rvert}\theta(c)^{0}(e_{L^{\flat}})=\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(e_{L^{\flat}},c).

When
c
is a cocycle, this is cohomologous to
(-1)^{\lvert c\rvert}c
by definition of cohomological unitality. Therefore
\Pi\circ\theta
induces the map
(-1)^{*}
on
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{(\mathrm{op})}
, which is an isomorphism.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

The reader may be puzzled by the fact that
\Pi\circ\theta
is supposed to be a chain map, but the map
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
given by
c\mapsto\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(e_{L^{\flat}},c)
intertwines
\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}
on the domain with
minus

\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}
on the codomain. This mystery is resolved when one notes that the domain is being treated as an
A_{\infty}
-algebra but the codomain is being treated (via
Corollary

B.4
) as an
A_{\infty}
-module. So
-\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}
is indeed the correct
\mu^{1}
operation on the codomain.

5.
Reduction mod
\mathfrak{m}^{2}
and Tonkonog’s criterion

In
[
60
]
Tonkonog considers a loop
\gamma
of Hamiltonian symplectomorphisms of
X
preserving our Lagrangian
L
setwise. Associated to
\gamma
is a
Seidel element

S(\gamma)\in\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, introduced in
[
49
]
, and Tonkonog’s goal is to compute
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}(S(\gamma))\in\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
. In
Section

5.1
we recall his main result, which is a criterion for
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}(S(\gamma))
to be linearly independent of the Hochschild cohomology unit, or more generally for
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)
to be linearly independent of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}(Q)
for arbitrary fixed
Q\in\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
. We then discuss the reduction of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{2}
, and how this gives a new interpretation of his result (
D
), explaining the potentially mysterious hypothesis in a natural way. Throughout this section we use a pearl model for Floer cohomology, as constructed by Biran–Cornea
[
9
]
, and we write
\mu^{k}
(without subscript) for the
A_{\infty}
-operations on
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
.

5.1.
Tonkonog’s criterion

Let
\rho:\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbf{k}^{\times}
denote the monodromy of the local system
\mathcal{L}
on
L^{\flat}
, as usual, and let
l\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
denote the homology class of an orbit of
\gamma
on
L
. Building on work of Charette–Cornea
[
12
]
, Tonkonog first calculates
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(S(\gamma))
.

Proposition 5.1

(
[
60
, Theorem 1.7(a)]
)
.

We have

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(S(\gamma))=(-1)^{\varepsilon(l)}\rho(l)\cdot 1_{L^{\flat}}\in\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),

where
1_{L^{\flat}}
is the unit in
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
and
(-1)^{\varepsilon(l)}
is a specific sign that is unimportant for our purposes.

Now assume that
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\neq 0
. We then have a canonical linear
PSS map

\operatorname{PSS}:\operatorname{H}^{1}(L)\to\operatorname{HF}^{1}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),

introduced by Albers
[
4
]
. In the context of the pearl model
[
9
]
, this map comes from the inclusion of degree
1
Morse cochains into the pearl complex. Tonkonog’s criterion then reads as follows.

Theorem 5.2

(
[
60
, Theorem 1.7(c)]
)
.

For
Q\in\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, if the classes
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}(Q)
are
\mathbf{k}
-linearly dependent in
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
then

(
\Diamond
)

\begin{gathered}\text{there exists $a\in\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})$ such that for all $y\in\operatorname{H}^{1}(L)$ we have}\\
\mu^{2}(a,\operatorname{PSS}(y))+\mu^{2}(\operatorname{PSS}(y),a)=\langle y,l\rangle\cdot\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(Q).\end{gathered}

Remark 5.3
.

The statement in
[
60
]
includes a factor of
(-1)^{\varepsilon(l)}\rho(l)
on the right-hand side, but this is irrelevant since it can be absorbed into
a
.

5.2.
Reduction modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{2}

By
B
and
C
, if
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}(Q)
are linearly dependent in
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
then
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)
and
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q)
are linearly dependent in
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{\widehat{R}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}
. Here
\widehat{R}
is the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completion of
R=S/I
, where
I
is any ideal of
S
contained in
\mathfrak{m}
. We shall show that
Theorem

5.2
is really a statement about linear dependence of
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)
and
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q)
for
I=\mathfrak{m}^{2}
. Note that in this situation
R
is already
\mathfrak{m}
-adically complete so
\widehat{R}=R
. But we will leave the hat on
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
to distinguish if from
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
(which is over
S
, rather than
R
).

Our first step is to describe the algebra
R=S/\mathfrak{m}^{2}
itself.

Lemma 5.4
.

There is a natural isomorphism of unital
\mathbf{k}
-algebras

R\cong\mathbf{k}\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}(L),

where the product on
\mathbf{k}\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
is defined by
(\lambda_{1},\sigma_{1})(\lambda_{2},\sigma_{2})=(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\sigma_{2}+\lambda_{2}\sigma_{1})
.

Proof.

As a
\mathbf{k}
-vector space,
S
splits as
\mathbf{k}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
, where the first summand is spanned by the unit. The induced product on
\mathbf{k}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
is

(\lambda_{1},m_{1})(\lambda_{2},m_{2})=(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}m_{2}+\lambda_{2}m_{1}+m_{1}m_{2}).

Then
S/\mathfrak{m}^{2}\cong\mathbf{k}\oplus(\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2})
, with product of the form claimed in the lemma, and it remains to show that
\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}
is naturally identified with
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
.

Define
\alpha:S\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
by
z^{\gamma}\mapsto\rho(\gamma)\gamma
, extended
\mathbf{k}
-linearly. Here
z
is the formal variable in the group algebra
S=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
, and
\rho
is the monodromy representation
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbf{k}^{\times}
as above. The ideal
\mathfrak{m}
is generated by the
z^{\gamma}-\rho(\gamma)
, from which it’s easily checked that
\alpha(\mathfrak{m}^{2})=0
, so
\alpha
induces a linear map
\bar{\alpha}:\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
. This will be our isomorphism.

To construct the inverse, consider the
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinear map
\mathbf{k}\times\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}
given by

(\lambda,\gamma)\mapsto\lambda(\rho(\gamma)^{-1}z^{\gamma}-1).

Note that linearity in
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
follows from the fact that for all
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
the element

(\rho(\gamma_{1})^{-1}z^{\gamma_{1}}-1)(\rho(\gamma_{2})^{-1}z^{\gamma_{2}}-1)

lies in
\mathfrak{m}^{2}
, so

\rho(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})^{-1}z^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}}-1=(\rho(\gamma_{1})^{-1}z^{\gamma_{1}}-1)+(\rho(\gamma_{2})^{-1}z^{\gamma_{2}}-1)

in
\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}
. This
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinear map induces a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear map
\beta:\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)=\mathbf{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}
which is manifestly
\mathbf{k}
-linear. One can then check directly that
\bar{\alpha}
and
\beta
are mutually inverse.
∎

Our next goal is to understand the algebra
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{(\mathrm{op})}
, and we will drop the
(\mathrm{op})
as it is irrelevant for present purposes. By splitting
R
as
\mathbf{k}\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
we get an identification

(26)

\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})=\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\oplus(\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)\otimes\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})).

Here, unsurprisingly,
\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
means the Floer complex of
\mathbf{L}
over
R
, i.e. the reduction of
\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
modulo
I=\mathfrak{m}^{2}
. With respect to this splitting the differential has the form

\begin{pmatrix}\mu^{1}&0\\
\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)}&\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)}\otimes\mu^{1}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mu_{\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)}:\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)\otimes\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
denotes the usual
\mu^{1}_{S}
differential but with
S
coefficients reduced to
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
via the map
\alpha
from the proof of
Lemma

5.4
.

Filtering the complex using this splitting, which is essentially equivalent to filtering
\mathfrak{m}
-adically, we obtain a spectral sequence whose
E_{1}
page is

\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\oplus(\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)\otimes\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})),

with differential
\mathrm{d}_{1}
induced by
\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)}
. Later differentials trivially vanish, so we get a surjection

\pi:\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})\to\ker\mathrm{d}_{1}\subset\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),

induced by projection of the
E_{1}
page to the first summand. The kernel of
\pi
is
\operatorname{coker}\mathrm{d}_{1}
, mapping into
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
via the map

\iota:\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)\otimes\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})

induced by inclusion of the second summand into the
E_{1}
page. Using the description of the product from
Lemma

5.4
we get

w\cdot\iota(x)=\iota(\pi(w)\cdot x)

for all
w\in\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
and
x\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)\otimes\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
. Note also that for
Q\in\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
we have
\pi(\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q))=\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(Q)
.

Remark 5.5
.

If we had used a Hamiltonian chord model for
\operatorname{CF}^{*}
, rather than a pearl model, then (
26
) would not quite be true.
\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
would still be a free
R
-module whose reduction modulo
\mathfrak{m}
was equal to
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
, but there would not be a canonical identification of the former with
R\otimes\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
. This is because the generators of
\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
would be morphism spaces between
different
fibres (namely, the fibres over the endpoints of each chord) of an abstract rank-
1
local system over
R
. Things simplify in the pearl model since we effectively use chords of length zero, so the generators are endomorphism spaces of single fibres, and these endomorphism spaces are canonically identified with
R
.

With this in place we are ready to reprove Tonkonog’s criterion.

5.3.
Tonkonog’s criterion revisited

Fix an arbitrary
Q\in\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
. We wish to show that if

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}(Q)

are
\mathbf{k}
-linearly dependent in
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
then condition (

◇

‣
5.2
) holds. We will in fact prove the following result, which implies Tonkonog’s criterion by the discussion at the beginning of
Section

5.2
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Taking
\widehat{R}=R=S/\mathfrak{m}^{2}
as above, the classes
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)
and
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q)
are
\mathbf{k}
-linearly dependent in
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
if and only if condition (

◇

‣
5.2
) holds.

Proof.

Suppose first that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(Q)=0
. Then
\pi(\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q))=0
(notation as in
Section

5.2
) so
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q)=\iota(x)
for some
x\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)\otimes\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
. Thus

\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)=\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma))\cdot\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q)=\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma))\cdot\iota(x)=\iota(\pi(\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma)))\cdot x)\\
=\iota(\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(S(\gamma))\cdot x)=\iota((-1)^{\varepsilon(l)}\rho(l)\cdot x)=(-1)^{\varepsilon(l)}\rho(l)\cdot\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q),

where the fifth equality uses
Proposition

5.1
. So
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)
and
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q)
are linearly dependent. But also (

◇

‣
5.2
) holds, for
a=0
, so the result is proved in this case.

From now on suppose that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(Q)\neq 0
. By considering their images under
\pi
, we see that
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)
and
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q)
are linearly dependent if and only if

\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)=(-1)^{\varepsilon(l)}\rho(l)\cdot\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q).

This in turn is equivalent to

(27)

\Big(\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma))-(-1)^{\varepsilon(l)}\rho(l)\cdot 1_{\mathbf{L}}\Big)\cdot\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q)=0.

The same proof as for
Proposition

5.1
shows that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma))=(-1)^{\varepsilon(l)}z^{l}\cdot 1_{\mathbf{L}}
in
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
, so

\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma))-(-1)^{\varepsilon(l)}\rho(l)\cdot 1_{\mathbf{L}}=\iota((-1)^{\varepsilon(l)}\rho(l)\cdot l\otimes 1_{L^{\flat}}).

Thus (
27
) is equivalent to

\iota((-1)^{\varepsilon(l)}\rho(l)\cdot l\otimes\pi(\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q)))=0,\quad\text{i.e.}\quad\iota(l\otimes\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(Q))=0.

This is the case if and only if
l\otimes\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(Q)
is in the image of the differential
\mathrm{d}_{1}
induced by
\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)}
, i.e. if and only if there exists
a\in\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
such that

\mathrm{d}_{1}(a)=l\otimes\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(Q)\quad\text{in}\quad\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)\otimes\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}).

To test an equality in
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)\otimes\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
, it suffices to test it after pairing the
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
part with each
y\in\operatorname{H}^{1}(L)
. So, by the previous paragraph,
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma)\mathbin{\star}Q)
and
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(Q)
are linearly dependent if and only if there exists
a\in\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
such that for all
y\in\operatorname{H}^{1}(L)
we have

\langle y,\mathrm{d}_{1}(a)\rangle=\langle y,l\rangle\otimes\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(Q)\quad\text{in}\quad\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)\otimes\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}).

To complete the proof it thus suffices to show that for all
a
and
y
we have

\langle y,\mathrm{d}_{1}(a)\rangle=\mu^{2}(a,\operatorname{PSS}(y))+\mu^{2}(\operatorname{PSS}(y),a)

We shall actually prove this up to an overall sign, which may depend on the degree of
a
, but that’s sufficient for our purposes.

Let our pearl complexes be defined using a Morse function
f
on
L
, and a path
J_{t}
of almost complex structures. So generators of the complexes are critical points of
f
, and the differentials count pearly trajectories weighted by monodromy in
\mathbf{k}
(from
\rho
),
S
, or
R
depending on the coefficients. Explicitly, a pearly trajectory is a Morse flowline interrupted by bivalent
J_{t}
-holomorphic discs, where each disc is parametrised so that after deleting the marked points the domain is
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
with holomorphic coordinate
s+it
, input at
s\to\infty
, and output at
s\to-\infty
.

The term
\mu^{2}(a,\operatorname{PSS}(y))
counts Y-shaped pearly trees whose inputs are (linear combinations of) critical points of
f
representing
a
and
y
respectively, weighted by monodromy from
\rho
. This is shown on the left in
Fig.

9
, where each bivalent disc in the tree is
J_{t}
-holomorphic, as above, whilst the trivalent disc at the centre carries a domain-dependent almost complex structure and Hamiltonian perturbation (indicated by the shading) to ensure transversality of the moduli space.

a
y
\mathrm{output}
a
\mathrm{output}
y

Figure 9.
Trees computing
\mu^{2}(a,\operatorname{PSS}(y))
(left) and
\mu^{1}(a)
weighted by the intersection of the
t=1
boundary with
y
(right). The shaded disc and dashed flowline carry different auxiliary data from the others.

The diagram shows one bivalent disc on each leg, but this is purely illustrative; there may be arbitrarily many, including zero.

Now consider deforming the almost complex structure and Hamiltonian perturbation on the trivalent disc so that it becomes a (possibly constant)
J_{t}
-holomorphic disc from the
a
-input to the output, with the
y
-input occurring as a marked point on the
t=1
boundary. We can ensure transversality for these configurations by deforming the Morse function on the
y
-leg of the tree to a nearby Morse function
f^{\prime}
. A standard cobordism argument shows that counts of old and new configurations agree at cohomology level.

The upshot is that
\mu^{2}(a,\operatorname{PSS}(y))\in\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
can also be computed by counting pearly trajectories from
a
but with an additional input leg to the
t=1
boundary corresponding to an
f^{\prime}
pearly trajectory from
y
. This additional input leg cannot contain any discs, otherwise deleting the leg would produce a pearly trajectory from
a
of negative virtual dimension. So, up to an overall sign depending on
\lvert a\rvert
and our conventions,
\mu^{2}(a,\operatorname{PSS}(y))
computes
\mu^{1}(a)
weighted by the pairing of the
t=1
boundary path with
y
, as shown on the right in
Fig.

9
. (Again the diagram is purely illustrative and there may be any number of discs before or after the one receiving the input from
y
. The dashed flowline indicates the different Morse function,
f^{\prime}
.) Similarly for
\mu^{2}(\operatorname{PSS}(y),a)
but now for the
t=0
boundary path, oriented the opposite way. Note that in both cases the
f^{\prime}
-leg does not contribute to the
\rho
weighting of the configuration as it can be contracted away. Therefore, up to sign,
\mu^{2}(a,\operatorname{PSS}(y))+\mu^{2}(\operatorname{PSS}(y),a)
computes
\mu^{1}(a)
weighted by the pairing of the whole boundary with
y
. This is exactly
\pm\langle y,\mathrm{d}_{1}(a)\rangle
, as claimed.
∎

6.
Real toric Lagrangians

In this section we apply our results to prove
E
. Throughout we will take
X
to be a compact monotone toric manifold of minimal Chern number
N_{X}\geq 2
, and assume that our coefficient field
\mathbf{k}
has characteristic
2
. We view
X
as a symplectic reduction of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
, via the Delzant construction; see
[
11
, Chapter 29]
for more details and
Section

C.1
for our conventions. Our Lagrangian
L^{\flat}
will be the real locus of
X
, defined to be the symplectic reduction of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
under this construction, and we equip it with the trivial local system
\mathcal{L}
. This real locus is a valid object of
\mathcal{F}(X)^{\mathrm{un}}_{0}
, and of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{0}
if it’s orientable: it’s monotone with minimal Maslov number equal to
N_{X}
; condition
(ii)
from
Section

2.2
holds automatically since
X
is simply connected; the pin structure in condition
(iii)
is not needed since we’re working in characteristic
2
, whilst the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading exists when needed (i.e. when
L
is orientable) by
2.4
; and
W_{L}(\mathcal{L})=0
by
6.14
.
E
asserts that
L^{\flat}
split-generates
\mathcal{F}(X)_{0}
if it’s orientable and split-generates
\mathcal{F}(X)_{0}^{\mathrm{un}}
in general. These will follow from the generation criterion if we can prove injectivity of

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})).

By
B
and
C
it suffices to show that

\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}_{\widehat{R}}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})

is injective for some ideal
I\subset S=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
contained in
\mathfrak{m}
. Recall that
\widehat{R}
is the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completion of
R=S/I
, and because the local system
\mathcal{L}
is trivial we can describe
\mathfrak{m}
explicitly as

(z^{\gamma}-1:\gamma\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})),

i.e. the ideal generated by expressions of the form
z^{\gamma}-1
as
\gamma
ranges over
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
. We will prove that
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
is injective for
I=(z^{2\gamma}-1:\gamma\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}))
. In this case we have

R=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}/2]=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}/2)].

Remark 6.1
.

If
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}/2)
has dimension
d
over
\mathbb{Z}/2
then we have

R\cong\mathbf{k}[z_{1},\dots,z_{d}]/(z_{1}^{2}-1,\dots,z_{d}^{2}-1)=\mathbf{k}[z_{1},\dots,z_{d}]/((z_{1}-1)^{2},\dots,(z_{d}-1)^{2}).

(Recall that
\mathbf{k}
has characteristic
2
!) Under this isomorphism,
\mathfrak{m}R
is
(z_{1}-1,\dots,z_{d}-1)
, so by the pigeonhole principle we have
\mathfrak{m}^{d+1}R=0
. Hence
\widehat{R}=R
and, as in
Section

5
, no completion is actually needed. However, also as in
Section

5
, we will leave the hat on
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
in order to distinguish it from
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
.

6.1.
Outline of the argument

Recall from
Section

1.5
that the main step in proving injectivity of
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}_{R}^{*}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
is to establish a commutative diagram of
\mathbf{k}
-algebras

(28)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{2*}_{R}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}}

Here
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
is an extension of quantum cohomology to
R
coefficients,
\pi
is reduction modulo
\mathfrak{m}
(which sends every monomial in
R
to
1
),
\mathcal{D}_{R}
is an isomorphism we will define, and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}
is ‘the
\mathbf{k}
-linear extension of Frobenius’ in the following sense. Take
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Z}/2
and consider the Frobenius map
x\mapsto x^{2}
on
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
; now pass to general
\mathbf{k}
by applying
\mathbf{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}{-}
and extending the map
\mathbf{k}
-linearly. Explicitly, a general element of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
is of the form
\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}z^{A_{j}}x_{i}
for
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbf{k}
,
A_{i}\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, and
x_{i}\in\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, and

\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}\Big(\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}z^{A_{j}}x_{j}\Big)=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}z^{2A_{j}}x_{j}^{2}=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}x_{j}^{2}.

Remark 6.2
.

In
[
60
, Theorem 1.13]
Tonkonog constructed a similar diagram over
\mathbf{k}
, instead of
R
, namely

(29)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{2*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Here
\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}
is the
\mathbf{k}
-linear extension of the Frobenius map on
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, and
\mathcal{D}
is an isomorphism constructed by Haug
[
38
]
and Hyvrier
[
40
]
. Our diagram extends Tonkonog’s, in the sense that reducing (
28
) modulo
\mathfrak{m}
(which collapses the left-hand vertical arrow to an identity map) gives (
29
).

The statement of
[
60
, Theorem 1.13]
is not strictly correct: it has the Frobenius map itself in place of
\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}
, but the proof given is really about
\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}
instead. The result is used by Evans–Lekili in
[
25
, Example 7.2.4]
, in their proof that
L^{\flat}
split-generates
\mathcal{F}(X)_{0}
when
L
is orientable, in order to show that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(e_{\alpha})\neq 0
for any non-zero idempotent
e_{\alpha}
in
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
. But it is straightforward to modify their argument to use
\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}
instead, as follows. Take a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-basis
x_{i}
for the kernel of Frobenius
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\operatorname{QH}^{2*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, and let
y_{j}
be a basis for a complementary subspace of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Note that the
y_{j}^{2}
are
\mathbb{Z}/2
-linearly independent. Now view the
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
as elements of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, i.e. tensor by
\mathbf{k}
. Note that they form a
\mathbf{k}
-basis, and that the
y_{j}^{2}
are now
\mathbf{k}
-linearly independent. Writing our non-zero idempotent
e_{\alpha}
as
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}x_{i}+\sum_{j}\mu_{j}y_{j}
for
\lambda_{i},\mu_{j}\in\mathbf{k}
, we have

\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}(e_{\alpha})=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}x_{i}^{2}+\sum_{j}\mu_{j}y_{j}^{2}=\sum_{j}\mu_{j}y_{j}^{2}.

We want to show
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(e_{\alpha})\neq 0
, and by (
29
) it suffices to show that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}(e_{\alpha})\neq 0
. So by
\mathbf{k}
-linear independence of the
y_{j}^{2}
it suffices to show that the
\mu_{j}
are not all zero, and this is true since

0\neq e_{\alpha}=e_{\alpha}^{2}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}^{2}x_{i}^{2}+\sum_{j}\mu_{j}^{2}y_{j}^{2}=\sum_{j}\mu_{j}^{2}y_{j}^{2}.

Taking the diagram (
28
) as given, note that since
\pi
is surjective we have

\ker\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}=\pi\big(\ker\big(\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}\circ\pi\big)\big)=\pi(\ker(\mathcal{D}_{R}\circ\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}))=\pi(\ker\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}).

The proof of
E
is thus completed by the following result.

Proposition 6.3
.

We have
\ker\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}\subset\ker\pi
, so
\ker\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}=0
.

Remark 6.4
.

If we can construct the diagram and prove this result in the case
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Z}/2
then we can obtain the general case by simply tensoring with the desired
\mathbf{k}
.
We will therefore assume for the rest of this section that
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Z}/2
. In particular, all (co)homology groups implicitly have
\mathbb{Z}/2
coefficients, unless stated otherwise.
With this in place,
\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}
simplifies to the genuine Frobenius map.

Proof of
Proposition

6.3
.

Let
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{N}
be the normals to the facets of the moment polytope
\Delta
of
X
. We can naturally view them as elements of
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T;\mathbb{Z})
, where
T
is the
n
-torus acting on
X
as part of its toric structure. For more details on this and the general toric background, see
Section

C.1
. We assume that the facets are ordered so that the last
n
of them meet at a vertex of
\Delta
. Smoothness of
X
then tells us, via the Delzant condition, that
\nu_{N-n+1},\dots,\nu_{N}
is a basis for
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T;\mathbb{Z})
.

From
Corollary

C.10
we have identifications

\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)\cong\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}]\Big/\Big(\sum_{j}\nu_{j}Z_{j}\Big)+(Z^{2A}-1:A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}))

and

\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\cong\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}]\Big/\Big(\sum_{j}\nu_{j}Z_{j}\Big)+(Z^{A}-1:A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})),

with
\pi
sending
Z_{j}
to
Z_{j}
. Here
Z^{A}
(similarly
Z^{2A}
) denotes the monomial
\prod_{j}Z_{j}^{\langle A,H_{j}\rangle}
, and an ideal generated by vectors like
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}Z_{j}
means the ideal generated by their components with respect to any basis of
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T;\mathbb{Z})
. Taking the latter basis to be
\nu_{N-n+1},\dots,\nu_{N}
, the components of
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}Z_{j}
have the form
Z_{N-n+1}-l_{1},\dots,Z_{N}-l_{n}
for some linear functions
l_{1},\dots,l_{n}
of
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N-n}
. We then have

\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)\cong\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N-n}^{\pm 1}][l_{1}^{-1},\dots,l_{n}^{-1}]\Big/(\widetilde{Z}^{2A}-1:A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}))

and

\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\cong\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N-n}^{\pm 1}][l_{1}^{-1},\dots,l_{n}^{-1}]\Big/(\widetilde{Z}^{A}-1:A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})),

where
\widetilde{Z}^{A}
(similarly
\widetilde{Z}^{2A}
) denotes
\prod_{j=1}^{N-n}Z_{j}^{\langle A,H_{j}\rangle}\prod_{j=1}^{n}l_{j}^{\langle A,H_{N-n+j}\rangle}
.

Given this setup, to prove that
\ker\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}
is contained in
\ker\pi
it suffices to prove that the lift of
\ker\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}
from
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
to
\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N-n}^{\pm 1}][l_{1}^{-1},\dots,l_{n}^{-1}]
is contained in the corresponding lift of
\ker\pi
. We denote these lifts by
I
and
J
respectively; we want to show that
I\subset J
. Take then a general element
f
of
\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N-n}^{\pm 1}][l_{1}^{-1},\dots,l_{n}^{-1}]
and suppose it lies in
I
, which means that

(30)

f^{2}\in(\widetilde{Z}^{2A}-1:A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}))\subset\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N-n}^{\pm 1}][l_{1}^{-1},\dots,l_{n}^{-1}].

We want to show that
f
also lies in
J
, i.e. that

(31)

f\in(\widetilde{Z}^{A}-1:A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}))\subset\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N-n}^{\pm 1}][l_{1}^{-1},\dots,l_{n}^{-1}].

From (
30
) we get that there exist
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N-n}^{\pm 1}][l_{1}^{-1},\dots,l_{n}^{-1}]
and
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
such that

f^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}g_{j}\cdot(\widetilde{Z}^{2A_{j}}-1).

Multiplying through by a large power of
l_{1}\dots l_{n}
, we can ensure that no negative powers of the
l_{j}
appear. More precisely, for a sufficiently large positive integer
k
we have that
F\coloneqq f\cdot(l_{1}\dots l_{n})^{2k}
, all
G_{j}\coloneqq g_{j}\cdot(l_{1}\dots l_{n})^{k}
, and all
(\widetilde{Z}^{2A_{j}}-1)(l_{1}\dots l_{n})^{k}
are Laurent polynomials in
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N-n}
. We also have that

(32)

F^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}G_{j}\cdot(\widetilde{Z}^{2A_{j}}-1)(l_{1}\dots l_{n})^{2k}.

Note that (since we’re in characteristic
2
) only
even
powers of
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N-n}
appear in each of the Laurent polynomials
F^{2}
and
