(\widetilde{Z}^{2A_{j}}-1)(l_{1}\dots l_{n})^{2k}
. So any terms in (
32
) that arise from monomials in the
G_{j}
containing odd powers must cancel each other out. In other words, if we write
G_{j}^{\mathrm{ev}}
for the part of
G_{j}
containing only even powers then we have

F^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}G^{\mathrm{ev}}_{j}\cdot(\widetilde{Z}^{2A_{j}}-1)(l_{1}\dots l_{n})^{2k}.

All of the expressions
F^{2}
,
G^{\mathrm{ev}}_{j}
, and
(\widetilde{Z}^{2A_{j}}-1)(l_{1}\dots l_{n})^{2k}
are now in the image of the Frobenius morphism on
\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N-n}^{\pm 1}]
, which is injective since this algebra is an integral domain. So we can apply the inverse of Frobenius (i.e. ‘square root both sides’) to obtain

F=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sqrt{G^{\mathrm{ev}}_{j}}\cdot(\widetilde{Z}^{A_{j}}-1)(l_{1}\dots l_{n})^{k},

where
\sqrt{G^{\mathrm{ev}}_{j}}
denotes the Laurent polynomial obtained from
G^{\mathrm{ev}}_{j}
by halving all of the exponents. Dividing through by
(l_{1}\dots l_{n})^{2k}
now gives

f=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\sqrt{G^{\mathrm{ev}}_{j}}}{(l_{1}\dots l_{n})^{k}}\cdot(\widetilde{Z}^{A_{j}}-1),

which proves (
31
) and hence completes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

The reverse inclusion,
\ker\pi\subset\ker\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}
, is an easy consequence of the fact that every monomial in
R
squares to
1
. So we actually have
\ker\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}=\ker\pi
.

The remainder of this section is devoted to constructing the commutative diagram (
28
). Most of the work goes into defining
\mathcal{D}_{R}
and showing that it is an isomorphism, and this is where we begin—first studying the classical case due to Duistermaat
[
22
]
, next recapping the Seidel and relative Seidel maps, and then combining these to construct
\mathcal{D}_{R}
, building on work of Haug
[
38
]
, Hyvrier
[
40
]
, and Tonkonog
[
60
]
. Finally, in
Section

6.5
we explain the rest of the diagram.

6.2.
The Duistermaat isomorphism

As promised, we start by reviewing the remarkable relationship between the classical cohomology rings of
X
and
L
in characteristic
2
, which goes back to Duistermaat
[
22
]
. As in the proof of
Proposition

6.3
, let
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{N}
be the normals to the facets of the moment polytope
\Delta
of
X
. Let
D_{1},\dots,D_{N}
be the toric divisors corresponding to the respective facets, and let
H_{1},\dots,H_{N}
be their Poincaré dual cohomology classes in
\operatorname{H}^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
. As before, see
Section

C.1
for more details. The following description of
\operatorname{H}^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is well-known but we outline a proof that is tailored towards our later needs.

Proposition 6.6

(
[
21
, Theorem 10.8]
)
.

As a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
\operatorname{H}^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is generated by the
H_{j}
modulo the linear relations
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}H_{j}=0
and the Stanley–Reisner relations (and no other relations are needed). The latter say that for a subset
J\subset\{1,\dots,N\}
we have

\prod_{j\in J}H_{j}=0\quad\text{if}\quad\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}=\emptyset.

Remark 6.7
.

The vectors
\nu_{j}
live in the rank-
n
lattice
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T;\mathbb{Z})
, and the expression
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}H_{j}=0
is an equality in that lattice. Concretely, it therefore imposes
n
linear relations between the
H_{j}
. These relations are independent since we may assume, as in the proof of
Proposition

6.3
, that
\nu_{N-n+1},\dots,\nu_{N}
form a basis for
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T;\mathbb{Z})
.

Sketch proof of
Proposition

6.6
.

All (co)homology groups in this proof are with
\mathbb{Z}
coefficients.

As a toric manifold,
X
comes equipped with an action of an
n
-torus
T
and a
T
-invariant Kähler metric
g
. Let
\mathfrak{t}
denote the Lie algebra of
T
, and
\mu:X\to\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
the moment map for the
T
-action. For generic
\xi
in
\mathfrak{t}
the map
f_{\xi}\coloneqq\langle\mu,\xi\rangle:X\to\mathbb{R}
is a perfect Morse function, so
\operatorname{H}^{*}(X)
is a free abelian group. Moreover, the pair
(f_{\xi},g)
is Morse–Smale and the descending manifolds are toric subvarieties, so
\operatorname{H}^{*}(X)
is spanned by the Poincaré duals of the toric subvarieties. Since each toric subvariety can be written as a transverse intersection of toric divisors
D_{j}
, under Poincaré duality we deduce that
\operatorname{H}^{*}(X)
is generated as an algebra by the
H_{j}
. The Stanley–Reisner relations follow immediately from Poincaré duality and the intersection product.

Next consider the linear relations between the
H_{j}
. We wish to show that, as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module,
\operatorname{H}^{2}(X)
is generated by the
H_{j}
modulo
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}H_{j}=0
. Equivalently, if
F
is the free
\mathbb{Z}
-module with basis
H_{1},\dots,H_{N}
and
\theta:\operatorname{H}^{1}(T)\to F
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear map given by

a\in\operatorname{H}^{1}(T)\mapsto\sum_{j}\langle\nu_{j},a\rangle H_{j}\in F

then we wish to show that the image of
\theta
is the set
R
of relators between the
H_{j}
, i.e. the kernel of the projection
F\to\operatorname{H}^{2}(X)
.

To do this, consider the dual map
\theta^{\vee}:F^{\vee}\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(T)
, which has matrix
\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{1}\cdots\nu_{N}\end{pmatrix}
with respect to the dual basis
H^{\vee}_{1},\dots,H^{\vee}_{N}
of
F^{\vee}
. Since (WLOG)
\nu_{N-n+1},\dots,\nu_{N}
form a basis for
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T)
, the map
\theta^{\vee}
is surjective and thus induces a splitting
F^{\vee}=\ker(\theta^{\vee})\oplus P
, where
P\subset F^{\vee}
is a submodule mapped isomorphically by
\theta^{\vee}
to
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T)
. Note that
P
must be a free module of rank
n
, because
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T)
is, and hence the complement
\ker(\theta^{\vee})
must be a free module of rank
N-n
. Dualising, we obtain a splitting
F=(\ker(\theta^{\vee}))^{\circ}\oplus P^{\circ}
, where
∘
denotes annihilator, under which
\theta
induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{H}^{1}(T)\to(\ker\theta^{\vee})^{\circ}
. Moreover,
(\ker(\theta^{\vee}))^{\circ}
and
P^{\circ}
are free modules of ranks
n
and
N-n
respectively. Our task—proving that
R
is the image of
\theta
—is thus reduced to showing that
R=(\ker\theta^{\vee})^{\circ}
.

If we can show that
R\subset(\ker(\theta^{\vee}))^{\circ}
then we have

\operatorname{H}^{2}(X)=F/R\cong\big((\ker(\theta^{\vee}))^{\circ}\big/R\big)\oplus P^{\circ},

where
P^{\circ}
is a free module of rank
N-n
. We saw already that
\operatorname{H}^{2}(X)
is a free module, and a more careful analysis (e.g. as in
[
57
, Lemma 2.4]
) shows that its rank is
N-n
, so we conclude that
(\ker(\theta^{\vee}))^{\circ}\big/R=0
. Hence
R=(\ker(\theta^{\vee}))^{\circ}
, which is what we want. It therefore suffices to show that
R\subset(\ker(\theta^{\vee}))^{\circ}
.

Suppose then that a linear relation
\sum_{j}r_{j}H_{j}=0
holds, and that
\sum_{j}s_{j}H^{\vee}_{j}
lies in the kernel of
\theta^{\vee}
, i.e.
\sum_{j}s_{j}\nu_{j}=0
, for integers
r_{j}
and
s_{j}
. We need to show that
\sum_{j}r_{j}s_{j}=0
. Let
K
denote the monotone toric fibre in
X
. For each
j
there is a
basic disc class

\beta_{j}\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X,K)
which meets
D_{j}
once (transversely) and is disjoint from the other
D_{i}
. Recall from the proof of
Proposition

6.3
that the
\nu_{j}
naturally live in
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T)
, which is identified with
\operatorname{H}_{1}(K)
since
K
is a free
T
-orbit. Under this identification, the boundary of
\beta_{j}
is exactly
\nu_{j}\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(K)
, so since the
s_{j}
satisfy
\sum_{j}s_{j}\nu_{j}=0
we have that
\sum_{j}s_{j}\beta_{j}\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X,K)
lifts to a class
A
in
\operatorname{H}_{2}(X)
. The intersection number
A\cdot D_{j}=\langle A,H_{j}\rangle
is exactly
s_{j}
, since
\beta_{i}\cdot D_{j}=\delta_{ij}
, so we conclude that

\sum_{j}r_{j}s_{j}=\sum_{j}r_{j}\langle A,H_{j}\rangle=\Big\langle A,\sum_{j}r_{j}H_{j}\Big\rangle=\langle A,0\rangle=0,

as we wanted. Therefore
\operatorname{H}^{2}(X)
is indeed generated by the
H_{j}
modulo
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}H_{j}=0
.

We have thus shown that
\operatorname{H}^{*}(X)
is generated by the
H_{j}
, modulo the linear relations
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}H_{j}=0
and the Stanley–Reisner relations, plus possibly some extra relations. To show that no other relations are required takes some extra work, but that is not relevant to our later purposes so we refer the interested reader to
[
19
, Theorem 12.4.4]
.
∎

Now consider the real Lagrangian
L\subset X
. Each toric subvariety
Z\subset X
intersects
L
cleanly, and
\dim Z\cap L=\frac{1}{2}\dim Z
. Let
d_{1},\dots,d_{N}
be the intersections of the toric divisors with
L
, and let
h_{1},\dots,h_{N}
be their Poincaré dual cohomology classes in
\operatorname{H}^{1}(L)
. We remind the reader that we have now returned to our implicit assumption for this section, that all coefficients are in
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Z}/2
unless stated otherwise. Our next result is again standard, but we explain the proof as it is less well-known.

Proposition 6.8
.

As a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-algebra
\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)
is generated by the
h_{j}
modulo the linear relations
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}h_{j}=0
, again interpreted as a vector equation, and the Stanley–Reisner relations (and no other relations are needed).

Proof.

Duistermaat
[
22
, Theorem 3.1]
showed that if one takes the perfect Morse function
f_{\xi}
on
X
from the proof of
Proposition

6.6
then the restriction
\left.f_{\xi}\right|_{L}
is a perfect Morse function on
L
, as long as mod-
2
coefficients are used. The critical points of the restricted function are the same as those of the original function, but of half their previous index. We thus have
\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{H}^{2j}(X)=\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{H}^{j}(L)
for each
j
. Moreover, using the restriction of the metric
g
from
Proposition

6.6
the descending manifolds on
L
are the intersections of those on
X
with
L
. In particular,
\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)
is spanned by the intersections of toric subvarieties with
L
. These are all transverse intersections of the
d_{j}
in
L
, so
\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)
is generated as an algebra by the
h_{j}
. The Stanley–Reisner relations hold as before.

Now consider the linear relations between the
h_{j}
. Following an analogous argument to
Proposition

6.6
, but with
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Z}/2
coefficients instead of
\mathbb{Z}
(which actually makes things much simpler since we’re now working over a field), it suffices to show that if
\sum_{j}r_{j}h_{j}=0
and
\sum_{j}s_{j}\nu_{j}=0
for some
r_{j}
and
s_{j}
in
\mathbf{k}
then
\sum_{j}r_{j}s_{j}=0
. In the proof of
Proposition

6.6
we had to use the fact that
\operatorname{H}^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{N-n}
, and we now need the corresponding fact for
\operatorname{H}^{1}(L)
, namely
\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{H}^{1}(L)=N-n
. This follows by tensoring
\operatorname{H}^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{N-n}
with
\mathbf{k}
and applying the equality
\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{H}^{2j}(X)=\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{H}^{j}(L)
established in the previous paragraph.

So suppose then that we have
r_{j}
and
s_{j}
in
\mathbf{k}
such that
\sum_{j}r_{j}h_{j}=0
and
\sum_{j}s_{j}\nu_{j}=0
. We need to show that
\sum_{j}r_{j}s_{j}=0
. To do this, consider the transverse intersection
K\cap L
. It comprises
2^{n}
points and is a torsor for the
2
-torsion subgroup of the torus
T
, which is naturally identified with
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T)
. Given a point
p\in K\cap L
there is a unique class
\eta_{j}^{p}
in
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L,K\cap L)
which starts at
p
, crosses once transversely over
d_{j}
, and then returns to
K\cap L
without meeting any other
d_{i}
. This can be viewed as the real part of the basic disc class
\beta_{j}
. The final endpoint of
\eta_{j}^{p}
is
\nu_{j}+p
, meaning the action of
\nu_{j}\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(T)
on
p
. Fix an arbitrary starting point
p\in K\cap L
, and suppose that
s_{j_{1}},\dots,s_{j_{m}}=1
whilst all other
s_{j}
are
0
. Let
p_{1}=p
and inductively define
p_{j+1}=\nu_{j}+p_{j}
. Because
\sum_{j}s_{j}\nu_{j}=0
we have that
p_{m+1}=p_{1}
, so the class
\eta_{j_{1}}^{p_{1}}+\dots+\eta_{j_{m}}^{p_{m}}
has empty boundary in
\operatorname{H}_{0}(K\cap L)
and hence lifts to
a\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
. We then have

\sum_{j}r_{j}s_{j}=\sum_{j}r_{j}\langle a,h_{j}\rangle=\Big\langle a,\sum_{j}r_{j}h_{j}\Big\rangle=\langle a,0\rangle=0,

which is what we wanted.

So
\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)
is generated by the
h_{j}
modulo the claimed linear and Stanley–Reisner relations. To show there are no other relations, and hence complete the proof, it suffices to show that

\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)=\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\mathbf{k}[h_{1},\dots,h_{N}]/(\text{linear and Stanley--Reisner relations}).

This follows by reducing
Proposition

6.6
mod
2
and using again the result of the first paragraph that
\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{H}^{2j}(X)=\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{H}^{j}(L)
.
∎

The following is an immediate consequence of
Propositions

6.6
and
6.8
.

Corollary 6.9
.

The map sending a toric subvariety
Z\subset X
to
Z\cap L
induces a well-defined algebra isomorphism

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{class}}:\operatorname{H}_{2*}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\operatorname{H}_{*}(L;\mathbb{Z}/2),

with respect to the intersection products.
∎

Remark 6.10
.

We call the map
\mathcal{D}
for ‘Duistermaat’, with subscript
class
for ‘classical’, to distinguish it from its quantum counterpart
\mathcal{D}_{R}
that we will define shortly.

To construct
\mathcal{D}_{R}
we will mimic the above strategy: give a presentation of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
(we have seen this already), show that
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
has the same dimension, then identify generators of
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
and show that they satisfy all of the relations satisfied by the corresponding generators of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
.

6.3.
The Seidel maps

In order to implement this plan to construct
\mathcal{D}_{R}
, we will use the Seidel representation
[
49
]
and the relative Seidel map introduced by Hu–Lalonde
[
39
]
. We now recap these, and discuss the extension of the latter to
R
coefficients.

Recall that the Seidel representation is a map
S:\pi_{1}\operatorname{Ham}(X)\to\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)^{\times}
, defined on a loop
\gamma
by counting pseudoholomorphic sections of the
X
-bundle over
S^{2}
clutched by
\gamma
. This map is group homomorphism when
\pi_{1}\operatorname{Ham}(X)
is equipped with the loop concatenation product; this is proved by gluing fibrations. By the usual Eckmann–Hilton argument one could equivalently define the product on
\pi_{1}\operatorname{Ham}(X)
by pointwise composition.

Recall also that there is a relative Seidel map
S_{L^{\flat}}:\pi_{0}\mathcal{P}_{L}\operatorname{Ham}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\times}
, where

\mathcal{P}_{L}\operatorname{Ham}(X)=\{\text{paths }\alpha:[0,1]\to\operatorname{Ham}(X):\alpha(0)=\operatorname{id}_{X}\text{ and }\alpha(1)(L)=L\},

defined as follows. Let
H
be the rounded half-strip

H=\{s+it:s\leq 0\text{ and }t\in[-1,1]\}\cup\{s+it:s^{2}+t^{2}\leq 1\}\subset\mathbb{C}.

Given
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{L}\operatorname{Ham}(X)
, let
\Lambda_{\alpha}
be the following moving Lagrangian boundary condition for
H
, i.e. the following family of Lagrangian submanifolds in
X
parametrised by
\partial H
:

\Lambda_{\alpha}(s\pm i)=L\text{ for $s\leq 0$}\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda_{\alpha}(e^{\pi i(\tau-\frac{1}{2})})=\alpha(\tau)(L)\text{ for $\tau\in[0,1]$}.

The element
S_{L^{\flat}}(\alpha)\in\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
is defined by counting finite-energy solutions
u:H\to X
of the Floer equation, with
u(z)\in\Lambda_{\alpha}(z)
for all
z\in\partial H
, and with output at the obvious strip-like end. Equivalently, this is the continuation element associated to the Hamiltonian isotopy
\alpha
.

Degenerating domains as shown in
Fig.

10
yields the twisted-homomorphism property

(33)

\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(\alpha_{1}(1)_{*}S_{L^{\flat}}(\alpha_{2}),S_{L^{\flat}}(\alpha_{1}))=S_{L^{\flat}}(\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2}),

where the product on
\pi_{0}\mathcal{P}_{L}\operatorname{Ham}(X)
is defined by

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}\text{ followed by (i.e.~concatenated with) }\alpha_{1}(1)\circ\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}\circ\alpha_{2}\text{ pointwise}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{2}\text{ followed by }\alpha_{1}\circ\alpha_{2}(1).

\alpha_{1}(1)(\Lambda_{\alpha_{2}})
\Lambda_{\alpha_{1}}

Figure 10.
Degenerating the half strip defining
S_{L^{\flat}}(\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2})
into two half strips and a half pair of pants, defining
\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(\alpha_{1}(1)_{*}S_{L^{\flat}}(\alpha_{2}),S_{L^{\flat}}(\alpha_{1}))
.

Note that these equalities are in
\pi_{0}\mathcal{P}_{L}\operatorname{Ham}(X)
and would only be homotopies rel endpoints if we were working in
\mathcal{P}_{L}\operatorname{Ham}(X)
itself.

If
L^{\flat}
is equipped with a non-trivial local system
\mathcal{L}
then to define
S_{L^{\flat}}(\alpha)
we need to choose an isomorphism from
\alpha(1)_{*}\mathcal{L}
to
\mathcal{L}
. Such a choice need not exist or be unique. (Outside characteristic
2
one also needs to choose an isomorphism from
\alpha(1)_{*}\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{s}
, where
\mathfrak{s}
is the spin structure on
L
, but this is irrelevant for us.)

Suppose now that we want to extend
S_{L^{\flat}}
to a map
S_{\mathbf{L}}
which lands in
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\times}
. Since
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
can be viewed as the Floer cohomology of
L
equipped with the tautological rank-
1
local system
\mathcal{L}_{R}
over
R
, the previous paragraph tells us that to define
S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha)
we need a choice of isomorphism
\alpha(1)_{*}\mathcal{L}_{R}\to\mathcal{L}_{R}
. Recalling that
R=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}/2)]
, we see that such an isomorphism exists if and only if
\alpha(1)
acts trivially on
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
(coefficients implicitly taken to be
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Z}/2
). In this case an isomorphism is freely and uniquely determined by an
R
-module isomorphism
(\alpha(1)_{*}\mathcal{L}_{R})_{p}\to(\mathcal{L}_{R})_{p}
over a single point
p\in L
. A natural way to define such an
R
-module isomorphism is by parallel transport along a homotopy class
\eta
of path rel endpoints from
\alpha(1)^{-1}(p)
to
p
. Given a choice of
\eta
, we denote the resulting relative Seidel element by
S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha,\eta)
. The analogue of the twisted homomorphism property (
33
) is now

\mu^{2}_{R}(\alpha_{1}(1)_{*}S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{2},\eta_{2}),S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{1},\eta_{1}))=S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2},(\alpha_{2}(1)^{-1}\circ\eta_{1})\cdot\eta_{2}),

assuming
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are defined using the same point
p
.

Hu–Lalonde
[
39
, Proposition 3.16]
show that for a loop
\gamma
in
\pi_{1}\operatorname{Ham}(X)\subset\pi_{0}\mathcal{P}_{L}\operatorname{Ham}(X)
we have
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(S(\gamma))=S_{L^{\flat}}(\gamma)
; in their notation the map
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}
is called
\mathscr{A}
. In this situation
\gamma(1)=\operatorname{id}_{X}
fixes the whole of
L
pointwise, so we can take
\eta
to be the constant path
\eta_{p}
at
p
, for any point
p
. The same proof as for
S_{L^{\flat}}
then goes through to show that

(34)

\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(S(\gamma))=S_{\mathbf{L}}(\gamma,\eta_{p}).

6.4.
The isomorphism
\mathcal{D}_{R}

We are now ready to define
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
and assemble the above ingredients to construct the
R
-algebra isomorphism

\mathcal{D}_{R}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})

appearing in (
28
). We do this by extending ideas of Haug
[
38
]
and Hyvrier
[
40
]
, who constructed an analogous
\mathbf{k}
-algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{D}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
.

To define
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
, first recall that
R=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}/2)]
and that we have an isomorphism

\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}/2)

from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{class}}
. We can therefore view
R
as
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)]
. We then define
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
to be the
R
-algebra with underlying
R
-module
R\otimes\operatorname{H}^{*}(X)
, and with product defined by counting pseudoholomorphic spheres in the usual way, but with the contribution of spheres in class
A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X)
weighted by the monomial
Z^{A}\in\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)]=R
.

Remark 6.11
.

We will usually use
Z
for the formal variable in group algebras built from the homology of
X
, and
z
in group algebras built from the homology of
L
. So under our twin perspectives of
R
as both
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)]
and
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}/2)]
, we could write
Z^{A}=z^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{class}}(A)}
.

Recall that from
Corollary

C.10
we have an identification of
\mathbf{k}
-algebras

(35)

\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)\cong\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}]\Big/\Big(\sum_{j}\nu_{j}Z_{j}\Big)+\Big(\prod_{j}Z_{j}^{\langle 2A,H_{j}\rangle}-1:A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\Big),

under which
H_{j}
on the left corresponds to
Z_{j}
on the right. The proof of
Corollary

C.10
actually shows that this is an identification of
R
-algebras, if
Z^{A\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2}\in R
is sent to
\prod_{j}Z_{j}^{\langle A,H_{j}\rangle}
on the right-hand side. (This is consistent with our earlier definition of
Z^{A}
in the proof of
Proposition

6.3
, and is well-defined—i.e. only depends on
A\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2
—because we already quotiented out by even powers on the right.) The desired isomorphism
\mathcal{D}_{R}
is then provided by the following result.

Proposition 6.12
.

(i)

We have
\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})=\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
.

(ii)

The algebra
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
is commutative.

(iii)

As an
R
-algebra,
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
is generated by
\operatorname{PSS}(h_{1}),\dots,\operatorname{PSS}(h_{N})
.

(iv)

Each
\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})
is invertible.

(v)

For each
A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
we have
\prod_{j}\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})^{\langle H_{j},A\rangle}=Z^{A\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2}\in R
.

(vi)

These classes satisfy

\sum_{j}\nu_{j}\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})=0\quad\text{and, for all $A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})$,}\quad\prod_{j}\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})^{\langle H_{j},2A\rangle}=1.

(The former represents
n
linear relations, as in
6.7
.)

Thus, by
(ii)
,
(iii)
,
(iv)
, and
(vi)
, there is a surjective
R
-algebra homomorphism

R[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}]\Big/\Big(\sum_{j}\nu_{j}Z_{j}\Big)+\Big(\prod_{j}Z_{j}^{\langle 2A,H_{j}\rangle}-1:A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\Big)\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L}),

given by
Z_{j}\mapsto\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})
. By
(v)
we can replace the
R
on the left with
\mathbf{k}
, if we make the left-hand side into an
R
-algebra by letting
Z^{A\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2}\in R
act as
\prod_{j}Z_{j}^{\langle A,H_{j}\rangle}
. Then (
35
) tells us that we can replace the domain with
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
, and by
(i)
the resulting map is an isomorphism. This map is our
\mathcal{D}_{R}
. By construction, it is the unique
R
-algebra homomorphism
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
sending each
H_{j}
to
\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})
.

Proof.

(i)
For this we follow Haug, who proved the analogous result for
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})

[
38
, Theorem A(i)]
. We compute
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
using a pearl model with a perfect Morse function, as in
Proposition

6.8
, and with the standard toric complex structure
J_{\mathrm{std}}
. Transversality can be achieved by appropriate perturbation of the Morse function and metric; see
[
38
, Section 7.2]
. The differential counts pearly trajectories, and there is a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-action on these by reflecting discs using the antisymplectic involution
\tau:X\to X
given by complex conjugation on homogeneous coordinates. Note that this reflection map
\tau
fixes
L
pointwise and is antiholomorphic with respect to
J_{\mathrm{std}}
. A disc and its reflection under
\tau
have the same boundary class in
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
—recall the implicit
\mathbb{Z}/2
coefficients—so they are counted with the same
R
weight. Since we’re working in characteristic
2
, we conclude that all contributions to the pearly differential cancel with their reflections, except those coming from fixed points of the action. These fixed points are precisely trajectories containing no discs (see
[
56
, Proposition 4.29]
), i.e. Morse trajectories, but these collectively cancel out because the Morse function is perfect. So the differential vanishes, and we get

\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})=\dim_{\mathbf{k}}R\cdot\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)=\dim_{\mathbf{k}}R\cdot\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{H}^{*}(X)=\dim_{\mathbf{k}}\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X).

(ii)
Here we follow
[
56
, Proposition 4.30]
(see also
[
33
, Corollary 1.6]
for related results in characteristic
0
), and again we use a pearl model with the standard complex structure. The Floer product counts Y-shaped trajectories, and
\tau
acts on these by reversing the order of the inputs, so the product is commutative. More precisely, we use different Morse data on each leg of the Y, and this Morse data is also exchanged by the action of
\tau
. But this does not affect the argument at the level of cohomology.

(iii)
This is essentially
[
40
, Lemma 5.6]
. Equip
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
with the filtration whose
p
th filtered piece is spanned by pearl complex generators of Morse index at most
p
. By monotonicity, the Floer product respects this filtration, and the associated graded product is the ordinary cup product. The result then follows from the fact, proved in
Proposition

6.8
, that the
h_{j}
generate
\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)
as a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra.

(iv)
For each
j
there is a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action on
X
that rotates anticlockwise around the
j
th toric divisor
D_{j}
. This is the restriction of the Hamiltonian
T
-action to the
1
-parameter subgroup generated by
\nu_{j}
in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}
. Let
\gamma_{j}
be the corresponding primitive loop in
\operatorname{Ham}(X)
. Doing ‘half of
\gamma_{j}
’ gives a path in
\operatorname{Ham}(X)
, generated by
\frac{1}{2}\nu_{j}
, whose endpoints preserve
L
setwise. We denote this path by
\alpha_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{L}\operatorname{Ham}(X)
. Hyvrier
[
40
, Theorem 1.3]
showed that
S_{L^{\flat}}(\alpha_{j})
is given by the
\operatorname{PSS}
image of
h_{j}
in
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
. Moreover, if we use a Floer datum for
L
for which the Hamiltonian is small then this
S_{L^{\flat}}(\alpha_{j})
is computed by approximately constant maps
H\to X
landing approximately in
d_{j}\subset L
. This makes sense since
\alpha_{j}
fixes
d_{j}=D_{j}\cap L
pointwise, and we can only say ‘approximately’ because of the presence of a (small) Hamiltonian perturbation in the Floer datum. So, if
\eta_{j}
is a constant path at a point in
d_{j}
then we have
S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j},\eta_{j})=\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})
in
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
. Letting
\alpha^{-1}_{j}
denote the pointwise inverse to
\alpha_{j}
, the twisted homomorphism property for
S_{\mathbf{L}}
then tells us that

\mu^{2}_{R}(\alpha_{j}(1)_{*}S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j}^{-1},\eta_{j}),S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j},\eta_{j}))=S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j}\cdot\alpha^{-1}_{j},\eta_{j}\cdot\eta_{j}).

The right-hand side is
S_{\mathbf{L}}(\operatorname{id}_{L},\eta_{j})=1_{\mathbf{L}}
, so we deduce that
\alpha_{j}(1)_{*}S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j}^{-1},\eta_{j})
is inverse to
\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})
. In fact,
\alpha_{j}(1)_{*}
acts trivially on
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
since it fixes each generator
\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})
, so we can write the inverse just as
S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j}^{-1},\eta_{j})
.

(v)
This is an extension of
[
40
, Proposition 5.5]
. Before getting into the argument we modify the paths
\eta_{j}
used in the previous part so that we can compose them more easily. Fix once and for all a basepoint
p\in L
in the complement of the
d_{j}
. There’s a unique homotopy class
\widehat{\eta}_{j}
of path rel endpoints from
\alpha_{j}(1)^{-1}(p)
to
p
which crosses
d_{j}
once, transversely, and avoids all other
d_{i}
. In the notation of the proof of
Proposition

6.8
, the path
\widehat{\eta}_{j}
represents the relative homology class
\eta_{j}^{q}
, where
q=\alpha_{j}(1)^{-1}(p)
. By ‘sucking in the ends’ of
\widehat{\eta}_{j}
to give a constant path in
d_{j}
, we see that
S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j},\widehat{\eta}_{j})=S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j},\eta_{j})
. Similarly, if
\widehat{\eta}^{\,-1}_{j}
denotes the analogous homotopy class of path from
\alpha_{j}(1)(p)
to
p
then
S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j}^{-1},\widehat{\eta}_{j}^{\,-1})=S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j}^{-1},\eta_{j})
.

Turning now to the argument itself, fix
A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
and pick
j_{1},\dots,j_{m}\in\{1,\dots N\}
and signs
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{m}
such that
\prod_{j}\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})^{\langle H_{j},A\rangle}=\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j_{1}})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j_{m}})^{\varepsilon_{m}}
, i.e. such that for each
J

\sum_{i:j_{i}=J}\varepsilon_{i}=\langle H_{J},A\rangle.

Writing each
\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})^{\pm 1}
as
S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j}^{\pm 1},\widehat{\eta}_{j}^{\,\pm 1})
, and using that each
\alpha_{j}(1)_{*}
acts trivially on
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
, the twisted homomorphism property gives

(36)

\prod_{j}\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})^{\langle H_{j},A\rangle}=S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j_{1}}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots\alpha_{j_{m}}^{\varepsilon_{m}},(\alpha_{j_{m}}(1)^{-\varepsilon_{m}}\circ\dots\circ\alpha_{j_{2}}(1)^{-\varepsilon_{2}}\circ\widehat{\eta}_{j_{1}}^{\,\varepsilon_{1}})\cdots\widehat{\eta}_{j_{m}}^{\,\varepsilon_{m}}).

Letting
\widetilde{\alpha}
denote the homotopy class of path rel endpoints
\alpha_{j_{1}}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots\alpha_{j_{m}}^{\varepsilon_{m}}\in\pi_{0}\mathcal{P}_{L}\operatorname{Ham}(X)
, we claim that:

(a)

\widetilde{\alpha}
is a closed loop (i.e.
\widetilde{\alpha}(1)=\operatorname{id}_{X}
) and is nullhomotopic rel endpoints.

(b)

The path
(\alpha_{j_{m}}(1)^{-\varepsilon_{m}}\circ\dots\circ\alpha_{j_{2}}(1)^{-\varepsilon_{2}}\circ\widehat{\eta}_{j_{1}}^{\,\varepsilon_{1}})\cdots\widehat{\eta}_{j_{m}}^{\,\varepsilon_{m}}
from
\widetilde{\alpha}(1)^{-1}(p)=p
to
p
in
L
represents the homology class
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{class}}(A\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2)\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
.

Assuming these results,
(b)
tells us that

(37)

S_{\mathbf{L}}(\widetilde{\alpha},(\alpha_{j_{m}}(1)^{-\varepsilon_{m}}\circ\dots\circ\alpha_{j_{2}}(1)^{-\varepsilon_{2}}\circ\widehat{\eta}_{j_{1}}^{\,\varepsilon_{1}})\cdots\widehat{\eta}_{j_{m}}^{\,\varepsilon_{m}})=z^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{class}}(A\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2)}S_{\mathbf{L}}(\widetilde{\alpha},\eta_{p}),

where
\eta_{p}
represents the constant path at
p
, as before. By
(a)
and (
34
) we have
S_{\mathbf{L}}(\widetilde{\alpha},\eta_{p})=1_{\mathbf{L}}
, so using the relationship between
Z
and
z
from
6.11
we obtain
z^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{class}}(A\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2)}S_{\mathbf{L}}(\widetilde{\alpha},\eta_{p})=Z^{A\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2}
. Plugging this into (
37
) and thence into (
36
) yields

\prod_{j}\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})^{\langle H_{j},A\rangle}=Z^{A\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2},

as wanted. To complete the proof of
(v)
it therefore remains to prove
(a)
and
(b)
.

For
(a)
recall that each
\alpha_{j}
is generated by the vector
\frac{1}{2}\nu_{j}
in the Lie algebra of the torus
T\subset\operatorname{Ham}(X)
. We can represent the homotopy class
\widetilde{\alpha}
by the pointwise composition of the
\alpha_{j_{i}}
, which is then generated by the vector

\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2}\nu_{j_{1}}+\dots+\frac{\varepsilon_{m}}{2}\nu_{j_{m}}=\sum_{j}\langle A,H_{j}\rangle\frac{1}{2}\nu_{j}=\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle A,\sum_{j}\nu_{j}H_{j}\Big\rangle.

The right-hand side vanishes since
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}H_{j}=0
, so
\widetilde{\alpha}
can be represented by the path in
\operatorname{Ham}(X)
generated by the zero vector, i.e. the constant path at
\operatorname{id}_{X}
.

For
(b)
, first let
A_{j}=\langle A,H_{j}\rangle\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2\in\mathbb{Z}/2
, or equivalently
A_{j}=A\cdot D_{j}\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2
. By
Corollary

6.9
we then have

(38)

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{class}}(A\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2)\cdot d_{j}=A_{j}\quad\text{for all $j$}.

Since the
d_{j}
form a basis for
\operatorname{H}_{n-1}(L)
, we conclude that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{class}}(A\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}2)
is uniquely determined by (
38
). But by construction
(\alpha_{j_{m}}(1)^{-\varepsilon_{m}}\circ\dots\circ\alpha_{j_{2}}(1)^{-\varepsilon_{2}}\circ\widehat{\eta}_{j_{1}}^{\,\varepsilon_{1}})\cdots\widehat{\eta}_{j_{m}}^{\,\varepsilon_{m}}
intersects
d_{j}
exactly
A_{j}
times mod
2
. This completes the proof of
(b)
and hence of
(v)
.

(vi)
The linear relations follow immediately from the corresponding relations between the
h_{j}
(proved in
Proposition

6.8
) and the fact that
\operatorname{PSS}
is a linear map. The multiplicative relations follow from
(v)
and the fact that every monomial in
R
squares to
1
.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

A possible alternative proof strategy, in the spirit of Haug
[
38
]
, is to consider the
Y
-shaped trajectories contributing to the Floer product on
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
and use the action of
\tau
to show that they all cancel except those in which the only non-constant disc is at the centre of the
Y
. In each of the latter trajectories, the central disc can be glued to its reflection under
\tau
to give a trajectory contributing to the quantum product on
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
, and one can use the action of
\tau
again to show that all other contributions to the quantum product cancel. Whilst this approach is more direct than that used above, it requires a more careful understanding of the relationship between
\tau
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{class}}
, and we were not easily able to verify the necessary transversality conditions on the moduli spaces.

Remark 6.14
.

Mod-
2
cancellation of discs with their reflections by
\tau
shows that
W_{L}(\mathcal{L})=0
, which we claimed at the beginning of
Section

6
.

6.5.
Concluding the construction of the diagram

Recall from
Section

6.1
that the proof of
E
is reduced to constructing the commutative diagram (
28
), namely

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{2*}_{R}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{R}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}}

Recall that
\pi
is reduction modulo
\mathfrak{m}
and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{f}}_{R}
is the
\mathbf{k}
-linear extension of the Frobenius map, which is simply the Frobenius map itself since we have reduced to the case
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Having constructed the isomorphism
\mathcal{D}_{R}
it remains to explain why the diagram commutes. Explicitly, for each
x\in\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
we need to show that

\mathcal{D}_{R}(x^{2})=\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(x\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}\mathfrak{m}).

Since all maps involved are
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphisms, and
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
is generated as a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra by the
Z^{A}\in R
and by the
H_{j}
, it suffices to check this for
x
equal to each
Z^{A}
and to each
H_{j}
. For
x=Z^{A}
it’s immediate, since then
x^{2}=1=x\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}\mathfrak{m}
. We are left to deal with
x=H_{j}
, for which the problem reduces to showing that
\mathcal{D}_{R}(H_{j})^{2}=\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(H_{j})
. For this we extend
[
60
, Theorem 1.13]
.

From the proof of
Proposition

6.12
we have
\mathcal{D}_{R}(H_{j})=\operatorname{PSS}(h_{j})=S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j},\widehat{\eta}_{j})
, so using the twisted homomorphism property we have

\mathcal{D}_{R}(H_{j})^{2}=S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j},\widehat{\eta}_{j})^{2}=S_{\mathbf{L}}(\alpha_{j}\cdot\alpha_{j},(\alpha_{j}(1)^{-1}\circ\widehat{\eta}_{j})\cdot\widehat{\eta}_{j}).

The concatenation
\alpha_{j}\cdot\alpha_{j}
is the loop
\gamma_{j}
in
\operatorname{Ham}(X)
that appears in the proof of
Proposition

6.12
(iv)
. The loop
(\alpha_{j}(1)^{-1}\circ\widehat{\eta}_{j})\cdot\widehat{\eta}_{j}
, meanwhile, represents the zero class in
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L)
since its mod-
2
intersection number with each
d_{i}
is zero. Therefore
\mathcal{D}_{R}(H_{j})^{2}=S_{\mathbf{L}}(\gamma_{j},\eta_{p})
. McDuff–Tolman
[
43
, page 9]
showed that
S(\gamma_{j})=H_{j}
, so (
34
) then gives

\mathcal{D}_{R}(H_{j})^{2}=\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(H_{j}),

which is what we needed.

Remark 6.15
.

The idea of
6.13
suggests another possible strategy, whereby one takes trajectories contributing to
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(x)
and glues them to their reflections under
\tau
to obtain trajectories contributing to
\mathcal{D}_{R}(x^{2})
. Again, however, it is unclear whether the necessary transversality can be achieved.

7.
Understanding
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})

In this penultimate section we prove the two remaining main results from the monotone setting, namely
G
and
J
, which both relate to the map

\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*}):\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}}).

Recall that
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{mf}(S,W_{L}-\lambda)
is the image of
L^{\flat}
under the localised mirror functor
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
is the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completion of its endomorphism dg-algebra, and
\widehat{\Phi}
is the
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphism
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\to\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
induced by
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{L,\lambda}
. The map
\widehat{\Phi}
makes
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
into a
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
-bimodule, and can also be viewed as a bimodule map
\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\to\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
. Then
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
is the map on Hochschild cohomology induced by pushforward of coefficient bimodules.

In
Section

7.1
we prove
G
, that
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
is an isomorphism if
L
is a torus and its local system
\mathcal{L}\in\operatorname{Spec}S
is a critical point of
W_{L}\in S
. By arguments analogous to those in
Section

3.4
, this is independent of the choice of auxiliary data, so it suffices to prove it for a single choice and this is what we do. Then in
Section

7.2
we prove
J
, namely that:

(i)

If
L
is simply connected then

\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*}):\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}

is projection to length zero.

(ii)

If
L
is simply connected and weakly exact then
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
is identified with

\operatorname{ev}_{*}:\operatorname{H}_{-*}(\Lambda L^{-TL})\to\operatorname{H}_{-*}(L^{-TL})\cong\operatorname{H}^{*}(L),

where
\Lambda L
is the free loop space of
L
and
\operatorname{ev}:\Lambda L\to L
is the evaluation-at-basepoint map.

7.1.
Tori

Assume throughout this subsection that
L
is a torus and that
\mathcal{L}
is a critical point of
W_{L}
. It is well-known that
L^{\flat}
is then
wide

[
10
, Proposition 3.3.1]
, meaning that
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
is additively isomorphic to
\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)
. This torus case was studied in
[
58
]
, building heavily on
[
14
,
15
]
, and we begin by recalling some results from there. Note that in
[
58
, Section 6]
all objects are decorated with primes to distinguish them from (different, but related!) unprimed versions appearing earlier in that paper.

Proposition 7.1
.

For a suitable choice of auxiliary data, based on a pearl model for a carefully chosen perfect Morse function, we have the following:

(i)

[
58
, Proposition 6.11]
The matrix factorisation
\mathcal{E}
is quasi-isomorphic in
\mathrm{mf}(S,W_{L}-\lambda)
to an explicit wedge-contraction matrix factorisation
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, and conjugating by this quasi-isomorphism induces a dg-algebra quasi-isomorphism
\Psi:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}_{0}
between their endomorphism algebras.

(ii)

[
58
, below Remark 6.12]
The composition
\Psi\circ\Phi:\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\to\mathcal{B}_{0}
is a quasi-isomorphism, so
\Phi:\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\to\mathcal{B}
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 7.2
.

For these auxiliary data the map
\widehat{\Phi}:\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\to\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

The map
\widehat{\Phi}
can be viewed as the composition of
\Phi
with
\widehat{S}\otimes_{S}{-}:\mathcal{B}\to\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
. The former is a quasi-isomorphism by
Proposition

7.1
(ii)
, whilst the latter is a quasi-isomorphism because for any Noetherian ring
R
and ideal
I\subset R
the
I
-adic completion of
R
is flat over
R

[
24
, Theorem 7.2b.]
.
∎

It is now straightforward to deduce the result we want.

Proposition 7.3
.

Using the same auxiliary data, the map

\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*}):\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Equip both Hochschild complexes with the length filtration. The map
\widehat{\Phi}_{*}
respects these filtrations so induces a map between spectral sequences. On
E_{1}
pages this looks like

\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi})_{*}:\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}})),

which is an isomorphism because
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}):\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})\to\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}})
is. The result then follows from the Eilenberg–Moore comparison theorem (
Theorem

4.5
).
∎

7.2.
Simply connected Lagrangians

For this subsection assume that
L
is simply connected. Then
S=\mathbf{k}
and
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})^{\mathrm{op}}=\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
, so
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
is a map

\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}.

J
(i)
is the following result.

Proposition 7.4
.

In this situation,
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
is projection to length zero.

Proof.

First we describe
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}
more explicitly. Since
S=\mathbf{k}
, we have that
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}=\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))
and that
\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}
reduces to the map

\theta:\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\operatorname{end}_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})))

given by (
25
). As explained in the proof of
Proposition

4.6
, a quasi-inverse to this map is given by

\Pi^{\prime}:\varphi\mapsto\varphi^{0}(e_{L^{\flat}}),

where
e_{L^{\flat}}
is a chain-level representative for the unit in
\operatorname{HF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
. (In
Proposition

4.6
we used
\Pi
, which differs from
\Pi^{\prime}
by a sign
(-1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert}
, but that was because there we were viewing its codomain as an
A_{\infty}
-module rather than an
A_{\infty}
-algebra; see
4.7
.)

Next recall that the pushforward map
\widehat{\Phi}_{*}:\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}),\widehat{\mathcal{B}})
is given by

\varphi\mapsto\sum(-1)^{(\lvert\varphi\rvert-1)\maltese_{i}}\widehat{\Phi}(\dots,\varphi(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}}).

Combining these, we see that
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})=\operatorname{H}(\Pi^{\prime}\circ\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
and that
\Pi^{\prime}\circ\widehat{\Phi}_{*}
is given by

\varphi\mapsto\Pi^{\prime}\Big(\sum(-1)^{(\lvert\varphi\rvert-1)\maltese_{i}}\widehat{\Phi}(\dots,\varphi(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})\Big)=\widehat{\Phi}^{1}(\varphi^{0})(e_{L^{\flat}})=\mu^{2}(\varphi^{0},e_{L^{\flat}}).

The last equality follows from the construction of
\Phi^{1}=\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}^{1}
in
Definition

2.10
. In cohomology
\mu^{2}(\varphi^{0},e_{L^{\flat}})
coincides with
\varphi^{0}
, so
\operatorname{H}(\Pi^{\prime}\circ\widehat{\Phi}_{*}
coincides with
\varphi\mapsto\varphi^{0}
, i.e. projection to length zero.
∎

Now assume that
L
is also weakly exact (
\omega
vanishes on
\pi_{2}(X,L)
). Then Floer cochain algebras reduce to singular cochain algebras, so
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})
reduces to a map
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{C}^{*}(L))\to\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)^{\mathrm{op}}=\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)
. Since
L
is simply connected, we also have an isomorphism
F:\operatorname{H}_{-*}(\Lambda L^{-TL})\to\operatorname{HH}(\operatorname{C}^{*}(L))

[
17
, Corollary 11]
.
J
(ii)
is the following result.

Proposition 7.5
.

In this situation,
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})\circ F
coincides with

\operatorname{ev}_{*}:\operatorname{H}_{-*}(\Lambda L^{-TL})\to\operatorname{H}_{-*}(L^{-TL})\cong\operatorname{H}^{*}(L),

where
\operatorname{ev}:\Lambda L\to L
is the evaluation-at-basepoint map.

Proof.

We begin by recapping the construction of the isomorphism
F:\operatorname{H}_{-*}(\Lambda L^{-TL})\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{C}^{*}(L))
, following
[
17
]
. Letting
\Delta^{k}=\{(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}:0\leq t_{1}\leq\dots\leq t_{k}\leq 1\}
be the standard
k
-simplex, there is a map
\Delta^{k}\times\Lambda L\to L^{k+1}
given by
((t_{1},\dots,t_{k}),\gamma)\mapsto(\operatorname{ev}(\gamma)=\gamma(0),\gamma(t_{1}),\dots,\gamma(t_{k}))
. This induces a map between Thom spectra

f_{k}:(\Delta^{k})_{+}\wedge\Lambda L^{-TL}\to L^{-TL}\wedge(L^{k})_{+},

where the virtual bundle
-TL
on
\Lambda L
implicitly means
\operatorname{ev}^{*}(-TL)
. The corresponding map on chains

\operatorname{C}_{*-k}(\Lambda L^{-TL})\to\operatorname{C}_{*}(L^{-TL})\otimes\operatorname{C}_{*}(L)^{\otimes k}\simeq\hom^{*}(\operatorname{C}^{-*}(L)^{\otimes k},\operatorname{C}_{*}(L^{-TL}))\simeq\hom^{*}(\operatorname{C}^{-*}(L)^{\otimes k},\operatorname{C}^{-*}(L))

simplifies to a chain map

(f_{k})_{*}:\operatorname{C}_{-*}(\Lambda L^{-TL})\to\hom^{*}(\operatorname{C}^{*}(L)[1]^{\otimes k},\operatorname{C}^{*}(L)),

and by
[
17
, Corollary 11]
these
(f_{k})_{*}
assemble to define an isomorphism

F:\operatorname{H}_{*}(\Lambda L^{-TL})\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{C}^{*}(L)).

Combining this description with
Proposition

7.4
we see that
\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{H}(\widehat{\Phi}_{*})\circ F
is
F
followed by projection to length zero, which is precisely
\operatorname{H}((f_{0})_{*})
. By construction, this is
\operatorname{ev}_{*}
, followed by the isomorphism
\operatorname{H}_{-*}(L^{-TL})\to\operatorname{H}^{*}(L)
.
∎

8.
The non-monotone case

In this section only we allow
X
and
L
to be non-monotone, and we work over the Novikov field

\Lambda=\mathbb{C}\llbracket T^{\mathbb{R}}\rrbracket=\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}T^{s_{j}}:a_{j}\in\mathbb{C}\text{ and }s_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ with }s_{j}\to\infty\Big\}

instead of
\mathbf{k}
as above. Let
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
denote the valuation

\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\Big(\sum_{j}a_{j}T^{s_{j}}\Big)=\mathrm{min}\{s_{j}:a_{j}\neq 0\},

with the convention that this minimum is
\infty
if the set is empty, i.e.
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}(0)=\infty
.

In
Section

8.1
we discuss some generalities of Floer theory in this setting. We then move on to describe how our main results adapt, before applying them to the case of toric fibres.

8.1.
Setup

In non-monotone Floer theory the counts of pseudoholomorphic curves are generally infinite, so to make sense of them we must work with suitably filtered and completed algebraic objects. In this section we will work with a specific notion of filtration, which we now make precise.

Definition 8.1
.

An abelian group
A
is
filtered
if it carries a decreasing filtration
F^{s}A
by subgroups, indexed by
s\in\mathbb{R}
, which is exhaustive (
\bigcup_{s}F^{s}A=A
) and Hausdorff (
\bigcap_{s}F^{s}A=0
).
A
is
complete
if the natural map
A\to\varprojlim A/F^{s}A
is an isomorphism; this automatically implies Hausdorffness. The prototypical example of a complete filtered object is
\Lambda
, with
F^{s}\Lambda=\Lambda_{\geq s}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq s})
. If
A
has extra algebraic structure then we require the filtration to be compatible with this. For example, a
filtered
\Lambda
-algebra
is a
\Lambda
-algebra
A
in the usual commutative algebra sense, carrying a filtration
F^{s}A
of the sort just discussed, such that the multiplication
A\otimes A\to A
and ring homomorphism
\Lambda\to A
defining the
\Lambda
-algebra structure are filtered, i.e.
(F^{s_{1}}A)(F^{s_{2}}A)\subset F^{s_{1}+s_{2}}A
for all
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
, and
\Lambda_{\geq s}
maps into
F^{s}A
for all
s
. Similarly, if
A
is augmented then we require the augmentation
A\to\Lambda
to be filtered.

Definition 8.2
.

Given a filtered
\Lambda
-algebra
A
, a rank-
1
local system
\mathcal{E}
on
L
over
A
is
c
-filtered
for
c\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
if each fibre is equipped with a filtration such that: each fibre is isomorphic to
A
as a
filtered

A
-module; for all paths
\gamma
and for all
s
the parallel transport
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}:\mathcal{E}_{\gamma(0)}\to\mathcal{E}_{\gamma(1)}
maps
F^{s}\mathcal{E}_{\gamma(0)}
into
F^{s-c}\mathcal{E}_{\gamma(1)}
. Note that the fibre filtrations need not be (and in general cannot be) chosen consistently in a locally constant way.

Lemma 8.3
.

In terms of the monodromy representation
\xi:\pi_{1}(L)\to A^{\times}
, a rank-
1
local system can be made
c
-filtered if and only if there exists a
c
such that
\xi
lands in
A^{\times}\cap F^{-c}A
.

Proof.

Fix a basepoint
p
in
L
and suppose there exists
c
such that the monodromy representation
\xi:\pi_{1}(L,p)\to A^{\times}
lands in
A^{\times}\cap F^{-c}A
. Now define the fibre filtrations as follows: fix a filtration
F^{s}\mathcal{E}_{p}
of
\mathcal{E}_{p}
; for each point
q
in
L
choose a path
\gamma_{q}
from
p
to
q
, with
\gamma_{p}
taken to be the constant path; equip
\mathcal{E}_{q}
with the filtration
F^{s}\mathcal{E}_{q}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{q}}(F^{s}\mathcal{E}_{p})
. We claim this has the desired property.

Well, take a path
\gamma
from
q
to
r
. We have
\gamma=\gamma_{r}\cdot\beta\cdot\gamma_{q}^{-1}
for some loop
\beta
based at
p
, where
\cdot
denotes concatenation and
-1
denotes reversal. Then
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{r}}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\beta}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{q}}^{-1}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{r}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{q}}^{-1}
preserve the filtration level exactly and
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}=\xi
shifts it down by at most
c
, so we’re done.

The converse is clear: if
\mathcal{E}
is
c
-filtered then apply the defining condition to paths from
p
to
p
.
∎

Technical foundations in pseudoholomorphic curve theory are also required, in order to define compatible virtual fundamental chains on the relevant moduli spaces. Recall that we encapsulate these in
Assumption

†

, which asserts the existence of sufficient machinery to:

(i)

Define the Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
over
\Lambda
and prove the operations satisfy the
A_{\infty}
-relations. We assume the category is strictly unital, but see
1.17
.

(ii)

Construct
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})
as a unital
\Lambda
-algebra homomorphism. We assume that it can be made to land in strictly unital Hochschild cochains.

(iii)

Prove a generation criterion for summands of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
based on injectivity of

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))

on factors of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
.

For the rest of the section we assume
(i)
holds. We will later also impose
(ii)
and
(iii)
.

Objects of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
are compact, connected Lagrangians
L
in
X
, equipped with a pin structure and
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading, plus a
c
-filtered rank-
1
local system over
\Lambda
(for some
c
), and a weak bounding cochain
b\in F^{>c}\operatorname{CF}^{\mathrm{odd}}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat})
whose curvature is
\lambda\in\Lambda
. The
A_{\infty}
-operations on
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
count pseudoholomorphic discs
u
as before, but now additionally weighted by
T^{\omega(u)}
. Each morphism space is a complete filtered
\Lambda
-vector space, so by Gromov compactness these weighted disc counts converge.

Remark 8.4
.

The reader may be surprised that in
Definition

8.2
we allowed the parallel transport maps to decrease the filtration level, i.e. we allowed
c
to be positive. This flexibility is necessary for our applications, and does not cause any convergence problems for the following reason. When defining the contribution of a Floer cochain
c
to an operation
\mu^{k}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})
, assuming first that there are no weak bounding cochains, we count discs with inputs
a_{i}
and output
c
, weighted by
T^{\text{area}}
and by
k+1
parallel transport maps around the boundary. By
Definition

8.2
there is an a priori lower bound on the combined filtration shifts of the
k+1
parallel transport maps, so Gromov compactness ensures that below any given filtration level the count of such discs is finite. Suppose we now also incorporate weak bounding cochains on our Lagrangians. By definition, if the local system on a Lagrangian
L
is
c
-filtered then the weak bounding cochain
b
on
L
must lie in
F^{>c}\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L,L)
, i.e. in
F^{c+\delta}\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L,L)
for some
\delta>0
. Then each insertion of
b
introduces a filtration shift of
\geq-c
from the additional parallel transport map and
\geq c+\delta
from
b
itself. So each time we insert
b
we increase the filtration level of the output by
\geq\delta
, and thus convergence is maintained.

Using exactly the same technical foundations, one can similarly define the Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)_{r}
over any complete filtered
\Lambda
-algebra
R
. Objects now carry
c
-filtered rank-
1
local systems over
R
, plus weak bounding cochains with curvature
r\in R
. Objects of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
can be converted into objects of
\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)_{\lambda}
by applying
R\otimes_{\Lambda}{-}
to their local systems and bounding cochains.

8.2.
Adapting our main results

We now explain how to prove
A+
,
B+
and
C+
, in the form of
Propositions

8.5
,
8.6
and
8.7
. Using
Assumption

†

these are essentially straightforward adaptations of what we have done already.

The starting point is an object
(L^{\flat},b)\in\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, a complete filtered augmented
\Lambda
-algebra
R
, and a lift
(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b})
of
(L^{\flat},b)
to
R
. Recall that the latter is an object in
\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)_{W_{L}}
, for some
W_{L}\in R
, whose reduction modulo the augmentation ideal
\mathfrak{m}
is
(L^{\flat},b)
. Since the localised mirror functor is defined purely in terms of the
A_{\infty}
-operations on
\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)_{\bullet}
(it involves both
\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{F}_{R}(X)_{W_{L}}
), it adapts immediately to the non-monotone setting to give
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),\lambda}:\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}\to\mathrm{mf}(R,W_{L}-\lambda)
. This gives us a mixed Hochschild complex
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}(R,W_{L}-\lambda))
as before, and we have the following.

Proposition 8.5
.

There is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphism

\Theta:\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{R}((\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}))^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda},\mathrm{mf}(R,W_{L}-\lambda)),

which is cohomologically unital and extends the module action of
\operatorname{CF}^{*}_{R}((\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}))^{\mathrm{op}}
on
\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),\lambda}
.

Proof.

The map is defined by the same schematic diagrams and sign twist as in
Definition

3.1
. The pseudoholomorphic curves being counted are of the same form as those defining the
A_{\infty}
-operations, and the fact that
\Theta
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra homomorphism is proved by the same form of argument as the
A_{\infty}
-relations on the category (as in
Proposition

3.3
), so no foundations beyond
Assumption

†

(i)
are needed. Compared with the monotone case, there is one new type of degeneration that can occur, namely formation of a boundary bubble carrying no marked points. This corresponds to bubbling off of
\mu^{0}
terms, which are all multiples of the unit by the assumption that the Lagrangians carry weak bounding cochains. So these degenerations cancel out by strict unitality of the
A_{\infty}
-operations.

The fact that
\Theta
extends the module action follows directly from its definition, as in
Lemma

3.4
. Cohomological unitality is a consequence of
Proposition

8.6
if one is willing to make
Assumption

†

(ii)
, or of the argument suggested in
3.12
if not.
∎

Proposition 8.6
.

If
Assumption

†

(ii)
also holds then
\operatorname{H}(\Theta)\circ\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b})}=\operatorname{H}((\operatorname{\mathcal{LM}}_{(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{b}),\lambda})_{*})\circ\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

This follows from the same form of argument as
Corollary

3.10
, which only uses moduli spaces analogous to those used in defining
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
and in proving that it lands in Hochschild cocycles, so no foundations beyond
Assumption

†

(i)
and
(ii)
are needed. Again there are additional degenerations coming from bubbling off of
\mu^{0}
, but these cancel since we’re assuming
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
lands in strictly unital Hochschild cochains.
∎

Proposition 8.7
.

If
R
is Noetherian then the
\mathfrak{m}
-adically completed map
\widehat{\Theta}_{L^{\flat}}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

The proof of
C
in
Section

4
is essentially purely algebraic, and translates directly to our new setting (working over
\Lambda
now, in place of
\mathbf{k}
, of course). The argument relies on
R
being Noetherian, which was automatic before, but now we have imposed it directly.
∎

Before moving on to study the special case of toric fibres we prove a general result that establishes, under natural conditions, various properties of
R
that are useful for applying the above machinery.

Lemma 8.8
.

Suppose
R
is a local
\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
-algebra which is complete, filtered, and finite-dimensional as a
\Lambda
-algebra. The following then hold:

(i)

The unique augmentation
\varepsilon:R\to\Lambda
, given by quotienting by the unique maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset R
, is automatically filtered.

(ii)

If
\widetilde{\rho}:\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to R^{\times}
denotes the map sending
\gamma
to the image of
\tau^{\gamma}
under the structure map
\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]\to R
, and if
\varepsilon\circ\widetilde{\rho}
lands in
\Lambda_{\geq 0}
, then there exists
c\geq 0
such that
\widetilde{\rho}
lands in
F^{-c}R
. (Note that in the rest of the paper we use
z
as the monomial recording
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
classes. Here we use
\tau
instead, for consistency with
[
57
]
.)

(iii)

R
is Noetherian and
\mathfrak{m}
-adically complete.

Proof.

(i)
Suppose for contradiction that
\varepsilon
is not filtered. Then there exist
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in F^{s}R
such that
\varepsilon(r)\notin\Lambda_{\geq s}
. Say
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\varepsilon(r)=s-\delta
, with
\delta>0
. Replacing
r
with
\varepsilon(r)^{-1}r
, we may assume that
r\in F^{\delta}R
and that
\varepsilon(r)=1
. Now consider the
\Lambda
-linear endomorphism of
R
given by multiplication by
r
, and the associated decomposition of
R
into generalised eigenspaces. Each generalised eigenspace must be an ideal of
R
, but
R
is local so any proper ideal must be contained in the unique maximal ideal. This means that the whole of
R
must be a single generalised eigenspace, so there exists
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
m=\dim_{\Lambda}R
such that
(r-\lambda)^{m}
annihilates
R
and hence
(r-\lambda)^{m}=0
. Applying
\varepsilon
to this expression gives
\lambda=1
, and expanding it out we then obtain

(39)

1=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\binom{m}{j}(-1)^{m-j}r^{j}.

The right-hand side of (
39
) lies in
F^{\delta}R
, so
1
also lies in
F^{\delta}R
. Then for all positive integers
p
we get
1=1^{p}\in F^{p\delta}R
and hence

1\in\bigcap_{p=1}^{\infty}F^{p\delta}R=0,

which is impossible (here we used that the filtration on
R
is Hausdorff). So
\varepsilon
is indeed filtered.

(ii)
Fix elements
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{2n}
which generate
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
as a monoid (e.g. a basis and their negatives) and let
\widetilde{\rho}_{j}=\widetilde{\rho}(\gamma_{j})
. It suffices to show that for each
j
there exists
c_{j}\geq 0
such that all positive powers of
\widetilde{\rho}_{j}
lie in
F^{-c_{j}}R
, since then
c=c_{1}+\dots c_{2n}
works. So fix
j
and consider
\widetilde{\rho}_{j}\in R^{\times}
. As in
(i)
, we can consider the generalised eigenspace decomposition for the action of
\widetilde{\rho}_{j}
on
R
, and deduce that
(\widetilde{\rho}_{j}-\varepsilon(\widetilde{\rho}_{j}))^{m}=0
for some
m
, so

(40)

\widetilde{\rho}_{j}^{m}=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\binom{m}{j}(-\varepsilon(\widetilde{\rho}_{j}))^{j}\widetilde{\rho}_{j}^{m-j}.

By assumption we have
\varepsilon(\widetilde{\rho}_{j})\in\Lambda_{\geq 0}
. So if we pick
c_{j}\geq 0
such that
\widetilde{\rho}_{j},\dots,\widetilde{\rho}_{j}^{m-1}\in F^{-c_{j}}R
then (
40
) tells us that
\widetilde{\rho}_{j}^{m}
also lies in
F^{-c_{j}}R
. Repeatedly multiplying (
40
) through by
\widetilde{\rho}_{j}
we get by induction that
\widetilde{\rho}_{j}^{p}\in F^{-c_{j}}R
for all positive integers
p
, which is what we wanted.

(iii)
The Noetherian condition is obvious from finite-dimensionality.
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completeness, meanwhile, follows from the fact that
\mathfrak{m}^{p}=0
for some
p
. The latter can be proved using the Artinian property (via
[
59
, Lemma 10.53.4]
, for example), or directly by picking a basis
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
for
\mathfrak{m}
and using generalised eigenspace decompositions as in
(i)
to show that each
r_{j}
is nilpotent.
∎

8.3.
Split-generation by toric fibres

In this subsection we specialise to the case where
X
is a compact toric manifold equipped with its canonical
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading, and prove
L
which states that its Fukaya category is generated by toric fibres. The precise statement is
Proposition

8.16
below. During the exposition we include several lemmas whose proofs we defer to the end of the subsection, to avoid distracting from the main thread of the argument.

First we introduce some notation. Let

(41)

\Delta=\{x\in\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}:\langle\nu_{j},x\rangle\geq-\lambda_{j}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,N\}

be the moment polytope of
X
as in
Section

C.1
, and let
L
be an arbitrary toric fibre. By translating
\Delta
we may assume that the
\lambda_{j}
are all positive and that
L=\mu^{-1}(0)
. As discussed in
Section

C.1
, the
\nu_{j}
can naturally be viewed as elements of
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, where they are the boundaries of the
basic disc classes

\beta_{j}
of area
\lambda_{j}
.

Definition 8.9
.

Let
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
be the submonoid of
\mathbb{R}\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
generated by
(\lambda_{1},\nu_{1}),\dots,(\lambda_{N},\nu_{N})
and
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\oplus 0
. Its monoid ring
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}]
naturally lies inside
A\coloneqq\mathbb{C}[T^{\mathbb{R}}][\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
, with
(s,\gamma)\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
represented by the monomial
T^{s}\tau^{\gamma}
. Now define a filtration on
A
by
F^{s}A=T^{s}\mathbb{C}[\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}]
. This is exhaustive since the
\nu_{i}
span
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
and is Hausdorff since all exponents of
T
appearing in
F^{s}A
are at least
s
. Let
\overline{A}
be the completion of
A
with respect to this filtration. This naturally contains
\Lambda
as the completion of
\mathbb{C}[T^{\mathbb{R}}]
, so is a complete filtered
\Lambda
-algebra.

For a specific closed ideal
I\subset\overline{A}
, Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono
[
32
]
construct an isomorphism of filtered
\Lambda
-algebras
\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\overline{A}/I
which they call the Kodaira–Spencer map. Their filtration is defined slightly differently from ours, but a proof using the present conventions is given in
[
57
]
(the fact that both
\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}}
and its inverse respect the filtration follows from the fact that the domain and codomain can both be defined over
\Lambda_{0}
, where the filtrations become
F^{s}{-}=T^{s}{-}
, and filteredness then follows from
\Lambda_{\geq 0}
-linearity). The inclusions of
\Lambda
and
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
into
\overline{A}
make
\overline{A}/I
, and hence
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, into a
\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
-algebra. Even though
\overline{A}
is strictly bigger than
\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
, the next result says that this difference disappears after quotienting by
I
.

Lemma 8.10
.

The structure map
\sigma:\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]\to\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
is surjective.

Remark 8.11
.

There is an obvious filtration of
\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
by
\Lambda_{\geq s}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
, but
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
is
not
generally a filtered
\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
-algebra with respect to this filtration, i.e.
\sigma
is not in general filtered.

Thus
\operatorname{Spec}\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
is a zero-dimensional closed subscheme of
\operatorname{Spec}\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
, and hence
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
is a product of local rings, each corresponding to a single closed point of
\operatorname{Spec}\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
. Since
\Lambda
is algebraically closed
[
30
, Lemma A.1]
, each of these closed points corresponds to a homomorphism
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\Lambda^{\times}
. Let
R
be one of the local factors of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, equipped with the filtration induced by the projection
\pi:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to R
. Note that
R
is finite-dimensional over
\Lambda
, and is a
\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
-algebra via
\pi\circ\sigma
. To start putting us in the setup of the previous subsection, we have the following.

Lemma 8.12
.

The filtration on
R
satisfies
Definition

8.1
and is complete.

We are thus in the situation of
Lemma

8.8
, so by part
(i)
of that result
R
is a complete filtered augmented
\Lambda
-algebra. The augmentation
\varepsilon:R\to\Lambda
is unique, with kernel given by the unique maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
in
R
. Let
\widetilde{\rho}:\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to R^{\times}
be the homomorphism
\gamma\mapsto\pi\circ\sigma(\tau^{\gamma})
, and let
\rho=\varepsilon\circ\widetilde{\rho}
. Note that
\rho
is the homomorphism
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\Lambda^{\times}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{m}
as a closed point of
\operatorname{Spec}\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
.

The next result is the key step in defining our objects
L^{\flat}
and
\mathbf{L}
.

Lemma 8.13

(Morally equivalent to
[
32
, Lemma 2.2.2]
)
.

By translating
\Delta
if necessary, i.e. by changing which toric fibre
L
is, we may assume that
\rho
lands in
\Lambda_{\geq 0}
.

Assume now that this translation has been done. Then
\rho
defines (the monodromy of) a
0
-filtered rank-
1
local system
\mathcal{L}
on
L
over
\Lambda
, and we take
L^{\flat}
to be
L
equipped with this local system, the standard spin structure (as defined in
Section

C.1
), and an arbitrary
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading (equivalent to an orientation). We use Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono’s de Rham model and torus-equivariant perturbation data from
[
30
]
, and take the weak bounding cochain
b
to be zero. This is indeed a weak bounding cochain: in
[
30
, Section 12]
they show that for any rank-
1
local system
\mathcal{L}_{0}
with monodromy
\rho_{0}:\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, every
b_{0}\in\operatorname{H}^{1}(L;\Lambda_{>0})
is a weak bounding cochain, and the same argument applies with our
\mathcal{L}
and
\rho
in place of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
\rho_{0}
. Let
\lambda
be the curvature of
(L^{\flat},b=0)
.

Remark 8.14
.

By
[
30
, Lemma 11.8]
we expect
(L^{\flat},0)
to be isomorphic to
L
equipped with
\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
b_{0}
if
\rho=\rho_{0}\cdot e^{b_{0}}
.

Similarly, by
Lemma

8.8
(ii)

\widetilde{\rho}
defines a
c
-filtered rank-
1
local system on
L
over
R
, whose reduction modulo
\mathfrak{m}
is
\mathcal{L}
, and we take
\mathbf{L}
to be
L
equipped with this local system and with the same spin structure and orientation as
L^{\flat}
. We use the same de Rham model and perturbation data, and take its weak bounding cochain
\mathbf{b}
also to be zero. By construction, this
(\mathbf{L},0)
is a lift of
(L^{\flat},0)
to
R
.

Lemma 8.15
.

The maps

\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to R\quad\text{and}\quad\pi:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to R

coincide.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
is generated as a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra by the
H_{j}
, it suffices to show that the maps agree on each
H_{j}
. By definition
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(H_{j})\in R
counts holomorphic discs
u
mapping an interior marked point to the divisor
D_{j}
, weighted by

\widetilde{\rho}([\partial u])=\pi\circ\sigma(\tau^{[\partial u]})=\pi\circ\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}}^{-1}(\tau^{[\partial u]}\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}I).

Meanwhile,
\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}}(H_{j})\in\overline{A}/I
counts the same thing, but weighted by
\tau^{[\partial u]}\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}I
. We can choose the same perturbation data for
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(H_{j})
as for
\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}}(H_{j})
, namely the torus-equivariant Kuranishi multisections of
[
31
, Lemma 6.5]
, and we then get

\widehat{\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}}{}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(H_{j})=\pi\circ\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}}^{-1}(\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}}(H_{j}))=\pi(H_{j}),

as wanted.
∎

Since
R
is Noetherian and
\mathfrak{m}
-adically complete by
Lemma

8.8
(iii)
,
K
gives the following, which is the precise statement of
L
.

Proposition 8.16
.

Under
Assumption

†

,
(L^{\flat},0)
split-generates the summand of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
corresponding to the factor
R
of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
.
∎

Remark 8.17
.

In
[
32
, Section 4.7]
, Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono factor their isomorphism
\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}}
through Hochschild cohomology via a closed–open map, denoted by
\widehat{q}
. However, they work over
\Lambda_{\geq 0}
, whereas for the generation criterion one must work over
\Lambda
. For example, their
\widehat{q}
is injective on the whole of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X;\Lambda_{\geq 0})
, regardless of which toric fibre one is looking at, whilst for split-generation and injectivity of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}
one must choose a specific toric fibre for each factor of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
.

We wrap up this subsection by filling in the promised proofs of
Lemmas

8.10
,
8.12
and
8.13
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.10
.

Let
\tau_{j}
denote
T^{\lambda_{j}}\tau^{\nu_{j}}
. It suffices to prove the following claim:
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
is spanned over
\Lambda
by finitely many monomials in the
\sigma(\tau_{j})
. To prove this claim, we use
[
57
, Lemma 2.20]
(corresponding to
[
30
, Theorem 4.6]
), which tells us that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}}(H_{j})\in\tau_{j}+F^{>0}\overline{A}
modulo
I
, and hence that
\sigma(\tau_{j})\in H_{j}+F^{>0}\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, where
H_{j}
is the Poincaré dual of the
j
th toric divisor. The claim then follows from the fact that
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X;\Lambda_{\geq 0})
is spanned over
\Lambda_{\geq 0}
by finitely many monomials in the
H_{j}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

8.12
.

The filtration on
R
is automatically decreasing and exhaustive, so we just need to show that it is complete (since this also implies Hausdorffness). For this note that if
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
is a
\Lambda_{\geq 0}
-basis for
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X;\Lambda_{\geq 0})
then the filtration on
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
is defined by
F^{s}\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{k}\Lambda_{\geq s}e_{j}
. Note further that we can always pick the basis
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
such that the first
l
elements form a
\Lambda_{\geq 0}
-basis for
(\ker\pi)\cap\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X;\Lambda_{\geq 0})
. Having done this, the images
\pi(e_{l+1}),\dots,\pi(e_{k})
of
e_{l+1},\dots,e_{k}
in
R
form a
\Lambda
-basis for
R
, and the induced filtration on
R
is
F^{s}R=\bigoplus_{j=l+1}^{k}\Lambda_{\geq s}\pi(e_{j})
. Thus
R
is isomorphic to
\Lambda^{k-l}
as a filtered
\Lambda
-module, and hence is complete.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

8.13
.

We need to show that by translating
\Delta
we can ensure that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\rho:\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{R}
is non-negative, which is equivalent to it being identically zero.

To begin, note that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}
is naturally an element of the space
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
in which
\Delta
lives (in fact,
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
is naturally identified with
\operatorname{H}^{1}(L;\mathbb{R})
). Translating
\Delta
by
-a\in\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
corresponds to changing
x
to
x+a
in (
41
), or equivalently to changing
\lambda_{j}
to
\lambda_{j}+\langle\nu_{j},a\rangle
. The allowed translations are those that keep
0
in the interior of the polytope, i.e. those satisfying
\langle\nu_{j},a\rangle>-\lambda_{j}
for all
j
. The effect of this translation on
\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}}
is to multiply
\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}}(H_{j})
by
T^{\langle\nu_{j},a\rangle}
. The effect on
\sigma
is therefore to multiply
\sigma(\tau^{\nu_{j}})
by
T^{-\langle\nu_{j},a\rangle}
. The effect on
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\rho(\nu_{j})
in turn is thus to subtract
\langle\nu_{j},a\rangle
. So to be able to make
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\rho
identically zero, we need to be able to choose
a
such that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\rho(\nu_{j})=\langle\nu_{j},a\rangle
for all
j
, and
\langle\nu_{j},a\rangle>-\lambda_{j}
for all
j
. In other words, the first part of the lemma reduces to showing that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\rho(\nu_{j})>-\lambda_{j}
for all
j
.

Next note that
\rho(\nu_{j})
can be written as
\varepsilon\circ\pi\circ\sigma(\tau^{\nu_{j}})=T^{-\lambda_{j}}\varepsilon\circ\pi\circ\sigma(\tau_{j})
, where
\tau_{j}
denotes
T^{\lambda_{j}}\tau^{\nu_{j}}\in\Lambda[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]
. Letting
r_{j}=\pi\circ\sigma(\tau_{j})
be the image of
\tau_{j}
in
R
, our task reduces to showing that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\varepsilon(r_{j})>0
for all
j
. Since
\tau_{j}
lies in filtration level
0
in
\overline{A}
we have that
\sigma(\tau_{j})
lies in
F^{0}\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
, and hence that
r_{j}
lies in
F^{0}R
. Since
\varepsilon
is filtered (
Lemma

8.12
) we immediately get
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\varepsilon(r_{j})\geq 0
for all
j
. We thus want to show that there are no
j
with
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\varepsilon(r_{j})=0
.

Suppose then, for contradiction, that such
j
do exist: call them
j_{1},\dots,j_{k}
, and let
\varepsilon(r_{j_{i}})=a_{i}+b_{i}
, where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
b_{i}\in\Lambda_{>0}
. If the toric divisors
D_{j_{1}},\dots,D_{j_{k}}
have empty intersection then
H_{j_{1}}\cdots H_{j_{k}}
lies in
F^{>0}\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
. Since
\sigma(\tau_{j})\in H_{j}+F^{>0}\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
we deduce that
r_{j_{1}}\cdots r_{j_{k}}\in F^{>0}R
. Applying
\varepsilon
we conclude that
a_{1}\cdots a_{k}\in\Lambda_{>0}
, which is nonsense. Therefore the divisors
D_{j_{1}},\dots,D_{j_{k}}
have non-empty intersection, and hence the vectors
\nu_{j_{1}},\dots,\nu_{j_{k}}
are linearly independent. Modulo elements of
F^{>0}\overline{A}
, the ideal
I\subset\overline{A}
contains the components of
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}\tau_{j}
. We therefore have
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}r_{j}\in F^{>0}R
and hence
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}\varepsilon(r_{j})\in\Lambda_{>0}
. But the constant term in
\sum_{j}\nu_{j}\varepsilon(r_{j})
is
\sum_{i}\nu_{j_{i}}a_{i}
, which is non-zero since the
\nu_{j_{i}}
are linearly independent. This gives the desired contradiction, and we conclude that there are no
j
with
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\varepsilon(r_{j})=0
. Hence
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\varepsilon(r_{j})>0
for all
j
and thus we can indeed translate
\Delta
to make
\operatorname{\mathfrak{v}}\circ\rho
identically zero.
∎

Remark 8.18
.

In the presence of a bulk deformation from
F^{>0}\operatorname{QH}^{\mathrm{even}}(X)
, the proofs go through unchanged. The results similarly hold in the presence of a B-field
B\in\operatorname{QH}^{2}(X;\mathbb{C})
, but now with one small modification: in the proofs of
Lemma

8.10
and
Lemma

8.13
we have
\sigma(\tau_{j})=a_{j}H_{j}+F^{>0}\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
for some
a_{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
(previously
a_{j}
was
1
); but this doesn’t affect the arguments.

Appendix A
The generation criterion

In this appendix we review the decomposition of the Fukaya category induced by decompositions of quantum cohomology, and derive the corresponding generation criterion. We then look at how this relates to the more standard splittings of the Fukaya category by eigenvalues of the first Chern class. This material is all well-known to experts, but we are not aware of the details being spelled out in the literature, especially
Lemma

A.7
.

We remind the reader that, having finished
Section

8
, we are now back in the monotone setting.

A.1.
Decompositions of the Fukaya category

Suppose that
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
decomposes into a product of
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded
\mathbf{k}
-algebras
Q_{i}
. For each
\lambda
this induces a splitting of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
into pairwise orthogonal subcategories
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
