, and the goal of this subsection is to summarise the construction and prove that it is compatible with the closed–open map. See also
[
52
, Section (5b)]
.

Remark A.1
.

Similarly, a decomposition of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
into a product of ungraded
\mathbf{k}
-algebras
Q_{i}
gives rise to a decomposition of the ungraded category
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{un}}
into pairwise orthogonal subcategories
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}^{\mathrm{un}}
. We will not separately mention this case further as the arguments are identical.

The decomposition of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
into the
Q_{i}
corresponds to a decomposition of its unit
1_{X}
into pairwise orthogonal idempotents
e_{i}
of even degree. Roughly speaking,
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
is the image of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
under the module action of
e_{i}
via
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}
. More precisely, let
\mathcal{Q}
denote the
A_{\infty}
-category
\operatorname{mod}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
of
A_{\infty}
-modules over
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, and let
\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
be the smallest full subcategory of
\mathcal{Q}
that contains the image of the Yoneda embedding
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}\to\mathcal{Q}
and is closed under taking shifts and mapping cones. We may implicitly view
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
as a full subcategory of
\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
or of
\mathcal{Q}
, via Yoneda. Given an object
T
in
\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
and an idempotent
\wp
in
\hom^{*}(T,T)
, Seidel shows in
[
51
, Section (4b)]
that we can construct an object in
\mathcal{Q}
representing the
abstract image
of
\wp
. Let
\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{Q}
comprising all such abstract images of idempotents. The category
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
is defined to be the full subcategory of
\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
given by the abstract images of specific idempotents, constructed as follows. There is a homomorphism
[
52
, Section (1c)]

\Gamma^{\mathrm{mod}}:\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{Q}),

such that the maps

\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\xrightarrow{\ \ \text{restrict}\,\circ\,\Gamma^{\mathrm{mod}}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\xrightarrow{\ \ \text{restrict}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})

are mutually inverse isomorphisms. We can therefore view
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
as a map to
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})
, and similarly talk about
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{T}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{T}^{0}
for objects
T
in
\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
. For each
T
in
\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
we get an idempotent
p_{T,i}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(e_{i})
in
\operatorname{Hom}^{*}(T,T)\coloneqq\operatorname{H}^{*}(\hom(T,T))
, and Seidel shows in
[
51
, Section (4b)]
that we can lift this
p_{T,i}
to a chain-level idempotent up to homotopy
\wp_{T,i}
in
\hom^{*}(T,T)
. He further shows that the abstract image
T_{i}
of
\wp_{T,i}
is independent of the choice of lift
\wp_{T,i}
, up to quasi-isomorphism. The category
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
is defined to be the full subcategory of
\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
comprising these images
T_{i}
.

For any objects
S
and
T
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, and any
i
and
j
, we have natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(S,T_{i})

\displaystyle=p_{T,i}\operatorname{Hom}(S,T)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(S_{j},T)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}(S,T)p_{S,j}

[
51
, Lemmas 4.4 and 4.5]
. Since
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}(e_{i})
is a Hochschild cocycle of even degree we also have

p_{T,i}\operatorname{Hom}(S,T)=\operatorname{Hom}(S,T)p_{S,i}.

Combining these results with the fact that
p_{S,i}p_{S,j}=0
for
i\neq j
(since
e_{i}e_{j}=0
), we see that for
i\neq j
the objects
S_{j}
and
T_{i}
—and hence the categories
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
and
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,j}
—are indeed orthogonal. Hence the Hochschild cohomology
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\cong\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})
decomposes as
\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i})
as a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded algebra.

Proposition A.2
.

The closed–open map

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)=\bigoplus_{i}Q_{i}\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})=\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i})

is block-diagonal with respect to this decomposition.

Proof.

Given distinct
i
and
j
we need to show that

(42)

\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\cong\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\xrightarrow{\ \ \text{restrict}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i})

vanishes on
Q_{j}
. Since this map is an algebra homomorphism, and we have
e_{i}Q_{j}=0
, it suffices to show that the image of
e_{i}
under (
42
) is invertible. By an argument
[
54
, Lemma 2.8]
with the Eilenberg–Moore comparison theorem, it suffices in turn to show that there exists a constant
\kappa\neq 0
such that for each object
Y
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
we have
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{Y}(e_{i})=\kappa\cdot 1_{Y}
, where
1_{Y}
denotes the unit in
\operatorname{Hom}^{*}(Y,Y)
. But we can write
Y
as
T_{i}
(using the above notation) for some
T
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, and then have

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{Y}^{0}(e_{i})=p_{T,i}\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(e_{i})=(p_{T,i})^{2}.

Since
p_{T,i}
is exactly
1_{Y}
, we’re done with
\kappa=1
.
∎

A.2.
The generation criterion for summands

Recall that the generation criterion stated in
Theorem

1.1
assumed injectivity of the whole map
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
. If, however, one is only interested in split-generating one of the pieces
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
then it suffices to check injectivity on
Q_{i}
. The result is well-known to experts, and the proof we give is based on
[
54
, Corollary 2.19]
, which in turn uses important ideas from
[
1
]
and
[
48
]
, but we present it for completeness.

Remark A.3
.

Again, identical arguments apply to the ungraded categories
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}^{\mathrm{un}}
.

Theorem A.4
.

Assuming that
X
is compact, if
\mathcal{G}
is a full subcategory of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
and if the composition

Q_{i}\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i})\xrightarrow{\ \ \text{restrict}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{G})

is injective then
\mathcal{G}
split-generates
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
. In particular, a single Lagrangian
L^{\flat}
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
split-generates if

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{L^{\flat}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\xrightarrow{\ \ \text{restrict}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\operatorname{CF}^{*}(L^{\flat},L^{\flat}))

is injective on
Q_{i}
.

Proof.

In order to show that
\mathcal{G}
split generates an object
Y
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
, it suffices to show that the composition

(43)

\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{\mathcal{OC}}_{\lambda}\ \ }\operatorname{QH}^{*+n}(X;\mathbf{k})\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{Y}\ \ }\operatorname{Hom}^{*+n}(Y,Y)

hits
1_{Y}
, where

\operatorname{\mathcal{OC}}_{\lambda}:\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\cong\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\to\operatorname{QH}^{*+n}(X;\mathbf{k})

is the
open–closed string map
. We refer the reader to
[
1
]
for the definition of this map and for the proof that hitting
1_{Y}
implies split-generation; the argument breaks into a geometric part
[
1
, Proposition 1.3]
based on the Cardy relation and a purely algebraic part
[
1
, Lemma 1.4]
. Writing
Y
as
T_{i}
as in the proof of
Proposition

A.2
, and using the fact that
1_{Y}=p_{T,i}=\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(e_{i})
, we see that for (
43
) to hit
1_{Y}
it is enough to show that
\operatorname{\mathcal{OC}}_{\lambda}
restricted to (the image of)
\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{G})
hits
e_{i}
. This is what we shall prove.

By
[
54
, Proposition 2.6]
there is a commutative diagram

(44)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)^{\vee}[-2n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})^{\vee}[-n]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\mapsto\langle\alpha,{-}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{OC}}_{\lambda}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where
∨
denotes linear dual and
\langle{-},{-}\rangle
is the Poincaré duality pairing on
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
(this is where compactness of
X
enters). Assume for now the following claim: the
Q_{j}
are pairwise orthogonal with respect to this pairing. Then the top horizontal arrow decomposes into isomorphisms
Q_{j}\to Q_{j}^{\vee}[-2n]
. It follows from the construction of the bottom horizontal arrow that it respects passage to full subcategories, so it also decomposes into isomorphisms

\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i})\to\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i})^{\vee}[-n].

We then deduce from
Proposition

A.2
that
\operatorname{\mathcal{OC}}_{\lambda}
is block diagonal

\operatorname{\mathcal{OC}}_{\lambda}:\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda})\cong\bigoplus_{j}\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,j})\to\operatorname{QH}^{*+n}(X;\mathbf{k})=\bigoplus_{j}Q_{j}[n].

and that each block is dual to the corresponding block in
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
.

Now focus on the
i
th blocks. We get that

\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i})\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{\mathcal{OC}}_{\lambda}\ \ }Q_{i}[n]\quad\text{and}\quad Q_{i}\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i})

are dual to each other, and by using again the compatibility of the bottom horizontal arrow in (
44
) with passage to full subcategories we can upgrade this to

(45)

\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{HH}_{*}(\text{inclusion})\ \ }\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i})\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{\mathcal{OC}}_{\lambda}\ \ }Q_{i}[n]

being dual to

(46)

Q_{i}\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i})\xrightarrow{\ \ \text{restrict}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{G}).

We are assuming that (
46
) is injective, so (
45
) is surjective and hence hits
e_{i}
, as we wanted.

It remains to prove the claim that the
Q_{j}
are pairwise orthogonal with respect to the Poincaré pairing. So take distinct
j
and
k
, and arbitrary elements
\alpha\in Q_{j}
and
\beta\in Q_{k}
. We then have

\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=\langle e_{j}\alpha,e_{k}\beta\rangle=\langle\alpha e_{j},e_{k}\beta\rangle=\langle\alpha,e_{j}e_{k}\beta\rangle=0,

completing the proof, where the second equality uses graded-commutativity of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
and the third uses the Frobenius algebra relation.
∎

A.3.
Eigenvalues of
c_{1}

This subsection only applies to the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded Fukaya category. We shall therefore assume throughout that all Lagrangians are orientable.

Let
c_{1}
denote the first Chern class of
X
, and consider the
\mathbf{k}
-linear endomorphism
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
given by quantum multiplication by
c_{1}
. The decomposition of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
that’s used most often is into generalised eigenspaces
Q_{i}
of
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
, corresponding to eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
. It is this decomposition that Sheridan considers in
[
54
]
, and for which he proves the analogue of
Theorem

A.4
. He works over
\mathbb{C}
, where, as we we will prove shortly,

(47)

\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}&\text{if }\lambda=\lambda_{i}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In particular,
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
is zero unless
\lambda
is equal to some
\lambda_{i}
, and in this case a full subcategory
\mathcal{G}
split-generates if
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
is injective on the generalised eigenspace
Q_{i}
(this is precisely
[
54
, Corollary 2.19]
; or, assuming (
47
), it is a special case of
Theorem

A.4
).

Remark A.5
.

Since
\mathbb{C}
is algebraically closed,
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X;\mathbb{C})
decomposes fully into generalised eigenspaces. We will phrase our results for general
\mathbf{k}
, without assuming this full decomposition.

The goal of this subsection is to explain the general version of (
47
) and the corresponding generation result. The first lemma we need is a precise statement of the result mentioned above
I
.

Lemma A.6

(Auroux–Kontsevich–Seidel)
.

For all
T
in
\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
we have

CO^{0}_{T}(c_{1})=\lambda\cdot 1_{T}.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the result when
T
is a single (orientable!) Lagrangian
L^{\flat}
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
. In this case, the argument of
[
7
, Lemma 6.7]
and
[
54
, Lemma 2.7]
, shows that

(48)

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(2c_{1})=2\lambda\cdot 1_{L^{\flat}}.

The proof involves lifting
2c_{1}\in\operatorname{H}^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
to the Maslov class
\mu\in\operatorname{H}^{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
of
L
, and picking a cycle
Z\subset X\setminus L
Poincaré dual to
\mu
. Then
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{L^{\flat}}(2c_{1})
can be computed as the class swept by pseudoholomorphic discs with an interior input on
Z
and a boundary output. Index
0
discs are (almost) constant so don’t meet
Z
and hence don’t contribute; index
2
discs without the interior marked point constraint sweep
\lambda\cdot 1_{L^{\flat}}
, and reintroducing the constraint doubles the result since each disc has intersection number
2
with
Z
; and higher index discs do not contribute for degree reasons.

Since
L
is orientable we can refine this using an observation of Tonkonog
[
60
, Section 1.2]
, as follows. The bundle pair
(\Lambda^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}TX,\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}}^{n}TL)
over
(X,L)
is classified by a homotopy class of map

\varphi:(X,L)\to(\mathrm{BU}(1),\mathrm{B}(\mathbb{Z}/2))\cong(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}),

and
\mu
is defined to be
\varphi^{*}g
, where
g\in\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})
is the unique generator which maps to twice the hyperplane class
h\in\operatorname{H}^{2}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})
. As
L
is orientable,
\varphi
factors as

(X,L)\xrightarrow{\ \ \psi\ \ }(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{\infty},\text{point})\xrightarrow{\ \ \text{inclusion}\ \ }(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}),

so
\mu=\varphi^{*}g=\psi^{*}(2h)
. By construction,
\psi^{*}h
maps to
c_{1}
under
\operatorname{H}^{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{H}^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
. Now pick a cycle
Z\subset X\setminus L
Poincaré dual to
\psi^{*}h
, and compute
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}(c_{1})
directly using the above argument but with the new
Z
.
∎

Now fix an arbitrary decomposition
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)=\bigoplus_{i}Q_{i}
as in the preceding subsections, and let the
e_{i}
be the corresponding idempotents. The generalisation of (
47
) is then the following.

Lemma A.7
.

Given
\lambda\in\mathbf{k}
, let
Q_{\lambda}
denote the generalised
\lambda
-eigenspace of
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
(this is zero unless
\lambda
is an eigenvalue) and let
Q_{\lambda}^{\perp}
denote the unique
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
-invariant complement, which we will construct during the proof. For each
i
we obtain factors
Q_{i}\cap Q_{\lambda}
and
Q_{i}\cap Q_{\lambda}^{\perp}
of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
. The corresponding pieces
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i,\cap}
and
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i,\perp}
of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
then satisfy

\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i,\cap}=\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i,\perp}=0.

Before proving
Lemma

A.7
, we mention some easy consequences. Morally these results mean that any given splitting may as well be refined by intersecting with the generalised eigenspace splitting.

Corollary A.8

(
[
7
, Lemma 6.8]
)
.

We have
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}=0
unless
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
.

Proof.

Consider the trivial splitting
Q_{1}=\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
. We get from
Lemma

A.7
that

\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,1,\cap}=\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,1}=\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}.

The left-hand side is the subcategory of
\operatorname{\Pi}\operatorname{Tw}\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
associated to the factor
Q_{1}\cap Q_{\lambda}=Q_{\lambda}
. But this factor, and hence this subcategory, is zero unless
\lambda
is an eigenvalue.
∎

Remark A.9
.

This can be proved directly as in
[
7
]
.

Remark A.10
.

The result may fail for the ungraded category. For example, in characteristic
2
the category
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2})^{\mathrm{un}}_{0}
contains the non-zero object
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, but the eigenvalues of
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
are the cube roots of unity. We learnt of this subtlety from Dmitry Tonkonog via
[
25
, Example 7.2.4]
.

Corollary A.11
.

If
Q_{i}\subset Q_{\lambda^{\prime}}
then
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}=0
unless
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}
.

Proof.

If
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
then
Q_{\lambda^{\prime}}\subset Q_{\lambda}^{\perp}
. If also
Q_{i}\subset Q_{\lambda^{\prime}}
then we get
Q_{i}\cap Q_{\lambda}=0
and hence

\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}=\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i,\cap}=0.\qed

Proof of
Lemma

A.7
.

Our first task is to construct the splitting
Q_{\lambda}\oplus Q_{\lambda}^{\perp}
. Let
\chi\in\mathbf{k}[t]
be the characteristic polynomial of
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
, of degree
n
, and let
q
be the quotient of
\chi
by the greatest power of
t-\lambda
dividing it. By Bézout’s lemma there exist
f
and
g
in
\mathbf{k}[t]
such that

(49)

(t-\lambda)^{n}f+qg=1.

Let
e
and
e^{\perp}
be the elements of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
obtained by evaluating
qg
and
(t-\lambda)^{n}f
at
c_{1}
respectively.

We claim that these are orthogonal idempotents generating the required splitting. (Here we mean orthogonal in the algebraic sense, i.e.
ee^{\perp}=0
, but by the Frobenius algebra property this implies orthogonality with respect to the inner product.) To prove this claim, note that by (
49
) we have
e+e^{\perp}=1_{X}
. Note also that
\chi
divides
(t-\lambda)^{n}q
and
\chi(c_{1})=0
by Cayley–Hamilton, so we have
ee^{\perp}=0
. Combining these two facts we get that
e
and
e^{\perp}
are indeed orthogonal idempotents. Next observe that the kernel of
(c_{1}-\lambda\cdot 1_{X})^{n}\mathbin{\star}
is annihilated by
e^{\perp}
and, again using
\chi(c_{1})=0
, that it contains
e
. It follows that this kernel, which is precisely
Q_{\lambda}
, is given by
e\mathbin{\star}\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
. It is clear that
Q_{\lambda}^{\perp}\coloneqq e^{\perp}\mathbin{\star}\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
is a
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
-invariant complement to
Q_{\lambda}
, so to complete the claim it suffices to explain why this is unique. Well, given any other
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
-invariant complement
V
we have
e\mathbin{\star}V\subset V
, since
e
is a polynomial in
c_{1}
. Then

e\mathbin{\star}V\subset V\cap e\mathbin{\star}\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)=V\cap Q_{\lambda}=0,

so
V
lies in the kernel of
e\mathbin{\star}
, which is
Q_{\lambda}^{\perp}
. Thus, by dimension,
V
must be equal to
Q_{\lambda}^{\perp}
.

With this in hand, the idempotents generating
Q_{i}\cap Q_{\lambda}
and
Q_{i}\cap Q_{\lambda}^{\perp}
are
e_{i}e
and
e_{i}e^{\perp}
. Given an object
T
in
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda}
, its projections to
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
,
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i,\cap}
, and
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i,\perp}
are thus determined respectively by the idempotents
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(e_{i})
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(e_{i}e)
, and
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(e_{i}e^{\perp})
in
\operatorname{Hom}^{*}(T,T)
. To prove the lemma, we therefore need to show that

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(e_{i})=\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(e_{i}e)\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(e_{i}e^{\perp})=0.

Since
e+e^{\perp}=1_{X}
it suffices to prove
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(e^{\perp})=0
, and the latter follows from
Lemma

A.6
via

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(e^{\perp})=(\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(c_{1})-\lambda)^{n}f(\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{T}(c_{1}))=(\lambda-\lambda)^{n}f(\lambda)\cdot 1_{T}=0.\qed

Combining
Lemma

A.7
with
Theorem

A.4
gives the following.

Corollary A.12

(
[
54
, Corollary 2.19]
)
.

Assuming
X
is compact, a full subcategory
\mathcal{G}
of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
split-generates if the composition

Q_{i}\cap Q_{\lambda}\xrightarrow{\ \ \operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i})\xrightarrow{\ \ \text{restrict}\ \ }\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{G})

is injective, where
Q_{\lambda}
denotes the generalised
\lambda
-eigenspace of
c_{1}\mathbin{\star}
as above.

Proof.

Using the notation of
Lemma

A.7
, consider the refined decomposition

\bigoplus_{i}(Q_{i}\cap Q_{\lambda}\oplus Q_{i}\cap Q_{\lambda}^{\perp})

of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
. By
Theorem

A.4
for this new decomposition, a full subcategory
\mathcal{G}
of
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i,\cap}
split-generates if
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}_{\lambda}
injects
Q_{i}\cap Q_{\lambda}
into
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{G})
. But by
Lemma

A.7
we have
\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i,\cap}=\mathcal{F}(X)_{\lambda,i}
.
∎

Appendix B
Some
A_{\infty}
-algebra

The purpose of this appendix is to establish some lemmas in
A_{\infty}
-algebra that play a key role in the proof of
C
in
Section

4
. They are not deep, and are probably well-known, but we need explicit formulae compatible with our sign conventions so we give the gory details.

Fix an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
over
\mathbf{k}
. Throughout this appendix, everything is implicitly either
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded or, if
\operatorname{char}\mathbf{k}=2
, ungraded. Given an
\mathcal{A}
-bimodule
\mathcal{P}
, with operations
\mu^{k|1|l}_{\mathcal{P}}
, the Hochschild cochain complex
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{P})
is

\prod_{r\geq 0}\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathcal{A}[1]^{\otimes r},\mathcal{P})

with differential given by

(50)

\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{CC}}(\varphi)(\dots)=\sum(-1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert\maltese_{l}+1}\mu^{k|1|l}_{\mathcal{P}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\dots}},\varphi(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle l}}{{\dots}})+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert+\maltese_{i}}\varphi(\dots,\mu(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}}).

Undecorated
\mu
operations are implicitly those on
\mathcal{A}
. When
\mathcal{P}
is the diagonal bimodule, as defined in
[
54
, (A.2.13)]
, this reduces to the ordinary Hochschild complex
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A})
as defined by (
12
).

Suppose now that
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
are (left)
\mathcal{A}
-modules. The
\mathbf{k}
-module
\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})
is naturally an
\mathcal{A}
-bimodule, with operations

(51)

\begin{gathered}\mu^{0|1|0}_{\mathbf{k}-\hom}(\zeta)(m)=(-1)^{\lvert m\rvert}\big(\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{1}(\zeta(m))-\zeta(\mu_{\mathcal{M}}^{1}(m))\big),\\
\mu^{k|1|0}_{\mathbf{k}-\hom}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1},\zeta)(m)=(-1)^{\lvert m\rvert}\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{k}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1},\zeta(m)),\\
\mu^{0|1|l}_{\mathbf{k}-\hom}(\zeta,a_{l}\dots,a_{1})(m)=(-1)^{\lvert m\rvert+1}\zeta(\mu_{\mathcal{M}}^{l}(a_{l},\dots,a_{1},m)),\\
\mu^{k|1|l}_{\mathbf{k}-\hom}=0.\end{gathered}

for
k,l>0
. We can thus consider the Hochschild cochain complex
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))
.

The other object we will need is the space

\hom^{*}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})=\prod_{r\geq 0}\hom_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}(\mathcal{A}[1]^{\otimes r}\otimes\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})

of
\mathcal{A}
-module pre-morphisms from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{N}
. Recall that this is a cochain complex with differential

\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{A}-\hom}(\psi)(\dots,m)=\sum\mu_{\mathcal{N}}(\dots,\psi(\dots,m))+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\psi\rvert+1}\psi(\dots,\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(\dots,m))\\
+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\psi\rvert+\maltese_{i}+\lvert m\rvert+1}\psi(\dots,\mu(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}},m).

The cocycles are the actual
\mathcal{A}
-module morphisms.

Our first result relates the two complexes
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))
and
\hom^{*}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})
.

Lemma B.1
.

There is an isomorphism of cochain complexes over
\mathbf{k}

U:\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))\to\hom^{*}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}).

Proof.

Define the map
U
(which denotes ‘uncurrying’) by

U(\varphi)(a_{k},\dots,a_{1},m)=(-1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert(\lvert m\rvert+1)}\varphi(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})(m).

By hom–tensor adjunction this is an isomorphism of the underlying
\mathbf{k}
-modules of the two cochain complexes. Combining (
50
) and (
51
) we see that the differential on
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))
is given by

\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{CC}}(\varphi)(\dots)(m)=\sum(-1)^{\lvert m\rvert+1}\mu_{\mathcal{N}}(\dots,\varphi(\dots)(m))+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert\maltese_{i}+\lvert m\rvert}\varphi(\dots)(\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}},m))\\
+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert+\maltese_{i}}\varphi(\dots,\mu(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}})(m).

A straightforward calculation shows that
U
intertwines this with
\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{A}-\hom}
.
∎

Now suppose that
\mathcal{M}
is actually
\mathcal{A}
, viewed as a left module over itself. Denoting the module operations by
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}
still, to avoid confusing them with the algebra operations on
\mathcal{A}
, they are given by
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})=-\mu(a_{k},\dots,a_{1})
. We then have the following reassuring result.

Lemma B.2
.

If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{N}
are cohomologically unital then the map

\pi:\hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{N})\to\mathcal{N}\quad\text{given by}\quad\pi(\psi)=\psi(e_{\mathcal{A}})

is a quasi-isomorphism. Here
e_{\mathcal{A}}
is a chain-level representative for the cohomological unit
1_{\mathcal{A}}
in
\mathcal{A}
.

Remark B.3
.

Cohomological unitality for
\mathcal{N}
means that
\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(e_{\mathcal{A}},n)
is cohomologous to
(-1)^{\lvert n\rvert+1}n
for all cocycles
n
. In particular,
\mathcal{A}
being cohomologically unital as an
\mathcal{A}
-module reduces to
\mathcal{A}
being cohomologically unital as an
A_{\infty}
-algebra.

Proof.

Define a map
\iota:\mathcal{N}\to\hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{N})
by

\iota(n)(a_{k}\dots a_{1})=(-1)^{\lvert n\rvert+1}\mu_{\mathcal{N}}(a_{k},\dots,a_{1},n).

The differential on
\hom^{*}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{N})
is given by

(52)

\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{A}-\hom}(\psi)(\dots)=\sum\mu_{\mathcal{N}}(\dots,\psi(\dots))+\sum(-1)^{\lvert\psi\rvert+\maltese_{i}}\psi(\dots,\mu(\dots),\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\dots}}),

and it is easily verified from this that both
\pi
and
\iota
are chain maps. We claim that they are mutually quasi-inverse. It is straightforward to check that
\pi\circ\iota
induces the identity map on
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathcal{N})
, so it remains to show that
\iota\circ\pi
induces an isomorphism on
\operatorname{Hom}^{*}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{N})
. For this we will use the Eilenberg–Moore comparison theorem.

Equip
\hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{N})
with the length filtration, whose
p
th filtered piece
F^{p}\hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{N})
comprises those
\psi
whose
r
th component
\psi^{r}
vanishes for all
r\leq p
. This filtration is exhaustive and complete, and is respected by the map
\iota\circ\pi
since
\pi
annihilates
F^{1}
. By Eilenberg–Moore it thus suffices to show that, for some
r
,
\iota\circ\pi
induces an isomorphism on the
E_{r}
page of the associated spectral sequence. We claim that this happens for
r=2
.

The zeroth page of the spectral sequence is

E_{0}^{p,q}=\begin{cases}\hom^{q}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)},\mathcal{N})&\text{if $p\geq 0$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The differential coincides with that on the space of maps of complexes from
\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}
to
\mathcal{N}
. Its cohomology is therefore the space of chain maps
\mathcal{A}^{\otimes(p+1)}\to\mathcal{N}
modulo chain homotopy. Because we’re working over a field, this simplifies to the space of linear maps between the corresponding cohomology groups. In other words, we have

E_{1}^{p,q}=\begin{cases}\operatorname{Hom}^{q}_{\mathbf{k}}(\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathcal{A})^{\otimes(p+1)},\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathcal{N}))&\text{if $p\geq 0$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

On this
E_{1}
page the differential
\mu^{1}_{E_{1}}:E_{1}^{p,q}\to E_{1}^{p+1,q}
is given by the
\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}
and
\mu^{2}
terms in (
52
). Now define the maps
h:E_{1}^{p,q}\to E_{1}^{p-1,q}
by

h(\psi)(a_{p-1},\dots,a_{1})=(-1)^{\lvert\psi\rvert-1}\psi(a_{p-1},\dots,a_{1},1_{\mathcal{A}}).

These satisfy

\mu^{1}_{E_{1}}(h(\psi))+h(\mu^{1}_{E_{1}}(\psi))=\psi-\iota\circ\pi(\psi),

so
\iota\circ\pi
is homotopic to the identity on
E_{1}
and hence induces an isomorphism (the identity) on
E_{2}
, completing the proof.
∎

Combining the two previous results gives the following.

Corollary B.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
\mathcal{N}
is an
\mathcal{A}
-module, and that both are cohomologically unital. Then the map

\pi\circ U:\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{A},\hom^{*}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{N}))\to\mathcal{N}

given by
\varphi\mapsto(-1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert}\varphi^{0}(e_{\mathcal{A}})
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Appendix C
Quantum cohomology of monotone toric manifolds

Throughout this appendix let
X
be a compact toric manifold and let
L\subset X
be a toric fibre. In
Section

C.1
we give a more precise description of this setup, part way through which we impose the additional condition that
X
and
L
are monotone, which will persist to the end of the appendix.
Sections

C.2
and
C.3
we then compute
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
and use this to give a presentation of
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
. Finally, in
Section

C.4
we discuss the enlargement of the coefficients of quantum cohomology, necessary for our results on real Lagrangians in
Section

6
. As in the previous appendices, this material is well-known to experts, and we spell it out for completeness and compatibility with our conventions.

C.1.
Toric geometry setup

The toric manifold
X
is described by a moment polytope
\Delta
as follows. Fix an abstract
n
-torus
T
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}
, let
M\subset\mathfrak{t}
be the lattice given by the kernel of the exponential map, and let
\langle{-},{-}\rangle
denote the pairing between
\mathfrak{t}
and its dual
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
. The polytope
\Delta
is a compact subset of
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
, of the form

(53)

\Delta=\{x\in\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}:\langle\nu_{i},x\rangle\geq-\lambda_{i}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,N\},

where
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{N}
are elements of
M
and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}
are real numbers. We require that exactly
k
facets (codimension-
1
faces) of
\Delta
meet at each codimension-
k
face, and that at each such meeting point the corresponding normal vectors
\nu_{i}
can be extended to a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis for
M
. We also require that none of the inequalities in (
53
) is redundant, so there are exactly
N
facets.

The manifold
X
is constructed by symplectic reduction of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
, using the data of
\Delta
. See
[
11
, Chapter 29]
for details.
X
inherits a Hamiltonian
T
-action, with moment map
\mu:X\to\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
whose image is exactly
\Delta
. The
toric fibres
are the preimages of interior points of
\Delta
under
\mu
, each of which is a Lagrangian free
T
-orbit. Letting
F_{i}
denote the
i
th facet, namely

\{x\in\Delta:\langle\nu_{i},x\rangle=-\lambda_{i}\},

the
toric divisors

D_{i}
are the preimages
\mu^{-1}(F_{i})
. These form a free basis for
\operatorname{H}_{2n-2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
. We write
H_{i}
for the Poincaré dual of
D_{i}
, in
\operatorname{H}^{2}(X)
.

The holomorphic index
2
discs in
X
with boundary on
L
, for the standard complex structure, were explicitly described by Cho and Cho–Oh
[
13
,
16
]
. To state their result, let
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\beta)
denote the moduli space of (smooth) holomorphic discs in class
\beta\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
with one boundary marked point. Let
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{N}
denote the basis of
H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
dual to the toric divisors
D_{1},\dots,D_{N}
. Equip
L
with the
standard
spin structure, meaning the spin structure induced by the trivialisation of
TL
induced by any diffeomorphic identification of
L
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}/\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Cho and Cho–Oh proved the following.

Proposition C.1
.

The index
2
disc classes
\beta
for which
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\beta)
is non-empty are precisely
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{N}
. For each
i
the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\beta_{i})
is a free
T
-orbit, and the evaluation map

\operatorname{ev}:\mathcal{M}_{1}(\beta_{i})\to L

at the marked point is
T
-equivariant. With respect to the standard spin structure,
\operatorname{ev}
has degree
+1
.
∎

The disc class
\beta_{i}
has area
2\pi(\langle\nu_{i},\mu(L)\rangle+\lambda_{i})
. There is a natural identification between
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
and
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T;\mathbb{Z})
since
L
is a free
T
-orbit, and between
\operatorname{H}_{1}(T;\mathbb{Z})
and
M
since
T\cong\mathfrak{t}/M
. Under these identifications, the boundary
\partial\beta_{i}\in\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
corresponds to
\nu_{i}\in M
.

Assume from now on that
X
is monotone. This is equivalent to being able to translate
\Delta
so that all
\lambda_{i}
are equal, which we assume has been done. We also assume that the symplectic form has been rescaled so that each
\lambda_{i}
is
1
. In this case, exactly one of the toric fibres is monotone, namely
\mu^{-1}(0)
. This will be our
L
. Viewing the
\nu_{i}
as elements of
\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
via the identification from the previous paragraph,
Proposition

C.1
gives the following.

Corollary C.2
.

The superpotential
W_{L}
is
z^{\nu_{1}}+\dots+z^{\nu_{N}}

∎

C.2.
Floer cohomology of the monotone fibre

Throughout this appendix we shall use a pearl model for Floer cohomology. So Floer cochains are Morse cochains associated to a fixed choice of Morse function, and the differential counts pearly trajectories as described in
Section

5.3
. In particular, when computing
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}
we weight each trajectory by the monomial
z^{[\partial u_{1}]+\dots+[\partial u_{k}]}
in
S
, where
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
are the discs in the trajectory.

With this in place, we can compute
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
using the Oh spectral sequence
[
45
,
9
]

E_{1}=\operatorname{H}^{*}(L;S)\implies\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L}).

Proposition C.3
.

There is a canonical
\mathbf{k}
-algebra isomorphism
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})\cong\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
.

Proof.

The restriction of the
E_{1}
differential to

\operatorname{H}^{1}(L)\to\operatorname{H}^{0}(L;S)=S

counts pearly trajectories that start at an index
1
critical point, contain a single index
2
disc, and then end at a fixed index
0
critical point
p
. More precisely, the input should be a coexact linear combination of index
1
critical points, representing a class
b\in\operatorname{H}^{1}(L)
, and we should weight each trajectory by the monomial representing the boundary of its disc. This amounts to counting index
2
discs
u
through
p
, weighted by
z^{[\partial u]}
, and additionally weighted by the pairing
\langle[\partial u],b\rangle
. Using
Corollary

C.2
, the result is
\sum_{i}\langle\nu_{i},b\rangle z^{\nu_{i}}
.

By
S
-linearity and the Leibniz rule, we see that the full
E_{1}
differential on
\operatorname{H}^{*}(L;S)=S\otimes_{\mathbf{k}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{k}}^{*}\operatorname{H}^{1}(L)
is given by contraction with
\sum_{i}\nu_{i}z^{\nu_{i}}
. Thus the
E_{1}
page is a Koszul complex resolving

S\Big/\Big(\sum_{i}\nu_{i}z^{\nu_{i}}\Big);

see
C.4
, below. (As usual, an ideal generated by vectors implicitly means the ideal generated by their components with respect to any basis.) The spectral sequence therefore collapses and

\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})=\operatorname{HF}^{0}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})=S\Big/\Big(\sum_{i}\nu_{i}z^{\nu_{i}}\Big).

To identify this quotient, fix a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
for
M
, and let
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
be the corresponding monomials
z^{e_{i}}
in
S
. Then for each
j
the
e_{j}
-component of
\sum_{i}\nu_{i}z^{\nu_{i}}
is

z_{j}\frac{\partial W_{L}}{\partial z_{j}},

so
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
is exactly
\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
.
∎

Remark C.4
.

The components
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
of
\sum_{i}\nu_{i}z^{\nu_{i}}
with respect to an arbitrary basis form a Koszul-regular sequence in
S
, i.e. their Koszul complex has vanishing (co)homology outside degree
0
, for the following reason. Since the vanishing of a module can be checked locally at maximal ideals, and since localisation is exact, it suffices to show that the sequence is Koszul-regular in each localisation
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
of
S
at a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{n}
. For each such
\mathfrak{n}
, either all
c_{i}
are contained in
\mathfrak{n}
or some
c_{i}
is not contained in
\mathfrak{n}
. In the former case, the
c_{i}
form a regular—and hence Koszul-regular—sequence by
[
61
, Exercise 26.2.D]
, because
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
is a regular local ring of Krull dimension
n
and
S_{\mathfrak{n}}/(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
has Krull dimension zero (since it’s finite-dimensional over
\mathbf{k}
). In the latter case, the Koszul complex over
S_{\mathfrak{n}}
is acyclic since multiplication by some
c_{i}
is an isomorphism
S_{\mathfrak{n}}\to S_{\mathfrak{n}}
.

C.3.
Small quantum cohomology

The map

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})\cong\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}

sends each toric divisor class
H_{i}
to the count of index
2
discs
u
meeting
D_{i}
, weighted by
z^{[\partial u]}
. Since the basic classes
\beta_{j}
are dual to the
D_{j}
,
Proposition

C.1
tells us that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(H_{i})
counts exactly one disc, which is in class
\beta_{i}
. Thus
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(H_{i})=z^{\nu_{i}}
. The following is proved in
[
57
, Proposition 4.6]
, building on work of Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono
[
32
]
.

Theorem C.5
.

The map
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
is an isomorphism of
\mathbf{k}
-algebras.
∎

Remark C.6
.

The statement of
[
57
, Proposition 4.6]
is superficially different, stating that a map

\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}_{\mathrm{mon}}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X;R[T])\to R[\Gamma]\Big/\Big(\sum_{i}\nu_{i}z_{i}\Big)

is an isomorphism of
R[T]
-algebras, for any ring
R
. Here
T
is the Novikov variable, and
\Gamma
is the submonoid of
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
generated by
(1,\nu_{1}),\dots,(1,\nu_{N})
. The element
z_{i}\in R[\Gamma]
is the monomial corresponding
(1,\nu_{i})
, and
R[\Gamma]
is viewed as an
R[T]
-algebra by letting
T
acts as the monomial corresponding to
(1,0)
. To obtain the statement we want, take
R
to be
\mathbf{k}
and reduce both sides modulo
(T-1)
. Then
R[\Gamma]
becomes
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})]=S
, and
\sum_{i}\nu_{i}z_{i}
becomes
\sum_{i}\nu_{i}z^{\nu_{i}}
as above, so the codomain of
\operatorname{\mathfrak{ks}_{\mathrm{mon}}}
becomes
\operatorname{Jac}W_{L}
. By inspecting the definition of the map itself in
[
57
, Section 2.3]
, one sees that it becomes exactly the weighted closed–open map
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}
.

More generally, it is proved in
[
57
, Proposition 4.14]
, again building on
[
32
]
, that the result still holds after twisting by any class in
\chi\in\operatorname{H}^{2}(X;\mathbf{k}^{\times})
(called
\rho
in
[
57
]
). Explicitly, fix such a
\chi
and choose a lift to
\widehat{\chi}\in\operatorname{H}^{2}(X,L;\mathbf{k}^{\times})
; the set of lifts (is non-empty and) forms a torsor for
\operatorname{H}^{1}(L;\mathbf{k}^{\times})
since the restriction map
\operatorname{H}^{*}(X;\mathbf{k}^{\times})\to\operatorname{H}^{*}(L;\mathbf{k}^{\times})
vanishes. Define
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\chi}(X)
by modifying the usual definition of quantum cohomology by weighting each count of holomorphic spheres
u
by
\chi([u])
. Similarly define
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S,\widehat{\chi}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
by weighting each count of holomorphic discs
u
by
\widehat{\chi}([u])
in addition to the monomial
z^{[\partial u]}
. The same argument as for
Proposition

C.3
shows that

\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S,\widehat{\chi}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})\cong\operatorname{Jac}W_{L,\widehat{\chi}},

where
W_{L,\widehat{\chi}}=\sum_{i}\widehat{\chi}(\beta_{i})z^{\nu_{i}}
. Here
\beta_{i}
is the
i
th basic disc class as above. The statement is then the following.

Proposition C.7
.

The twisted closed–open map

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L},\widehat{\chi}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\chi}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S,\widehat{\chi}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})

is an isomorphism of
\mathbf{k}
-algebras.
∎

C.4.
Enlarging the coefficients

We wish to extend
Proposition

C.7
slightly, by incorporating ‘every
\chi
at once’, analogously to the way that working over
S
incorporates ‘every local system at once’. More precisely, define
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\operatorname{H}_{2}}(X)
by working over
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
and weighting each count of holomorphic spheres
u
by the monomial
Z^{[u]}
corresponding to the homology class
[u]
. Similarly, let
\widetilde{S}=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})]
and define
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{\widetilde{S}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
by working over
\widetilde{S}
and weighting each pearly trajectory by the monomial
Z^{[u_{1}]+\dots+[u_{k}]}
associated to the sum of its disc classes.

Since the basic disc classes
\beta_{i}
form a free basis for
\operatorname{H}_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
, we have
\widetilde{S}=\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}]
, where
Z_{i}
denotes
Z^{\beta_{i}}
. The long exact sequence of the pair
(X,L)
tells us that

\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})=\Big\{\sum_{i}p_{i}\beta_{i}:p_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }\sum_{i}p_{i}\nu_{i}=0\Big\}

so
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
is the subalgebra of
\widetilde{S}
spanned by monomials
Z_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdots Z_{N}^{p_{N}}
with
\sum_{i}p_{i}\nu_{i}=0
. A class
\chi\in\operatorname{H}^{2}(X;\mathbf{k}^{\times})
induces an algebra homomorphism
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]\to\mathbf{k}
, and we denote its kernel by
\mathfrak{n}
. Reducing
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\operatorname{H}_{2}}(X)
modulo
\mathfrak{n}
gives exactly
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\chi}(X)
from the previous subsection. Similarly a choice of lift of
\chi
to
\widehat{\chi}\in\operatorname{H}^{2}(X,L;\mathbf{k}^{\times})
induces an isomorphism
\widetilde{S}/\mathfrak{n}\widetilde{S}\to S
by
Z^{\beta}\mapsto\widehat{\chi}(\beta)z^{[\partial\beta]}
. The analogue of
W_{L}
in
\widetilde{S}
is
\sum_{i}Z_{i}
, and similar arguments to
Proposition

C.3
and
C.4
show that

\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{\widetilde{S}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})\cong\widetilde{S}\Big/\Big(\sum_{i}\nu_{i}Z_{i}\Big),

whose reduction modulo
\mathfrak{n}
is
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{S,\widehat{\chi}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
.

The main result of this appendix is the following.

Theorem C.8
.

The weighted closed–open map

\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L},\operatorname{H}_{2}}:\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\operatorname{H}_{2}}(X)\to\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{\widetilde{S}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})=\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}]\Big/\Big(\sum_{i}\nu_{i}Z_{i}\Big)

sends
H_{i}
to
Z_{i}
and is an isomorphism of
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
algebras.

Proof.

The computation
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L},\operatorname{H}_{2}}(H_{i})=Z_{i}
is completely analogous to
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(H_{i})=z^{\nu_{i}}
from the previous subsection. To prove that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L},\operatorname{H}_{2}}
is an isomorphism, we first show it’s surjective. We have just seen that it hits each
Z_{i}
, and we also know that it hits the image of
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
, namely those monomials
Z_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdots Z_{N}^{p_{N}}
with
\sum_{i}p_{i}\nu_{i}=0
, so to prove surjectivity it suffices to show that together these elements generate each
Z_{i}^{-1}
.

To do this, fix an
i
and consider the linear functional
\langle\nu_{i},\bullet\rangle:\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}\to\mathbb{R}
. Its restriction to
\Delta
attains its maximum at some vertex
v
, which is the intersection of
n
facets, with normals
\nu_{j_{1}},\dots,\nu_{j_{n}}
say. The
\nu_{j_{k}}
form a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis for
M=\operatorname{H}_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
so we can write
-\nu_{i}
as
\sum_{k}r_{k}\nu_{j_{k}}
for unique integers
r_{k}
. Then
(Z_{i}\prod_{k}Z_{j_{k}}^{r_{k}})^{-1}
lies in
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
. And since
\langle\nu_{i},\bullet\rangle
attains its maximum at
v
the
r_{k}
are all non-negative. Therefore

Z_{i}^{-1}=Z_{j_{1}}^{r_{1}}\cdots Z_{j_{n}}^{r_{n}}\Big(Z_{i}\prod_{k}Z_{j_{k}}^{r_{k}}\Big)^{-1}

is generated by
Z_{j_{1}},\dots,Z_{j_{n}}
and the image of
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
, as wanted, so
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L},\operatorname{H}_{2}}
is surjective.

It now just remains to show that
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L},\operatorname{H}_{2}}
is injective. Assume first that
\mathbf{k}
is infinite. For
\chi\in\operatorname{H}^{2}(X;\mathbf{k}^{\times})
, let
\mathfrak{n}
denote the kernel of the induced algebra homomorphism
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]\to\mathbf{k}
as above. Reducing the domain and codomain of
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L},\operatorname{H}_{2}}
modulo
\mathfrak{n}
gives exactly
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L},\chi}
, which we know is injective by
Proposition

C.7
. It therefore suffices to show that for all non-zero
x\in\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\operatorname{H}_{2}}(X)
there exists
\chi
such that
x
is non-zero modulo
\mathfrak{n}
. And since
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\operatorname{H}_{2}}(X)
is a free
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
-module, it is in fact enough to show that for all non-zero
f\in\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
there exists
\chi
such that
f
is non-zero modulo
\chi
.

Suppose then that we’re given non-zero
f\in\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
. After picking a basis for
\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
we can view
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
as a Laurent polynomial ring
\mathbf{k}[Y_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Y_{N-n}^{\pm 1}]
, where
N-n
is the rank of
\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
(recall that
n
is the complex dimension of
X
and
N
is the number of facets of its moment polytope
\Delta
). Then
f
is a non-zero Laurent polynomial
f(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{N-n})
in the
Y_{i}
, and we need to show that there exist
y_{1},\dots,y_{N-n}\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
such that
f(y_{1},\dots,y_{N-n})\neq 0
. This follows by induction on
N-n
, using the fact that
\mathbf{k}
is infinite but a non-zero polynomial can have only finitely many zeros.

We are left to deal with finite
\mathbf{k}
. In this case note that if
\operatorname{\mathcal{CO}}^{0}_{\mathbf{L},\operatorname{H}_{2}}
were not injective then it would continue to be non-injective after tensoring with
\mathbf{k}(t)
. But the resulting map would necessarily be injective by the argument just given for infinite fields.
∎

Remark C.9
.

The isomorphism
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\operatorname{H}_{2}}(X)\cong\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}]/(\sum_{i}\nu_{i}Z_{i})
still holds if
\mathbf{k}
is allowed to be an arbitrary ring, by arguing as in
[
57
, Section 4.2, especially Proposition 4.6]
. The isomorphism can still be constructed as a weighted closed–open map, but it’s no longer clear that the analogue of
C.4
holds, so
\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}]/(\sum_{i}\nu_{i}Z_{i})
may in principle be a proper subalgebra of
\operatorname{HF}^{*}_{\widetilde{S}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L})
.

Finally, let
R=\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)]
, viewed as a
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
-algebra via the natural map
\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, and let

\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)=R\otimes_{\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]}\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\operatorname{H}_{2}}(X).

The consequence of
Theorem

C.8
that we actually need is as follows.

Corollary C.10
.

We have identifications of
\mathbf{k}
-algebras

\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)\cong\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}]\Big/\Big(\sum_{j}\nu_{j}Z_{j}\Big)+\Big(\prod_{j}Z_{j}^{\langle 2A,H_{j}\rangle}-1:A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\Big)

and

\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)\cong\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}]\Big/\Big(\sum_{j}\nu_{j}Z_{j}\Big)+\Big(\prod_{j}Z_{j}^{\langle A,H_{j}\rangle}-1:A\in\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\Big),

under which
H_{i}
on the left-hand side corresponds to
Z_{i}
on the right-hand side.

Proof.

Take the identification of
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
-algebras

\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\operatorname{H}_{2}}(X)=\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}]\Big/\Big(\sum_{i}\nu_{i}Z_{i}\Big)

from
Theorem

C.8
, under which
H_{i}
is sent to
Z_{i}
. Recall that the
\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
-action on the right-hand side arises from the map

\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]\to\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})]=\widetilde{S}=\mathbf{k}[Z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,Z_{N}^{\pm 1}].

Under this map, the monomial
Z^{A}\in\mathbf{k}[\operatorname{H}_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})]
is sent to
\prod_{j}Z_{j}^{p_{j}}
, where
A=\sum_{j}p_{j}\beta_{j}
in
\operatorname{H}_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
. Since each
\beta_{j}
has intersection number
\delta_{ij}
with the
i
th toric divisor
D_{i}
, we can express each
p_{j}
as
\langle A,H_{j}\rangle
, and hence
Z^{A}
as
\prod_{j}Z_{j}^{\langle A,H_{j}\rangle}-1
. The corollary then follows immediately from the fact that
\operatorname{QH}^{*}(X)
and
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{R}(X)
are obtained from
\operatorname{QH}^{*}_{\operatorname{H}_{2}}(X)
by setting each
Z^{A}
, respectively
Z^{2A}
, to
1
.
∎
